electromagnetism.bigb
= Electromagnetism
{wiki}

Electromagnetism describes how <electric charge> and <electric current> interact through the <electric field> $\mathbf E$ and <magnetic field> $\mathbf B$. The <Maxwell equations> describe the fields and their sources; the <Lorentz force> describes the response of a particle of <electric charge> $q$ and <velocity> $\mathbf v$:
$$
\mathbf F=q(\mathbf E+\mathbf v\times\mathbf B),\qquad
\frac{d\mathcal E_{\mathrm{particle}}}{dt}=q\mathbf E\cdot\mathbf v.
$$
The <electric field> can change the particle's <energy>. The magnetic part of the <Lorentz force> is perpendicular to <velocity>, so <magnetic forces do no work>; they can bend a trajectory. These are local interactions: a particle responds to the <electromagnetic field> at its position. https://www.feynmanlectures.caltech.edu/II_01.html[Feynman lectures, II.1: fields and forces]{ref}.

The remarkable unification is that the same <Maxwell equations> connect <Coulomb's law>, <Faraday's law of induction>, and freely propagating <electromagnetic waves>. A changing <electric field> contributes to magnetic circulation through <displacement current>; together with <Faraday's law>, this permits waves in vacuum without conducting matter. The <Poynting theorem> then explains how the fields store and transport <energy>. This connects the force on a charge to the energy carried by light.

In his 1865 paper, <James Clerk Maxwell> derived transverse disturbances and compared the propagation speed inferred from electrical measurements with independently measured light speeds. Part VI, especially §§95–97, argues that their agreement supports identifying light as an <electromagnetic wave>. This was a comparison with finite experimental accuracy, rather than a numerical identity between the historical measurements. https://royalsocietypublishing.org/doi/10.1098/rstl.1865.0008[Maxwell, A Dynamical Theory of the Electromagnetic Field, Philosophical Transactions 155 (1865), 459–512]{ref}; https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/A_Dynamical_Theory_of_the_Electromagnetic_Field/Part_VI[transcription of Maxwell's original Part VI]{ref}.

= Thermoelectric effect
{parent=Electromagnetism}
{wiki}

A <thermoelectric effect> couples <heat transfer> and the transport of <electric charge>: a <temperature gradient> can generate an <electromotive force>, while an <electric current> can carry <heat> and cause heating or cooling at a junction of different materials. This coupling makes electrical measurement of <temperature> possible through a <thermocouple>, and permits conversion of a sustained flow of <heat> into electrical <work>. A finite open-circuit voltage does not itself deliver <power>: electrical <power> requires an <electric current> through a load. Thermoelectric conversion therefore involves both <electromagnetism> and <thermodynamics>. https://thermoelectrics.matsci.northwestern.edu/thermoelectrics/history.html[Northwestern thermoelectrics research group: thermoelectric effects and applications]{ref}.

= Seebeck effect
{c}
{parent=Thermoelectric effect}

The <Seebeck effect> is the generation of a thermoelectric <electromotive force> by a <temperature> difference. In a <thermocouple>, two different homogeneous <electrical conductors> A and B connect a measuring junction at $T_h$ to two reference connections at a common $T_c$. With negligible <electric current>, the measured terminal voltage is determined by the difference of their <Seebeck coefficients>. A hot junction alone does not specify the reading: the reference <temperature>, the second material, and the terminal order matter.

\Image[seebeck-thermocouple-experiment.png]
{title=Two-material <thermocouple>: junction <temperatures> and the voltage sign}
{description=An idealized open-circuit <Seebeck effect> experiment. The A and B wires meet at the measuring junction; each reference connection meets an identical meter lead at the same $T_c$. The positive meter input connects to A. The shown $20\,{}^\circ\mathrm C$, $70\,{}^\circ\mathrm C$, and $+2\,\mathrm{mV}$ belong to the explicitly toy constant-<Seebeck-coefficient> example below, not measured data.}

For a motivating experiment, join one end of A to one end of B, insulating the wires elsewhere. Connect their free ends to identical meter leads at reference <temperature> $T_c$, for example in an ice-water <heat bath>. A meter with input <electrical resistance> much larger than the <thermocouple>'s <electrical resistance> approximates an open circuit, meaning $I\approx0$. Keep the measuring junction in a separately controlled <heat bath> at $T_h$ and wait for stable junction <temperatures>. Both reference connections must share $T_c$; identical leads with identical endpoint <temperatures> have cancelling thermoelectric contributions, allowing the meter to be elsewhere. https://www.nist.gov/system/files/documents/director/planning/report97-1.pdf[Marx, Link and Scott, Economic Assessment of the NIST Thermocouple Calibration Program (1997), §2.1]{ref}.

Define the reading as $V_{AB}=V_A-V_B$, with the positive input on the A reference terminal and the negative input on B. Use the <Seebeck coefficient> convention in which the voltage change along a homogeneous wire at zero <electric current> is $dV=-S(T)\,dT$. Following the path from A at $T_c$ to the common junction at $T_h$, then back along B to $T_c$, gives
$$
V_{AB}(T_h,T_c)=\int_{T_c}^{T_h}\bigl[S_A(T)-S_B(T)\bigr]\,dT.
$$
This formula assumes homogeneous materials whose <Seebeck coefficients> depend only on the local <temperature>, and no additional uncompensated lead contributions. Here voltage is the electrical transport voltage associated with <electrochemical potential>, rather than a direct measurement of an isolated equilibrium contact <electric potential>.

Start with $T_h=T_c$, then increase $T_h$ while holding $T_c$ fixed and record $V_{AB}$ at each stable setting. In the ideal model the equal-<temperature> reading is zero. If $S_A-S_B>0$ throughout the interval, A reads positive when $T_h>T_c$. Exchanging meter inputs reverses the reading; exchanging the two junction <temperatures> reverses the oriented <integral>. Repeating the run with identical homogeneous materials gives zero because $S_A=S_B$. These controls test the stated model; a nonzero experimental baseline should be measured rather than silently removed by assuming it is zero.

For an explicitly toy linear example, stipulate constant <Seebeck coefficients> $S_A=25\,\mu\mathrm{V/K}$ and $S_B=-15\,\mu\mathrm{V/K}$. Choose reference <temperature> $T_c=20\,{}^\circ\mathrm C$ and measuring-junction <temperature> $T_h=70\,{}^\circ\mathrm C$. A <temperature> difference has the same numerical size in <kelvins> and on the <degree Celsius> scale, so
$$
S_A-S_B=40\,\mu\mathrm{V/K},\qquad \Delta T=50\,\mathrm K,
\qquad V_{AB}=(40\,\mu\mathrm{V/K})(50\,\mathrm K)=+2.00\,\mathrm{mV}.
$$
Swapping the inputs gives $-2.00\,\mathrm{mV}$. Bringing both junction regions to the same <temperature> gives zero. With <temperature>-dependent <Seebeck coefficients>, shifting both junction <temperatures> equally while keeping their difference fixed can change the reading. No actual material pair is claimed to have the stipulated coefficients over this interval.

\Image[seebeck-toy-voltage-slope.png]
{title=Toy <Seebeck effect> calibration: voltage slope and reversed inputs}
{description=Calculated lines, not measured data. The solid line is $V_{AB}=(40\,\mu\mathrm{V/K})\Delta T$; the dashed line shows the same device with reversed meter inputs. The <slope> is the difference of the two <Seebeck coefficients>, not either coefficient separately. The reference <temperature> is fixed at $20\,{}^\circ\mathrm C$; negative $\Delta T$ means the measuring junction is colder than the reference connections.}

Real <thermocouples> need a calibrated voltage-versus-<temperature> relation: the constant-<Seebeck-coefficient> approximation is useful only where the coefficient difference changes little. Wire inhomogeneity can make the result depend on where the <temperature gradient> lies, and reference connections at unequal or unknown <temperatures> introduce errors. An electronic reference correction uses a measured reference <temperature> in place of an ice bath. https://www.nist.gov/system/files/documents/director/planning/report97-1.pdf[Marx, Link and Scott (1997), §2.1: reference correction and wire homogeneity]{ref}. Burns and colleagues provide the standardized reference functions for common letter-designated <thermocouple> types; the toy line above is not a replacement for them. https://www.nist.gov/publications/temperature-electromotive-force-reference-functions-and-tables-letter-designated[Burns et al., Temperature-electromotive force reference functions and tables (1993), NIST Monograph 175]{ref}.

The historical experiment used a different detector. In his 1821–1823 investigations, Seebeck found that a closed <electrical circuit> of two dissimilar materials with junctions at different <temperatures> deflected a compass needle. The modern explanation is that thermoelectric <electromotive force> drives an <electric current>, whose <magnetic field> deflects the needle. The open-circuit experiment above detects voltage while drawing negligible <electric current>. The striking connection is that a thermal imbalance can become an electrical signal and then a magnetic response. https://thermoelectrics.matsci.northwestern.edu/thermoelectrics/history.html[Northwestern thermoelectrics research group: Seebeck's compass experiment]{ref}.

= Seebeck coefficient
{c}
{parent=Seebeck effect}
{title2=$S$}

= Thermopower
{synonym}

The <Seebeck coefficient> $S(T)$ quantifies the thermoelectric voltage response per small <temperature> difference at zero <electric current>. Use $S=-dV/dT$ along a homogeneous material, with voltage understood through <electrochemical potential>. Its units are <volts> per <kelvin>, commonly $\mu\mathrm{V/K}$. The word <thermopower> is also used, despite this being a voltage response rather than <power>; some authors use that word for $|S|$, so check their convention. https://thermoelectrics.matsci.northwestern.edu/thermoelectrics/[Northwestern thermoelectrics research group: Seebeck coefficient and thermopower convention]{ref}.

For the A-minus-B terminal order in the <Seebeck effect> experiment,
$$
\frac{\partial V_{AB}}{\partial T_h}=S_A(T_h)-S_B(T_h),\qquad
\frac{\partial V_{AB}}{\partial T_c}=-\bigl[S_A(T_c)-S_B(T_c)\bigr].
$$
Thus a small <temperature> difference around $T_0$ gives $V_{AB}\approx[S_A(T_0)-S_B(T_0)]\Delta T$. The finite-interval ratio $V_{AB}/\Delta T$ is an average of the coefficient difference, not necessarily its value at either endpoint. Ordinary two-material measurements determine a relative <Seebeck coefficient>: to recover $S_A$ from the measured difference, add a separately known $S_B$. Neither the choice of a zero for voltage nor the use of a familiar reference material makes $S_B$ identically zero.

A concrete research example is Martin and colleagues' 2021 certification experiment. They measured small <temperature> differences and voltages on six <silicon>–germanium specimens at 13 base <temperatures> between $295$ and $900\,\mathrm K$. At each setting they acquired 40 voltage-and-<temperature>-difference pairs and used <linear regression> with a free intercept, so a constant voltage offset did not become part of the <slope>. Platinum voltage leads required a reference-material correction; their terminal order gave $S_{\rm measured}=S_{\rm Pt}-S_{\rm specimen}$, hence $S_{\rm specimen}=S_{\rm Pt}-S_{\rm measured}$. This is an actual measurement protocol, unlike the toy plot above. https://tsapps.nist.gov/publication/get_pdf.cfm?pub_id=932592[Martin et al., Development of a high-temperature (295–900 K) Seebeck coefficient Standard Reference Material (2021), certification measurements and Eq. (1)]{ref}.

Microscopically, a <temperature gradient> changes <charge carrier> transport until an opposing electrical response balances it at zero net <electric current>. In the usual simple <semiconductor> picture, <electrons> and <electron holes> give opposite <Seebeck-coefficient> signs, and their contributions are weighted by their <electrical conductivities>. The <Seebeck coefficient> is a transport property: <density of states> alone does not determine it, since carrier motion and scattering also matter. Its usefulness extends beyond <temperature> sensing to diagnosing which carriers transport <electric charge>. https://thermoelectrics.matsci.northwestern.edu/thermoelectrics/[Northwestern thermoelectrics research group: Seebeck coefficient, scattering contributions, and bipolar transport]{ref}.

= Thermocouple
{parent=Seebeck effect}
{wiki}

A <thermocouple> uses two different <electrical conductors> and the <Seebeck effect> to infer a measuring-junction <temperature> from a voltage and a known reference <temperature>. The reference material and the reference <temperature> are distinct choices: B specifies the comparison <Seebeck coefficient>, while $T_c$ specifies the lower limit of the <integral>. The two-material experiment in <Seebeck effect> gives the geometry, sign convention, and calibration limitations.

= Lorenz-gauge reduction of the electromagnetic action
{parent=Electromagnetism}
{c}

The <electromagnetic field tensor> action differs from the component-gradient potential action by $(2\mu_0)^{-1}\int \partial^bA^a\partial_aA_b\,d^4x$. Integration by parts expresses this term as a boundary integral plus $(2\mu_0)^{-1}\int(\partial_aA^a)^2\,d^4x$. With the <Lorenz gauge> and fixed or vanishing boundary data, the two actions therefore differ only by a boundary term. Varying the component-gradient action without restriction gives the potential wave equations; the Lorenz condition must be supplied to recover all <Maxwell equations>.

= Bioelectromagnetism
{parent=Electromagnetism}
{wiki}

= Volume conductor
{parent=Bioelectromagnetism}

A <volume conductor> is a conducting three-dimensional medium through which <electric currents> flow. In the quasistatic bioelectric model, the total <current density> is $\mathbf J=\mathbf J^p-\sigma\nabla u$, comprising an impressed source current and its Ohmic return current. <Electric charge conservation> gives $\nabla\cdot(\sigma\nabla u)=\nabla\cdot\mathbf J^p$. An insulating boundary has zero normal total current.

= Geselowitz formula
{c}
{parent=Volume conductor}
{title2=$\mathbf B=\mathbf B^p-\frac{\mu_0\sigma}{4\pi}\int_S u\,\mathbf n'\times\frac{\mathbf r-\mathbf r'}{|\mathbf r-\mathbf r'|^3}\,dS'$}

For a homogeneous <volume conductor> with an insulating boundary $S$, the <Geselowitz formula> expresses the exterior <magnetic field> as the primary <Biot-Savart law> field minus a surface contribution from the induced <electric potential>. For a <current dipole>, $\mathbf B^p=\mu_0\mathbf Q\times(\mathbf r-\mathbf r_0)/(4\pi|\mathbf r-\mathbf r_0|^3)$. On a spherical boundary, $\mathbf n'$ is parallel to $\mathbf r'$, so the surface term has zero radial component at the observation point.

= Spherical volume conductor model
{parent=Volume conductor}
{title2=$|\mathbf r|<a$}

A <spherical volume conductor model> approximates a conducting body by a sphere, usually with uniform or radially stratified conductivity. It provides explicit forward models for <electroencephalography> and <magnetoencephalography>. Its symmetry makes a <radial current dipole> magnetically silent outside the sphere; departures from spherical symmetry need not retain that property.

= Sarvas formula
{c}
{parent=Spherical volume conductor model}
{title2=$\mathbf B=\frac{\mu_0}{4\pi}\nabla\frac{(\mathbf Q\times\mathbf r_0)\cdot\mathbf r}{F}$}

For the exterior of a <spherical volume conductor model>, put $P=|\mathbf r-\mathbf r_0|$, $r=|\mathbf r|$ and $F=rP^2+P\mathbf r\cdot(\mathbf r-\mathbf r_0)$. The displayed <Sarvas formula> follows by taking the radial component of the <Geselowitz formula> and integrating the <magnetic scalar potential> from infinity. It is independent of a spherically symmetric conductivity profile. The sign shown uses $\mathbf B=+\mu_0\nabla U/(4\pi)$; conventions defining a magnetic potential with a minus sign reverse $U$. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/3823129/[Sarvas's original paper] derives the spherical-conductor field and its consequences for the inverse problem.

= Spherical current-dipole logarithmic kernel
{parent=Sarvas formula}
{title2=$\phi=\frac1b\log\frac{r+P+b}{r+P-b},\quad b=|\mathbf r_0|$}

For $r>b$ and $P=|\mathbf r-\mathbf r_0|$, the displayed kernel satisfies $\phi=\int_r^\infty ds/[s|s\widehat{\mathbf r}-\mathbf r_0|]$. The limit at $b=0$ is $1/r$. Differentiating along $\mathbf Q\times\mathbf r_0$, which preserves $b$, gives $(\mathbf Q\times\mathbf r_0)\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf r_0}\phi=(\mathbf Q\times\mathbf r_0)\cdot\mathbf r/F$. Thus it generates the normalized <magnetic scalar potential> in the <Sarvas formula>.

= Maxwell–Chern–Simons one-form equation
{c}
{parent=Electromagnetism}
{title2=$d{*F}+kF=0$}

For a <differential one-form> $A$ on an oriented three-dimensional spacetime, varying $\frac12\int(F\wedge*F+kA\wedge F)$, with $F=dA$, gives $d*F+kF=0$. With signature $(-,+,+)$, ${*}^2=-1$ on one- and two-forms; writing $B=*F$ gives $dB-k*B=0$ and the identity $d*B=0$. An Abelian <gauge transformation> changes the action by $\frac k2\int_{\partial M}\Lambda F$. Thus the bulk field equation is invariant, while invariance of the integrated action depends on boundary conditions.

= Electromagnetic field
{parent=Electromagnetism}
{title2=$\mathbf E,\mathbf B$}
{wiki}

= Maxwell field
{synonym}
{c}

The combined <electric field> and <magnetic field> that determine the <Lorentz force> on a charged particle.

= Electrical circuit
{parent=Electromagnetism}

An electrical circuit connects components that carry <electric current> and sustain <electric potential difference> differences. Its dynamics may couple <electrical resistance>, <inductance> and <capacitance>.

= Passive sign convention
{parent=Electrical circuit}
{title2=$p_{\rm absorbed}=VI$}

The <passive sign convention> defines terminal <electric potential difference> $V=\phi(A)-\phi(B)$ and positive <electric current> entering at $A$. Then $VI$ is absorbed electrical <power>: positive means the component receives <energy>, negative means it supplies <energy>. Reversing both reference directions leaves the product unchanged. For a positive <electrical resistance> obeying <Ohm's law>, both $V$ and $I$ reverse together and <Joule heating> remains $I^2R\geq0$.

= Electrical impedance
{parent=Electrical circuit}
{title2=$Z(\omega)=\widehat V/\widehat I$}
{wiki}

Electrical impedance is the ratio of voltage and current <complex amplitudes> for sinusoidal steady response at a specified <angular frequency>. With time dependence $e^{\mathrm i\omega t}$, an ideal <resistor>, <inductor> and <capacitor> have $Z_R=R$, $Z_L=\mathrm i\omega L$ and $Z_C=1/(\mathrm i\omega C)$. Their <electrical impedances> add in a <series RLC circuit>, giving $Z=R+\mathrm i(\omega L-1/(\omega C))$. The current <amplitude> is $V_0/|Z|$, so cancellation of the imaginary terms explains the <resonant series RLC response>. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-2/pages/15-3-rlc-series-circuits-with-ac[OpenStax University Physics 2, §15.3: impedance and series resonance]{ref}.

= Electrical reactance
{parent=Electrical impedance}
{title2=$X=\operatorname{Im}Z$}

Electrical reactance $X$ is the signed <imaginary part> of <electrical impedance> $Z=R+\mathrm iX$, measured in <ohms>. It describes the part of the terminal <electric potential difference> in <phase quadrature> with the <electric current> in a sinusoidal <steady state>. In the $e^{\mathrm i\omega t}$ convention, an ideal <inductor> has $X_L=\omega L>0$ and an ideal <capacitor> has $X_C=-1/(\omega C)<0$ for $\omega>0$. A large $|X|$ reduces <electric current> at fixed applied voltage, but an ideal purely reactive component returns the <energy> it receives over each cycle. This is why <electrical reactance> and <electrical resistance> have the same units yet describe different physical behavior. https://www.feynmanlectures.caltech.edu/II_22.html#Ch22-S5[Feynman, Leighton and Sands, The Feynman Lectures on Physics, II.22.5: resistance, reactance and energy loss]{ref}.

\Image[reactance-frequency-cancellation.png]
{title=Signed <electrical reactances> cancel in a toy <series RLC circuit>}
{description=Calculated ideal curves, not measured data: <inductance> $L=10\,\mathrm{mH}$, <capacitance> $C=1\,\mu\mathrm F$, total <electrical resistance> $R_\Sigma=100+10=110\,\Omega$, and applied <root mean square> voltage $1.00\,\mathrm V$. The $10\,\Omega$ is the sensing <resistor> in <electrical reactance bench measurement>. Top: $X_L$, signed $X_C$, and their sum. Bottom: <electric current> and voltage-minus-current <phase difference>. At $f_0=1.592\,\mathrm{kHz}$ the sum vanishes, the <electric current> peaks, and terminal voltage and <electric current> are in phase.}

In a <series RLC circuit>, <electrical impedances> add because the same <electric current> passes through every component. For ideal positive <inductance> $L$, <capacitance> $C$, and total <electrical resistance> $R_\Sigma>0$,
$$
X(\omega)=\omega L-\frac1{\omega C},\qquad
|Z|=\sqrt{R_\Sigma^2+X^2},\qquad
I_{\rm rms}=\frac{V_{\rm rms}}{|Z|},\qquad
\phi=\arctan\frac{X}{R_\Sigma}.
$$
Here $\phi$ is voltage <wave phase> minus current <wave phase>. Below $\omega_0=1/\sqrt{LC}$, $X<0$ and <electric current> leads terminal voltage; above it, $X>0$ and <electric current> lags. At $\omega_0$, the two signed <electrical reactances> cancel, leaving $Z=R_\Sigma$ and the maximum <electric current> at fixed terminal voltage. This is the <resonant series RLC response>. <Electrical resistance> does not cancel: the toy circuit gives $f_0=\omega_0/(2\pi)=1591.55\,\mathrm{Hz}$ and $I_{\rm rms}=9.091\,\mathrm{mA}$ there. These numbers follow directly from the stipulated component values. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-2/pages/15-3-rlc-series-circuits-with-ac[Ling, Moebs and Sanny, University Physics 2, §15.3: series impedance and resonance]{ref}.

Cancellation concerns terminal voltage, not the disappearance of stored <energy>. In the toy circuit at <resonance>, the individual <inductor> and <capacitor> voltages are each $0.9091\,\mathrm V$ in <root mean square> magnitude, with opposite <wave phases>, while their sum is zero. Their peak stored <magnetic energy> and <capacitor energy> are each $0.8264\,\mu\mathrm J$, attained at different times. With smaller positive $R_\Sigma$, either component voltage can exceed the applied voltage. This ability to select a <frequency> while sustaining internal <energy> exchange connects <electrical reactance> to tuning and <frequency response>.

For an ideal <inductor> or <capacitor>, the absorbed instantaneous <power> equals the rate of change of stored <energy>:
$$
p_L=v_Li=\frac{d}{dt}\left(\frac12Li^2\right),\qquad
p_C=v_Ci=\frac{d}{dt}\left(\frac12Cv_C^2\right).
$$
Positive <power> stores <energy>; negative <power> returns it. Over a periodic <steady state>, each stored <energy> returns to its initial value, so its cycle-averaged absorbed <power> is zero. An ideal <resistor> instead has $p_R=Ri^2\geq0$, transferring <energy> into <Joule heating>. More generally, the average terminal <power> of this <series RLC circuit> is
$$
\overline p=V_{\rm rms}I_{\rm rms}\cos\phi=I_{\rm rms}^2R_\Sigma.
$$
Zero average <power> in an ideal reactive component does not imply zero instantaneous <power>, zero <electric current>, or zero stored <energy>. Real coils and <capacitors> have losses, so zero ideal reactive loss is a model assumption rather than a claim about every component. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-2/pages/15-4-power-in-an-ac-circuit[Ling, Moebs and Sanny, University Physics 2, §15.4: instantaneous and average AC power]{ref}.

= Reactance phase convention
{parent=Electrical reactance}
{title2=$v(t)=\operatorname{Re}(\widehat V e^{\mathrm i\omega t})$}

Use <complex amplitudes> only for signals at one common positive <angular frequency> $\omega$. Define the real terminal voltage and <electric current> by
$$
v(t)=\operatorname{Re}\left(\widehat V e^{\mathrm i\omega t}\right),\qquad
i(t)=\operatorname{Re}\left(\widehat I e^{\mathrm i\omega t}\right),\qquad
Z=\frac{\widehat V}{\widehat I}.
$$
The <complex amplitudes> here use peak magnitudes; both can instead use <root mean square> magnitudes without changing their ratio. Choose positive <electric current> entering the terminal assigned positive voltage, so $p=vi$ is absorbed <power>. For an ideal linear <inductor> and <capacitor>, their terminal laws $v_L=L\,di/dt$ and $i_C=C\,dv_C/dt$ give
$$
\widehat V_L=\mathrm i\omega L\widehat I,\qquad
\widehat I_C=\mathrm i\omega C\widehat V_C,\qquad
Z_L=\mathrm i\omega L,\qquad
Z_C=\frac1{\mathrm i\omega C}=-\frac{\mathrm i}{\omega C}.
$$
The <derivative> multiplies a <complex amplitude> by $\mathrm i\omega$: this single algebraic step explains both signs. Taking $i(t)=I_0\cos\omega t$ as a reference gives $v_L(t)=-\omega LI_0\sin\omega t$ and $v_C(t)=I_0\sin\omega t/(\omega C)$ for the sinusoidal response. Thus <inductor> voltage leads <electric current> by $\pi/2$, while <capacitor> voltage lags <electric current> by $\pi/2$. A constant offset or a free transient is outside this single-<frequency> representation. https://www.feynmanlectures.caltech.edu/II_22.html#Ch22-S1[Feynman, Leighton and Sands, II.22.1: complex sinusoidal representation and component impedances]{ref}.

With the alternative $e^{-\mathrm i\omega t}$ convention, the same real signals use conjugated <complex amplitudes> and $Z_L=-\mathrm i\omega L$, $Z_C=+\mathrm i/(\omega C)$. Their physical leads and lags stay the same; the sign of $\operatorname{Im}Z$ changes. Also, some textbooks call the positive magnitude $1/(\omega C)$ the capacitive reactance and write the series expression as $X_L-|X_C|$. This article consistently uses signed $X_C=-1/(\omega C)$ and hence $X=X_L+X_C$. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-2/pages/15-2-simple-ac-circuits[Ling, Moebs and Sanny, University Physics 2, §15.2: positive capacitive-reactance magnitude and quarter-cycle phase relations]{ref}.

= Electrical reactance bench measurement
{parent=Electrical reactance}
{title2=$X=|Z|\sin\phi$}

The motivating observation is a pair of sinusoids on an oscilloscope: changing <frequency> changes both their relative <amplitudes> and their <phase difference>. Gary Tuttle's EE 201 laboratory uses resistor voltage as the current-phase reference, compares predictions with readings at several <frequencies>, and explicitly asks students to measure component values and account for coil <electrical resistance>. The following bench arrangement applies that method to a <series RLC circuit>; its component values and illustrated traces are our own toy example. https://gtuttle.net/circuits/lab/ac_circuits_1.pdf[Gary Tuttle, EE 201 Lab 9: AC circuits, especially the notes and RC/RL measurements]{ref}.

\Image[reactance-bench-phase-sensing.png]
{title=Toy AC bench arrangement and predicted voltage/current timing}
{description=Top: a sine generator drives ideal $L=10\,\mathrm{mH}$, $C=1\,\mu\mathrm F$, $R=100\,\Omega$, and current-sensing $R_s=10\,\Omega$ in series. Channel A measures loaded terminal voltage; channel B measures $v_s=R_si$ relative to the common return. Bottom: calculated separate voltage traces at $f=500\,\mathrm{Hz}$ and terminal $V_{\rm rms}=1.00\,\mathrm V$, not an oscilloscope recording or measured data. The rising zero of channel B occurs $0.3835\,\mathrm{ms}$ earlier: $\phi=-69.02^\circ$ and predicted $I_{\rm rms}=3.255\,\mathrm{mA}$. The two trace axes have different voltage scales.}

Connect a low-voltage sine generator to a series chain ending in a known sensing <resistor> $R_s$ at the common return. Use a <resistor> whose unwanted <inductance> is negligible over the chosen <frequency> range. An oscilloscope displays voltage against time: channel A reads the loaded voltage across the whole chain, and channel B reads the voltage $v_s$ across $R_s$. Both channel ground connections and the generator return meet at that same node. Ordinary bench oscilloscope grounds are electrically common; attaching one to an intermediate circuit node would change the circuit. A separate floating component voltage needs a suitable differential measurement, rather than moving a ground clip. https://www.tek.com/en/documents/technical-brief/floating-oscilloscope-measurements-and-operator-protection[Tektronix, Floating Oscilloscope Measurements and Operator Protection: ground-referenced inputs]{ref}.

After the free transient decays, record <frequency> $f$, period $T=1/f$, both voltage <amplitudes>, and matched rising zero-crossing times $t_A,t_B$. Check that the traces are sinusoidal. Define $\Delta t=t_B-t_A$ on the branch $|\Delta t|<T/2$. With negligible probe loading and negligible sensing-<resistor> <electrical reactance>, $v_s=R_si$, so channel B is the <electric current> reference, and in the <reactance phase convention>
$$
I_{\rm rms}=\frac{V_{s,\rm rms}}{R_s},\qquad
|Z_{\rm chain}|=\frac{V_{A,\rm rms}}{I_{\rm rms}},\qquad
\phi=\frac{2\pi\Delta t}{T},\qquad
X_{\rm chain}=|Z_{\rm chain}|\sin\phi,\qquad
R_{\rm chain}=|Z_{\rm chain}|\cos\phi.
$$
A later current crossing gives $\Delta t>0$, hence positive <electrical reactance>; an earlier crossing gives negative <electrical reactance>. Use the same <amplitude> convention for both channels. An amplitude-only ratio recovers $|Z|$, so it cannot distinguish the two signs: timing supplies the missing information. The formulas follow from $Z=|Z|e^{\mathrm i\phi}$ and the chosen terminal orientation.

Begin with a known <resistor> replacing the reactive chain: its expected <phase difference> is approximately zero, testing channel polarity and relative delay. Measure the same signal with both channels to establish the instrumental delay baseline, then exchange probes to check that a phase offset does not belong to one channel. Next test an <inductor> and a <capacitor> separately with $R_s$: after subtracting $R_s$ from the inferred real part, expect approximately $X/f=2\pi L$ and $fX=-1/(2\pi C)$, respectively. Finally combine $L,C,R,R_s$ and sweep through the predicted <resonance>. These are proposed controls and consequences of the model, not reported observations.

For the toy <series RLC circuit>, the prediction at $500\,\mathrm{Hz}$ is $X=-286.894\,\Omega$, $|Z|=307.259\,\Omega$, $\phi=-69.02^\circ$, and $V_{s,\rm rms}=32.55\,\mathrm{mV}$. At $5000\,\mathrm{Hz}$, $X=+282.328\,\Omega$ and $\phi=+68.71^\circ$. Near $1591.55\,\mathrm{Hz}$ the model predicts zero <phase difference> and $V_{s,\rm rms}=90.91\,\mathrm{mV}$. Keep actual loaded $V_{A,\rm rms}$ fixed or normalize by it at each <frequency>; the generator's display alone does not establish that voltage. The $10\,\Omega$ sensing <resistor> is part of the $110\,\Omega$ total and therefore changes the predicted peak <electric current>.

Real components add unintended <electrical impedance>: coil wire has <electrical resistance>, nearby turns add <capacitance>, and <capacitor> leads add <inductance>. These effects can produce a component's own <resonance>, beyond which the ideal $\omega L$ or $-1/(\omega C)$ approximation fails. Work in a range where that approximation is adequate, measure coil wire <electrical resistance>, and compare the inferred $R_{\rm chain}$ with the sensing <resistor>, intended <resistor>, and component losses. A measured nonzero real part is evidence of loss in the assembly, not negative or positive <electrical reactance> becoming heat. https://www.keysight.com.cn/cn/zh/assets/7018-06840/application-notes/5950-3000.pdf[Keysight, Impedance Measurement Handbook, §§1.5.1 and 5.2.1: component frequency response and parasitic elements]{ref}; https://gtuttle.net/circuits/lab/ac_circuits_1.pdf[Tuttle, EE 201 Lab 9: parasitic coil series resistance]{ref}.

The probes also add <electrical resistance> and <capacitance> to the measured nodes; their loading becomes more important as <frequency> increases. Use compensated probes with known input <electrical impedance> and short ground connections, and repeat with a smaller drive voltage to test whether the inferred $X$ depends on <amplitude>. https://www.tek.com/en/documents/application-note/how-oscilloscope-probes-affect-your-measurement[Tektronix, How Oscilloscope Probes Affect Your Measurement: resistive and capacitive loading]{ref}. Record actual voltage and phase readings separately from calculated predictions, with component tolerances and timing uncertainty. A phase error $\delta\phi$ alone produces approximately $\delta X=|Z|\cos\phi\,\delta\phi$, so near zero <electrical reactance>, a small delay can imitate a small residual $X$. Agreement within stated uncertainties tests the model; the smooth toy curves establish no experimental accuracy.

= Electric battery
{parent=Electrical circuit}
{wiki}

= Battery
{synonym}

An <electric battery> supplies <energy> to an <electrical circuit> by moving <electric charge> through a terminal <electric potential difference>. In the ideal fixed-voltage model used in <fixed-voltage capacitor energy balance>, it maintains a prescribed terminal <electric potential difference> while <electric charge> flows to or from the <capacitor>.

= RL circuit
{c}
{parent=Electrical circuit}
{title2=$L\,dI/dt+RI=V$}
{wiki}

An <RL circuit> combines <electrical resistance> $R$ and <self-inductance> $L$. In the series model, the same <electric current> $I$ flows through both components. An ideal <battery> maintains terminal <electric potential difference> $V$; the drops in the direction of positive <electric current> obey <Ohm's law> and the <inductor> relation:
$$
v_R=RI,\qquad v_L=L\frac{dI}{dt},\qquad
V=v_R+v_L.
$$
This is <Kirchhoff's voltage law> with the <inductor>'s terminal drop, rather than its oppositely signed induced <electromotive force>. For constant $R>0$, $L>0$, and $V$, the <first-order linear differential equation> has solution
$$
I(t)=I_\infty+(I(0)-I_\infty)e^{-t/\tau},\qquad
I_\infty=\frac VR,\qquad \tau=\frac LR.
$$
The <time constant> $\tau$ measures <exponential decay> of the difference from the final <electric current>. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-2/pages/14-4-rl-circuits[OpenStax University Physics 2, §14.4: RL circuits]{ref}.

For a stipulated example, take <electrical resistance> $R=2\,\Omega$, <self-inductance> $L=0.5\,\mathrm H$, terminal <electric potential difference> $V=6\,\mathrm V$, and initial <electric current> $I(0)=0$. Then
$$
\tau=0.25\,\mathrm s,\qquad I_\infty=3\,\mathrm A,
\qquad I(t)=(3\,\mathrm A)(1-e^{-t/(0.25\,\mathrm s)}).
$$
With $t$ measured numerically in seconds, this is $I(t)=3(1-e^{-4t})$ in amperes. At one <time constant>,
$$
I(\tau)=3(1-e^{-1})\,\mathrm A\approx1.89636\,\mathrm A,
$$
and the terminal <electric potential difference> drops are
$$
v_R(\tau)=6(1-e^{-1})\,\mathrm V\approx3.79272\,\mathrm V,
\qquad v_L(\tau)=6e^{-1}\,\mathrm V\approx2.20728\,\mathrm V.
$$
Their sum is exactly $6\,\mathrm V$. At $t=0^+$, the <inductor> takes the entire terminal <electric potential difference> while the <electric current> is still zero. At steady <electric current>, the <inductor>'s terminal drop is zero and the entire drop is across the <electrical resistance>.

\Image[rl-current-and-voltage.png]
{title=<Electric current> and terminal <electric potential difference> drops during an <RL circuit> response}
{description=The <battery> maintains $6\,\mathrm V$ across <electrical resistance> $2\,\Omega$ and <self-inductance> $0.5\,\mathrm H$ in series. The dashed vertical line marks <time constant> $0.25\,\mathrm s$; $v_L$ uses the positive terminal-drop convention.}

The final <magnetic energy> stored by the <inductor> is $U_\infty=LI_\infty^2/2=2.25\,\mathrm J$. At one <time constant>, the <electric current> has reached about $63.21\%$ of its final value, but its <magnetic energy> has reached only about $39.96\%$:
$$
U(\tau)=\frac12L I(\tau)^2
=2.25(1-e^{-1})^2\,\mathrm J\approx0.899047\,\mathrm J.
$$
This difference follows from the square of <electric current> in the <inductor>'s <magnetic energy>.

For release, take $t=0$ at the start of a separate experiment with initial <electric current> $3\,\mathrm A$. Remove the <battery> from the model while maintaining a closed path through the same <electrical resistance> and <inductor>. Then
$$
L\frac{dI}{dt}+RI=0,\qquad
I(t)=(3\,\mathrm A)e^{-t/(0.25\,\mathrm s)}.
$$
Now $v_L=-v_R$: the induced <electromotive force> supports the continuing positive <electric current> as its magnitude decreases. This closed-loop model describes <exponential decay>, not opening the sole path and imposing an instantaneous jump to zero <electric current>. The <inductor>'s <magnetic energy> and the <power> of <Joule heating> are
$$
U(t)=(2.25\,\mathrm J)e^{-2t/\tau},\qquad
P_R(t)=RI(t)^2=(18\,\mathrm W)e^{-2t/\tau},\qquad
\frac{dU}{dt}=-P_R.
$$
Their <time constant> is $\tau/2=0.125\,\mathrm s$. The total <energy> dissipated by <Joule heating> is exactly the initial <magnetic energy>:
$$
\int_0^\infty P_R(t)\,dt
=(18\,\mathrm W)\frac{\tau}{2}=2.25\,\mathrm J.
$$
The second diagram in <inductor> shows this <energy> transfer. A finite closed path lets us predict both how long the <electric current> persists and how much <energy> becomes heat. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-2/pages/14-4-rl-circuits[OpenStax University Physics 2, §14.4: decay in a closed RL loop]{ref}.

= RLC circuit
{c}
{parent=Electrical circuit}
{wiki}

An <RLC circuit> combines <electrical resistance> $R$, <inductance> $L$, and <capacitance> $C$. The <capacitor> stores <energy> in its <electric field>; the <inductor> stores <energy> in its <magnetic field>. Their exchange can produce an <oscillation>, while <Joule heating> removes <energy>. A <series RLC circuit> gives a particularly clear example: changing $R$ turns ringing into a direct return to equilibrium, and changing $L$ or $C$ tunes a <resonance>.

These circuits connect two useful viewpoints. In time, they describe how stored <energy> is released after switching. In <frequency>, they select a range of signals: the <resonant series RLC response> explains radio tuning and simple filters. The same mathematics describes a <damped harmonic oscillator>, so insight about mechanical ringing transfers directly to an <electrical circuit>. https://www.feynmanlectures.caltech.edu/I_23.html#Ch23-S3[Feynman lectures, I.23.3: electrical and mechanical resonance]{ref}; https://www.feynmanlectures.caltech.edu/II_22.html#Ch22-S7[Feynman lectures, II.22.7: frequency filters]{ref}.

= Series RLC circuit
{parent=RLC circuit}
{title2=$Lq''+Rq'+q/C=V(t)$}

In a <series RLC circuit>, the same <electric current> $i=q'$ flows through the <resistor> with <electrical resistance> $R$, the <inductor>, and the <capacitor>. Choose $q$ as the signed <electric charge> on the <capacitor> plate into which positive $i$ flows. With terminal drops measured in that direction, <Kirchhoff's voltage law> gives
$$
v_R=Ri,\qquad v_L=Li',\qquad v_C=q/C,\qquad
Lq''+Rq'+q/C=V(t).
$$
Here $V(t)$ is the applied <electric potential difference>; assume constant $L>0$, $C>0$, and $R>0$. With the applied source set to zero and the loop kept closed, the free response satisfies
$$
q''+2\alpha q'+\omega_0^2q=0,\qquad
\alpha=\frac{R}{2L},\qquad \omega_0=\frac{1}{\sqrt{LC}}.
$$
The <angular frequency> $\omega_0$ is the value for lossless free <oscillation>; $\alpha$ is the decay parameter. The three possibilities are <underdamped RLC response> for $\alpha<\omega_0$, <critically damped RLC response> for $\alpha=\omega_0$, and <overdamped RLC response> for $\alpha>\omega_0$. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-2/pages/14-6-rlc-series-circuits[OpenStax University Physics 2, §14.6: free RLC response]{ref}.

For a fair comparison, start each circuit with the same <electric charge> $q(0)=q_0$ and zero <electric current> $q'(0)=0$. Set $\tau=\omega_0t$ and $a=\alpha/\omega_0$; then $x=q/q_0$ obeys $d^2x/d\tau^2+2a\,dx/d\tau+x=0$. The plot compares $a=0.5,1,2$. These dimensionless curves apply to any $L,C$ after rescaling time and <electric charge>.

\Image[rlc-free-damping.png]
{title=Free <series RLC circuit> responses with identical <initial conditions>}
{description=Left: <electric charge> for <underdamped RLC response>, <critically damped RLC response>, and <overdamped RLC response>. Right: total stored <energy>, which decreases in all three cases even when <electric charge> oscillates. The normalization is $\omega_0=1$, $q_0=1$, $q'(0)=0$.}

The stored <energy> is the sum of <magnetic energy> and <capacitor energy>:
$$
E=\frac12Li^2+\frac{q^2}{2C}.
$$
Differentiating and using the circuit equation gives the complete <power> balance,
$$
E'=i\left(Li'+\frac qC\right)=V(t)i-Ri^2.
$$
In the free response, $E'=-Ri^2\leq0$: <Joule heating> accounts exactly for the lost <energy>. For the plotted <initial conditions>, $E(0)=q_0^2/(2C)$ and $E/E(0)=x^2+(dx/d\tau)^2$. Since every free solution tends to zero for $R>0$,
$$
\int_0^\infty Ri(t)^2\,dt=E(0).
$$
In the lossless limit $R=0$, <energy> stays constant and shuttles between the <capacitor> and <inductor>. Thus an <oscillation> of <electric charge> and a decrease of total <energy> are compatible statements.

The analogy with a <damped harmonic oscillator> is exact within these ideal models: <electric charge> corresponds to displacement, <electric current> to <velocity>, <inductance> to <mass>, $1/C$ to <spring constant>, and $R$ to the coefficient of a resisting force proportional to <velocity>. The two stored <energy> terms play the roles of <kinetic energy> and elastic <potential energy>. https://www.feynmanlectures.caltech.edu/I_23.html#Ch23-S3[Feynman lectures, I.23.3: the oscillator analogy]{ref}.

Differentiating the driven equation also gives $Li''+Ri'+i/C=V'$. When using this equation to solve for <electric current>, retain the original constraint $Li'(0)+Ri(0)+q(0)/C=V(0)$: the initial <electric charge> fixes the initial rate of change of <electric current>.

= Resonant series RLC response
{parent=Series RLC circuit}
{title2=$I_{\mathrm{amp}}(\omega_0)=V_0/R$}

Drive a <series RLC circuit> with <electric potential difference> $V(t)=V_0\cos(\omega t)$, where $V_0>0$ and $\omega>0$. After the free transient decays, the <electric current> has the same <angular frequency> as the source:
$$
i_{\mathrm{ss}}(t)=I_{\mathrm{amp}}\cos(\omega t-\phi),\qquad
I_{\mathrm{amp}}(\omega)=\frac{V_0}{\sqrt{R^2+(\omega L-1/(\omega C))^2}},\qquad
\tan\phi=\frac{\omega L-1/(\omega C)}{R},\quad -\frac\pi2<\phi<\frac\pi2.
$$
For fixed positive $R,L,C,V_0$, the <electric current> <amplitude> is largest exactly at $\omega=\omega_0=1/\sqrt{LC}$. The two <electrical reactance> contributions cancel there, leaving only the drop from <electrical resistance>. In particular,
$$
i_{\mathrm{ss}}(t)=\frac{V_0}{R}\cos(\omega_0t),\qquad
q_{\mathrm{ss}}(t)=\frac{V_0}{R\omega_0}\sin(\omega_0t).
$$
Every solution approaches this periodic response because its difference from the displayed solution is a decaying free response. The peak remains at $\omega_0$ even for an <overdamped RLC response>; free ringing is not required for a maximum of driven <electric current>. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-2/pages/15-3-rlc-series-circuits-with-ac[OpenStax University Physics 2, §15.3: current amplitude, phase, and resonance]{ref}.

For example, stipulate $L=10\,\mathrm{mH}$, $C=1\,\mu\mathrm F$, $R=20\,\Omega$, and $V_0=1\,\mathrm V$. Then $\omega_0=10^4\,\mathrm{s}^{-1}$, $f_0=\omega_0/(2\pi)\approx1591.55\,\mathrm{Hz}$, and the peak <electric current> <amplitude> is $50\,\mathrm{mA}$. Holding the source <amplitude> fixed is essential when comparing responses at different <frequencies>.

\Image[rlc-current-resonance.png]
{title=Driven <electric current> <resonance> and the distinct <electric charge> peak}
{description=Left: fixed $L=1\,\mathrm H$, $C=1\,\mathrm F$, $V_0=1\,\mathrm V$, with positive $R=0.5,1,2,4\,\Omega$. Right: at $R=1\,\Omega$, the charge-amplitude peak, free ringing frequency, and current-amplitude peak occur at $\omega/\omega_0\approx0.707,0.866,1$, respectively. Both driven curves are divided by their own peak amplitude.}

Three <frequencies> must be distinguished. The free <underdamped RLC response> rings at $\omega_d=\sqrt{\omega_0^2-\alpha^2}$. Driven <electric current> peaks at $\omega_0$. Driven <electric charge> has <amplitude>
$$
q_{\mathrm{amp}}(\omega)=\frac{I_{\mathrm{amp}}(\omega)}{\omega}
=\frac{V_0/L}{\sqrt{(\omega_0^2-\omega^2)^2+4\alpha^2\omega^2}},
$$
whose peak occurs at $\sqrt{\omega_0^2-2\alpha^2}$ only if $\alpha<\omega_0/\sqrt2$. Otherwise it decreases from its zero-frequency limit $CV_0$, with no positive-frequency peak. The different peak locations follow because <electric current> is the time <derivative> of <electric charge>, multiplying a sinusoidal <amplitude> by $\omega$. The limiting responses are $I_{\mathrm{amp}}\sim V_0C\omega$ as $\omega\to0^+$ and $I_{\mathrm{amp}}\sim V_0/(L\omega)$ as $\omega\to\infty$.

The <Q factor> measures selectivity:
$$
Q=\frac{L\omega_0}{R}=\frac{\omega_0}{2\alpha}.
$$
The average <Joule heating> <power> is $RI_{\mathrm{amp}}^2/2$. Its half-maximum <angular frequencies> and their separation are exactly
$$
\omega_\pm=\sqrt{\omega_0^2+\alpha^2}\pm\alpha,\qquad
\Delta\omega=\omega_+-\omega_-=2\alpha=\frac RL=\frac{\omega_0}{Q}.
$$
These follow by setting $|\omega L-1/(\omega C)|=R$. In the numerical example, $Q=5$ and $\Delta\omega=2000\,\mathrm{s}^{-1}$. Reducing $R$ makes a taller, narrower <electric current> peak; varying $C$ tunes the selected <frequency>. Measuring the drop $v_R=Ri$ gives a <band-pass filter> that suppresses very low and very high <frequencies> while passing a range near <resonance>. This is useful for selecting a radio signal, though the range must be broad enough to pass its modulation. https://www.feynmanlectures.caltech.edu/II_22.html#Ch22-S7[Feynman lectures, II.22.7: resonant filters and radio sidebands]{ref}.

= Overdamped RLC response
{parent=Series RLC circuit}
{title2=$\alpha>\omega_0$}

An <overdamped RLC response> is a free <series RLC circuit> response with $R>2\sqrt{L/C}$, equivalently $\alpha>\omega_0$. Put $\beta=\sqrt{\alpha^2-\omega_0^2}$. The distinct negative <characteristic roots> are $r_\pm=-\alpha\pm\beta$, so the general <electric charge> is
$$
q(t)=Ae^{r_+t}+Be^{r_-t},\qquad i(t)=q'(t).
$$
For <initial conditions> $q(0)=q_0$, $i(0)=0$,
$$
q(t)=q_0e^{-\alpha t}\left(\cosh(\beta t)+\frac\alpha\beta\sinh(\beta t)\right),\qquad
i(t)=-\frac{q_0\omega_0^2}{\beta}e^{-\alpha t}\sinh(\beta t).
$$
For $q_0>0$, this <electric charge> decreases to zero without reversing sign. In the dimensionless example $\omega_0=1$, $\alpha=2$, the <characteristic roots> are $-2\pm\sqrt3\approx-0.268,-3.732$. The slow term dominates late times. Stronger <damping> can therefore make the final return slower: for $\alpha\gg\omega_0$, $r_+\sim-\omega_0^2/(2\alpha)=-1/(RC)$. Nonoscillation alone does not imply a fast response, and monotonicity here uses the specified <initial conditions>.

= Critically damped RLC response
{parent=Series RLC circuit}
{title2=$\alpha=\omega_0,\quad R=2\sqrt{L/C}$}

A <critically damped RLC response> is the boundary between <underdamped RLC response> and <overdamped RLC response>. The free <series RLC circuit> has a repeated <characteristic root> $-\omega_0$, with general solution
$$
q(t)=(A+Bt)e^{-\omega_0t}.
$$
For <initial conditions> $q(0)=q_0$, $i(0)=0$,
$$
q(t)=q_0(1+\omega_0t)e^{-\omega_0t},\qquad
i(t)=-q_0\omega_0^2t e^{-\omega_0t}.
$$
In the plotted normalization, $q(1)=2/e\approx0.735759$ and $i(1)=-1/e\approx-0.367879$. The <electric charge> returns without ringing; the <polynomial> factor $1+\omega_0t$ matters, so a single <exponential function> does not give the whole response.

For fixed $L,C$ and these <initial conditions>, <critical damping> avoids ringing while giving a faster asymptotic return than any larger $R$: the <overdamped RLC response> has a root closer to zero. This makes <critical damping> a useful tuning target for settling after switching. An MIT laboratory exercise makes the distinction observable by asking students to compare all three cases with $L=47\,\mathrm{mH}$ and $C=47\,\mathrm{nF}$. Those values give $R_{\mathrm{crit}}=2\sqrt{L/C}=2000\,\Omega$ and $\omega_0\approx21276.6\,\mathrm{s}^{-1}$. https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/6-071j-introduction-to-electronics-signals-and-measurement-spring-2006/491e7e50ee65521c601ee51d3c6f7e5f_lab15_transients.pdf[MIT 6.071, Spring 2006, Laboratory 15, exercise 2]{ref}.

= Underdamped RLC response
{parent=Series RLC circuit}
{title2=$\omega_d=\sqrt{\omega_0^2-\alpha^2}$}

An <underdamped RLC response> is a free <series RLC circuit> response with $0<R<2\sqrt{L/C}$, equivalently $0<\alpha<\omega_0$. Its <characteristic roots> are $-\alpha\pm\mathrm{i}\omega_d$, and its general <electric charge> is
$$
q(t)=e^{-\alpha t}\left(A\cos(\omega_dt)+B\sin(\omega_dt)\right).
$$
For <initial conditions> $q(0)=q_0$, $i(0)=0$,
$$
q(t)=q_0e^{-\alpha t}\left(\cos(\omega_dt)+\frac\alpha{\omega_d}\sin(\omega_dt)\right),\qquad
i(t)=-\frac{q_0\omega_0^2}{\omega_d}e^{-\alpha t}\sin(\omega_dt).
$$
In the dimensionless example $\omega_0=1$, $\alpha=0.5$, the ringing <angular frequency> is $\omega_d=\sqrt3/2\approx0.866025$, below the driven <electric current> <resonance> at $\omega_0=1$. The <electric charge> first crosses zero at $t=4\pi/(3\sqrt3)\approx2.41840$ and reaches its first negative extremum at $t=2\pi/\sqrt3\approx3.62760$, where $q/q_0=-e^{-\pi/\sqrt3}\approx-0.163034$.

The sinusoidal factor changes sign while its envelope decays as $e^{-\alpha t}$. The total <energy> follows the exact balance $E'=-Ri^2$, rather than a pure <exponential decay> at every instant; in particular, its rate of loss is zero whenever $i=0$. As $\alpha\to0$, the displayed solution tends to the lossless <harmonic oscillator> $q=q_0\cos(\omega_0t)$. As $\alpha\to\omega_0$ from below, $\sin(\omega_dt)/\omega_d\to t$, recovering the <critically damped RLC response> continuously. The <overdamped RLC response> reaches the same limit through $\sinh(\beta t)/\beta\to t$.

= Gaussian units
{c}
{parent=Electromagnetism}
{wiki}

= Capacitor
{parent=Electromagnetism}
{wiki}

A <capacitor> stores <electrostatic energy> by separating <electric charge> between two <electrical conductors>. In the ideal two-terminal model their <electric charges> are $+Q$ and $-Q$, with a vacuum or insulating gap preventing direct transfer between them. Write $V=\phi_+-\phi_-$ for the terminal <electric potential difference>, where $\phi_+$ and $\phi_-$ are the <electric potentials> of the two <electrical conductors>. A linear <capacitor> obeys
$$
Q=CV,
$$
where its <capacitance> $C$ depends on the geometry and <permittivity>, and is independent of the applied <electric potential difference> while these stay fixed. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-2/pages/8-1-capacitors-and-capacitance[OpenStax University Physics 2, §8.1: capacitors and capacitance]{ref}.

For example, the <parallel-plate capacitor> below has <capacitance> $C=88.54\,\mathrm{pF}$. At terminal <electric potential difference> $V=10\,\mathrm V$, the <electric charge> magnitude on either plate is
$$
Q=CV=(8.854\times10^{-11})(10)\,\mathrm C
=8.854\times10^{-10}\,\mathrm C=0.8854\,\mathrm{nC}.
$$
The total <electric charge> of the device is $Q+(-Q)=0$: <charge neutrality> does not imply zero <capacitor energy>. This <capacitor> stores $4.427\,\mathrm{nJ}$ in its <electric field>. The schematic in <parallel-plate capacitor> shows the separation of <electric charge>; the plots in <capacitor energy> show how storage grows with terminal <electric potential difference>.

The useful distinction is between storing net <electric charge> and storing separated <electric charge>. A <capacitor> can release <electrostatic energy> into an <electrical circuit>; in an ideal <RLC circuit>, <energy> moves between the <capacitor>'s <electric field> and the <magnetic field> associated with <inductance>. An ideal disconnected <capacitor> retains its separated <electric charges> and <capacitor energy> because there is no path for discharge.

= Parallel-plate capacitor
{parent=Capacitor}
{title2=$C=\epsilon A/d$}

A <parallel-plate capacitor> consists of two parallel <electrical conductors> with overlapping <area> $A$ and separation $d$. If the gap is small compared with both plate widths and is filled by a uniform material of positive <permittivity> $\epsilon$, its <capacitance> is approximately
$$
C\simeq\frac{\epsilon A}{d}.
$$
This neglects the <fringing field of a capacitor> near the plate edges. The interior <electric field> points from the plate of positive <electric charge> to the plate of negative <electric charge>, and is approximately uniform. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-2/pages/8-1-capacitors-and-capacitance[OpenStax University Physics 2, §8.1: the parallel-plate approximation]{ref}.

For a worked vacuum example, stipulate $A=0.01\,\mathrm{m^2}$, $d=0.001\,\mathrm m$, and <vacuum permittivity> $\epsilon_0\approx8.854\times10^{-12}\,\mathrm{F/m}$. This rounds the value in <Fundamental Physical Constants — Complete Listing by National Institute of Standards and Technology and others>. Within the approximation,
$$
C\simeq\frac{(8.854\times10^{-12})(0.01)}{0.001}\,\mathrm F
=8.854\times10^{-11}\,\mathrm F=88.54\,\mathrm{pF}.
$$
At terminal <electric potential difference> $V=10\,\mathrm V$, the <electric field> magnitude is $E\simeq V/d=10^4\,\mathrm{V/m}$, the plate <electric charge> magnitude is $Q=CV=0.8854\,\mathrm{nC}$, and the <capacitor energy> is $U=CV^2/2=4.427\,\mathrm{nJ}$. These numbers use the stipulated rounded <vacuum permittivity>; finite plates also have corrections from the <fringing field of a capacitor>.

\Image[parallel-plate-capacitor-field.png]
{title=Uniform <electric field> in a <parallel-plate capacitor>}

The formula makes design changes easy to predict: doubling the overlapping <area> doubles the <capacitance>, and halving the gap doubles the <capacitance>, with <permittivity> fixed and the <fringing field of a capacitor> neglected. Doubling <permittivity> also doubles <capacitance> if the material uniformly fills the gap and remains linear. This lets a compact device store more separated <electric charge> at the same terminal <electric potential difference>.

The short derivation uses <surface charge density> $\sigma=Q/A$. In the interior, <Gauss's law> gives <electric field> magnitude $E\simeq\sigma/\epsilon=Q/(\epsilon A)$. The <electric potential difference> is $V\simeq Ed=Qd/(\epsilon A)$, so dividing $Q$ by $V$ gives $C\simeq\epsilon A/d$. A narrow curved <capacitor> gap has the same leading local approximation when its width is much less than the curvature radius.

= Fringing field of a capacitor
{parent=Parallel-plate capacitor}

= Capacitor fringing field
{synonym}

The <fringing field of a capacitor> is the nonuniform <electric field> near the edges of its <electrical conductors>, extending outside the gap. For a <parallel-plate capacitor>, neglecting it is useful when the gap is much smaller than both plate widths; the approximation $C\simeq\epsilon A/d$ is less reliable when these lengths become comparable.

Finite plates break the translational <symmetry> of the infinite-plate model. Each <conductor in electrostatic equilibrium> is an <equipotential>, so the <electric field> must meet a smooth conducting surface normally. Near an edge, the <equipotentials> curve into the surrounding space and the <electric field> changes both direction and magnitude. The <surface charge density> adjusts to enforce the conducting <boundary conditions>; it is not uniform all the way to a sharp edge. The <electrostatic boundary conditions at a conductor> explain why zero <electric field> inside the metal does not imply zero <electric field> just outside it.

The practical issue is where the nearly uniform region ends. A probe near the centre can support $E\simeq V/d$ while a probe close to an edge detects a lateral component and a different magnitude. A total <capacitance> reading also includes <electric flux> outside the simple footprint. The <fringing field of a capacitor> can therefore matter in <permittivity> measurements even when a central <electric field> measurement looks nearly ideal. Keysight's three-<electrode> fixture diverts the fringe contribution away from the measured response.<Materials Measurement: Dielectric Materials>[Solution: fringe capacitance and the three-electrode 16451B fixture]{ref}

\Image[capacitor-fringing-field-and-guard-electrode.png]
{title=Finite-plate <electric field> and a separate <guard electrode of a capacitor>}
{description=Left: original <bounded two-dimensional capacitor field model> with $d/W=0.25$, showing computed <equipotentials> and <electric field> direction. The visible window is a crop of the stated bounded domain. Right: a conceptual cross-section through a surrounding guard ring, with an insulated central sensing <electrode>; the arrows are schematic, not a computed guarded solution. Neither panel is measured data.}

= Capacitor gap aspect ratio
{parent=Fringing field of a capacitor}
{title2=$\eta=d/W$}

The <capacitor gap aspect ratio> compares the gap $d$ with a lateral plate width $W$. For rectangular plates of widths $W_x,W_z$, require both $d/W_x\ll1$ and $d/W_z\ll1$ for the leading <parallel-plate capacitor> approximation. Large <area> alone is insufficient: a long narrow strip can have large <area> and still have $d$ comparable with its smaller width. In addition, the observation point must be many gap widths from an edge. Reducing $d/W$ improves the interior approximation without making the edge itself disappear.

In a two-dimensional straight gap, write the <electric potential> as the infinite-plate solution plus a perturbation:
$$
\phi(x,y)=\frac{V}{d}y+\delta\phi(x,y),\qquad -\frac d2<y<\frac d2.
$$
The perturbation satisfies the <Laplace equation> and vanishes on the two ideal plates. <Separation of variables> in a semi-infinite strip, with $s$ the distance inward from its entrance, gives transverse modes
$$
\delta\phi_n(s,y)=a_n e^{-n\pi s/d}\sin\!\left(\frac{n\pi(y+d/2)}{d}\right),\qquad n=1,2,\ldots.
$$
Thus edge disturbances have a natural penetration scale set by $d$. Modes forbidden by a particular <symmetry> have zero coefficients; this expression is an explanation of localization in an ideal strip, not a universal numerical correction to finite-plate <capacitance>. The outside <boundary conditions> determine the coefficients.

\Image[capacitor-fringing-small-gap-aspect-ratio.png]
{title=<Capacitor gap aspect ratio> and the width of the nearly uniform <electric field> region}
{description=Three original solutions of the same <bounded two-dimensional capacitor field model> at $d/W=0.10,0.25,0.50$. The lower curves show $-E_y d/V$ on the midplane $y=0$; the dashed reference is the infinite-plate value inside the gap. The dashed value is not the infinite-plate prediction outside the plate footprint. All curves are calculated, with the same mesh and outer <Dirichlet boundary conditions>; they are neither measurements nor an exact open-domain three-dimensional <capacitance> calculation.}

There is no single aspect-ratio cutoff guaranteeing an assigned accuracy: the requested observable, plate shape and thickness, surroundings, <dielectric> distribution, and guard geometry all matter. The small-gap approximation is local and is not uniform across an edge. It also does not justify extrapolating $C\simeq\epsilon A/d$ to arbitrarily large $d$, where finite <electrical conductors> and their external <electric field> dominate. At fixed $V$, formally shrinking an ideal gap makes the interior <electric field> grow like $1/d$; real <electric currents> across an imperfectly insulating gap, surface roughness and contact eventually invalidate that ideal limit.

= Bounded two-dimensional capacitor field model
{parent=Fringing field of a capacitor}
{title2=$\partial_x^2\phi+\partial_y^2\phi=0$}

The diagrams solve an explicitly bounded <Dirichlet problem> in a uniform charge-free medium of constant <permittivity>. Take a width scale $W=1$ and terminal <electric potential difference> $V=1$. Two zero-thickness ideal plate segments occupy $|x|\leq W/2$ at $y=\pm d/2$, with <electric potentials> $\pm V/2$. Fix $\phi=0$ on the entire rectangle $x=\pm2W$, $y=\pm1.5W$. This boundary is an additional enclosing <equipotential>, rather than a condition at infinity. The model is invariant along the omitted third direction. The connection between the <electric potential>, its <gradient>, and the charge-free <Laplace equation> follows from <electrostatics>.<The Feynman Lectures on Physics, Volume II by Richard Feynman and others>[II.6–1, equations (6.3)–(6.6)]{ref}

On a square mesh of spacing $h=W/160$, the <finite difference method> imposes
$$
\phi_{i,j}=\frac14\left(\phi_{i+1,j}+\phi_{i-1,j}+\phi_{i,j+1}+\phi_{i,j-1}\right)
$$
at free nodes, while plate and outer nodes retain their prescribed <electric potentials>. There are $641\times481$ nodes. Each plotted gap aligns exactly with the mesh; the smallest gap has 16 mesh intervals. The generators solve the resulting <linear system> by the <conjugate gradient method>, stopping when the maximum dimensionless five-point residual is below $2\times10^{-10}$, and use <central finite differences> to approximate $\mathbf E=-\nabla\phi$ away from plate nodes.

The <discrete maximum principle> keeps the computed <electric potential> between $-V/2$ and $+V/2$. Reflection checks require evenness in $x$ and oddness in $y$; halving $h$ tests discretization effects, while enlarging the outer rectangle tests sensitivity to its artificial <boundary conditions>. These are different checks: a small residual only establishes that the discrete equations were solved. Differentiating near zero-thickness endpoints is especially sensitive to the mesh, so the plots do not report a converged edge peak. No total <electric charge>, exact open-domain three-dimensional <capacitance>, or measured material property is extracted from this model.

For a bounded numerical check, compare $h=W/80$ with the plotted $h=W/160$ at midplane positions $x/W=0,0.25,0.40,0.50,0.60,0.80$. At $d/W=0.10$, the largest change in $-E_y d/V$ is approximately $0.0265$, at $x/W=0.50$; the other five sampled changes are below $0.00472$. At $d/W=0.25$, moving the outer rectangle from $\pm2W\times\pm1.5W$ to $\pm3W\times\pm2W$ while retaining $h=W/80$ changes these samples by at most approximately $0.00622$. These calculated sensitivities support the qualitative comparison and expose its limits; they are not experimental uncertainties or evidence that the artificial boundary has converged to infinity.

= Mapping a capacitor fringing field
{parent=Fringing field of a capacitor}

The <Field Mapper Kit (PK-9023), Product Manual 012-04346D by PASCO scientific> (p. 3, Parallel plate capacitor) proposes a direct investigation of the plate-length-to-gap ratio using <conductive-paper electric potential mapping>. Its finite straight <electrodes> represent a cross-section of the plates. The apparatus is a gridded resistive sheet on a corkboard, silver-ink <electrodes>, pushpin electrical contacts, a $5$–$20\,\mathrm V$ direct supply capable of $25\,\mathrm{mA}$, and a <voltmeter> with input <electrical resistance> at least $10\,\mathrm{M\Omega}$. The manual specifies waiting 20 minutes for the ink to reach its maximum <electrical conductivity>, then checking each <electrode>: its internal voltage drop should be no more than $1\%$ of the applied <electric potential difference>.<Field Mapper Kit (PK-9023), Product Manual 012-04346D by PASCO scientific>[pp. 1–2: required equipment, drying, contacts, electrode-conductivity check]{ref}

For the readout, keep one <voltmeter> lead on the reference <electrode> and scan the other across the sheet. Mark points having the same reading to trace an <equipotential>. A second method holds the two probe tips at a fixed separation and pivots them to find the largest <electric potential difference>; a suggested separation is one centimetre. Avoid printed grid marks when making contact.<Charge, Equipotential, and Field Mapper (ES-9060), Instruction Manual by PASCO scientific>[pp. 8–10, Parts III–IV: equipotential tracing and paired-probe field-gradient mapping]{ref}

The inference follows from the measured <electric potential>, rather than assuming straight <electric field> lines. With probe separation $\Delta s$ and direction $\widehat{\mathbf t}$, a local <finite difference> gives
$$
\mathbf E\cdot\widehat{\mathbf t}\simeq-\frac{\phi(P+\Delta s\widehat{\mathbf t})-\phi(P)}{\Delta s}.
$$
An arrow toward rising <electric potential> follows $+\nabla\phi$, so it must be reversed to show $\mathbf E$. To investigate the <fringing field of a capacitor>, compare contours well inside the gap with contours near and beyond the ends, then repeat at a changed gap while keeping the sheet boundary and plate length fixed. Curving contours and an outside <gradient> establish nonuniformity in this planar setup. Compare $-E_y d/V$ at recorded positions to distinguish simple voltage rescaling from geometric changes; no measured values are supplied here.

Additional useful controls are repeat reference readings to detect drift, reversal of supply polarity, and repeating selected readings with reduced probe spacing. Keep contact quality and sheet edges fixed during the aspect-ratio comparison. Finite probe separation averages the <gradient>; reducing it improves spatial resolution but magnifies the effect of voltage uncertainty. <Voltmeter loading>, imperfect ink <electrodes>, and inhomogeneous sheet <electrical conductivity> can mimic distortions. These proposed checks supplement the manufacturer's electrode check, rather than claiming a completed experiment.

The sheet carries a steady <electric current>. For uniform <electrical conductivity>, <Ohm's law> and <electric charge conservation> give the two-dimensional <Laplace equation> away from <electrodes>, as explained in <conductive-paper electric potential mapping>. Uncontacted sheet edges have zero normal <electric current>, whereas the diagram model fixes zero outer <electric potential>. These distinct <boundary conditions> must be matched before a quantitative comparison. The apparatus reveals a planar <electric potential> map; it does not directly measure the free-space three-dimensional <capacitance> of metal plates.

= Guard electrode of a capacitor
{parent=Fringing field of a capacitor}

A <guard electrode of a capacitor> surrounds an electrically isolated sensing <electrode> and is held at the same <electric potential>. The small insulating slot separates their readout paths. The guard extends the nearly planar <equipotential>, moving the main outer edge away from the sensing region; it does not abolish the <fringing field of a capacitor> everywhere. In a capacitance fixture, fringe <electric current> is routed through the guard instead of the sensing readout. Keysight describes this three-<electrode> method in <Materials Measurement: Dielectric Materials> (Solution, 16451B fixture).<Materials Measurement: Dielectric Materials>[Solution: guard absorbs current through fringe capacitance]{ref}

Measuring the sensing and guard <electric currents> together would include the diverted response again. Grounding an arbitrary surrounding <electrode> also does not reproduce a guard unless its <electric potential> matches the sensing <electrode>. Finite guard width, the insulating slot, plate misalignment and specimen air gaps limit the approximation. Use <capacitance measurement calibration> and the geometry appropriate to the sensing region when inferring <permittivity>. The source also warns that guarding changes the <electromagnetic field> unfavourably at high <frequency>, so the low-<frequency> electrostatic picture has a restricted range.<Materials Measurement: Dielectric Materials>[Solution: air gaps, high-frequency guard limitations, fixture compensation]{ref}

= Spherical capacitor
{parent=Capacitor}
{title2=$C=4\pi\epsilon_0ab/(b-a)$}

Two concentric conducting spherical shells of radii $a<b$ with charges $Q,-Q$ have an <electric field> only in the intervening vacuum: $E_r=Q/(4\pi\epsilon_0r^2)$ by <Gauss's law>. Integrating gives a potential difference $Q(1/a-1/b)/(4\pi\epsilon_0)$, and hence the displayed <capacitance>. The <electrostatic energy> is $Q^2/(2C)$, either by charging work or by integrating $\epsilon_0|E|^2/2$ over the gap.

= Capacitor energy
{title2=$U=Q^2/(2C)$}
{parent=Capacitor}

The <capacitor energy> is the <electrostatic energy> stored when <electric charge> is separated. For a linear <capacitor> of fixed positive <capacitance> $C$, plate <electric charge> magnitude $Q$, and terminal <electric potential difference> $V=Q/C$, it is
$$
U=\frac{Q^2}{2C}=\frac12CV^2=\frac12QV.
$$
The zero is the uncharged state. Thus the <capacitor energy> is positive for nonzero $Q$, even though the total <electric charge> of the two plates is zero. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-2/pages/8-3-energy-stored-in-a-capacitor[OpenStax University Physics 2, §8.3: energy stored in a capacitor]{ref}.

For the <parallel-plate capacitor> example, <capacitance> $C=8.854\times10^{-11}\,\mathrm F$ and terminal <electric potential difference> $V=10\,\mathrm V$ give
$$
U=\frac12(8.854\times10^{-11})(10)^2\,\mathrm J
=4.427\times10^{-9}\,\mathrm J=4.427\,\mathrm{nJ}.
$$
The plate <electric charge> magnitude is $Q=0.8854\,\mathrm{nC}$. Doubling terminal <electric potential difference> with <capacitance> unchanged doubles the plate <electric charge> magnitude and quadruples the <capacitor energy>.

\Image[capacitor-charge-and-energy.png]
{title=Linear <electric charge> and quadratic <capacitor energy> versus <electric potential difference>}

The <electric charge> is a <linear function> of terminal <electric potential difference>, while <capacitor energy> is a <quadratic function>. The second curve in each plot uses twice the <capacitance>: at a given terminal <electric potential difference>, both plate <electric charge> magnitude and <capacitor energy> double. These relations let us calculate how much <energy> an <electrical circuit> can receive when a <capacitor> discharges.

A brief charging argument explains the factor $1/2$. If the plate <electric charge> magnitude has reached $q$, the terminal <electric potential difference> is $q/C$. Transferring a further amount $dq$ of <electric charge> quasistatically requires <work> $dW=(q/C)\,dq$. With the geometry and <permittivity> fixed, the <integral> is
$$
U=\int_0^Q\frac qC\,dq=\frac{Q^2}{2C}.
$$
Using $Q=CV$ gives the other two forms. This <integral> accounts for the gradually rising terminal <electric potential difference>; it is the <work> stored in the <capacitor>'s <electric field>, rather than a claim that all <energy> drawn from a fixed-voltage <battery> during charging ends up stored.

The field picture gives the same result. In the vacuum gap of a <parallel-plate capacitor>, the <electric field> has <energy density> $u=\epsilon_0 E^2/2$. Multiplying by the gap <volume> $Ad$ gives the <electrostatic energy>
$$
U\simeq\frac12\epsilon_0E^2Ad
=\frac12\frac{\epsilon_0 A}{d}V^2
=\frac12CV^2.
$$
For the worked example, $u=4.427\times10^{-4}\,\mathrm{J/m^3}$ and $Ad=10^{-5}\,\mathrm{m^3}$ reproduce $U=4.427\,\mathrm{nJ}$. The <electrostatic energy> resides in the <electric field> throughout the gap, despite overall <charge neutrality>.

When <capacitance> changes, specify the constraint. At fixed plate <electric charge> magnitude $Q$, increasing <capacitance> decreases <capacitor energy> $Q^2/(2C)$; doubling <capacitance> halves it. At fixed terminal <electric potential difference> $V$, increasing <capacitance> increases <capacitor energy> $CV^2/2$; doubling <capacitance> doubles it. In the latter case a connected <battery> supplies additional <electric charge> and <energy>, and there may also be mechanical <work>. Use the <fixed-voltage capacitor energy balance> to account for all transfers before inferring a <force> from changing <capacitor energy>.

= Fixed-voltage capacitor energy balance
{parent=Capacitor energy}
{title2=$dU_{\rm field}=dW_{\rm mech}+V\,dQ$}

For a quasistatic change of <capacitance> at fixed terminal <electric potential difference>, the <capacitor energy> is $U=CV^2/2$ and plate <electric charge> is $Q=CV$. Electrical <work> supplied to the <electric field> by the <battery> is $V\,dQ$, so mechanical <work> done on the <capacitor> is $dW_{\rm mech}=dU-V\,dQ=-V^2dC/2$. Increasing the separation of a <parallel-plate capacitor> makes $dC<0$: mechanical <work> is positive even though <electrostatic energy> decreases, because electrical <energy> returns to the source. Conversely, when $dC>0$, the <battery> supplies $V^2dC$: half increases <capacitor energy> and half is available as mechanical <work> done by the <electric field>. Differentiating <capacitor energy> alone at fixed terminal <electric potential difference> omits this exchange with the <battery>.

= Electromagnetic interaction
{parent=Electromagnetism}

The electromagnetic interaction is the long-range interaction of electrically charged matter with the electromagnetic field. Its massless gauge boson is the <photon>.

= Electromagnetic radiation
{parent=Electromagnetism}
{wiki}

<Electromagnetic radiation> consists of propagating oscillations of the <electric field> and <magnetic field> that carry <energy> and <momentum>.

= X-ray
{c}
{parent=Electromagnetic radiation}

X-rays are high-frequency <electromagnetic radiation>, conventionally with wavelengths of roughly $0.01$ to $10\,\mathrm{nm}$. The precise wavelength convention varies with context. Hot accreting gas and energetic electronic processes can emit X-rays, whose short wavelengths make them useful probes of compact objects and atomic structure.

= Larmor formula
{parent=Electromagnetic radiation}
{c}
{title2=$P=\frac{q^2\mu_0a^2}{6\pi c}$}
{wiki}

The nonrelativistic radiated power of an accelerated point charge is $P=q^2\mu_0a^2/(6\pi c)$ in <SI> units. Integrating its dipole radiation pattern over solid angle gives this result; relativistic motion requires the Lienard generalization.

= Liénard radiated-power formula
{c}
{parent=Larmor formula}
{title2=$P=\mu_0q^2\gamma^6(|a|^2-|v\times a|^2)/(6\pi)$, $c=1$}

= Lienard formula
{c}
{synonym}

The relativistic generalization of the <Larmor formula> uses the invariant proper-acceleration magnitude: $P=-\mu_0q^2 A^aA_a/(6\pi)$ in signature $(+---)$. In coordinate time this is the displayed expression. For perpendicular magnetic circular motion, $\gamma m|a|=|q|vB$, giving $P=\mu_0q^4(\gamma^2-1)B^2/(6\pi m^2)$. The statement concerns total instantaneous emitted power; directional radiation flux is not itself invariant.

= Mie scattering
{parent=Electromagnetic radiation}
{c}
{wiki}

The electromagnetic scattering solution for a sphere describes the transition between <Rayleigh scattering> and <geometrical optics>. Grain-size-dependent efficiencies can produce structure in the <radiation-pressure coefficient>.

= Bremsstrahlung
{parent=Electromagnetic radiation}
{wiki}

<Bremsstrahlung> is <electromagnetic radiation> emitted when a charged particle accelerates during interaction with another charged particle or field. The microscopic process $e^-+Z\to e^-+Z+\gamma$ creates a <photon>, while its inverse absorbs one. Thus it contributes to a photon-number collision source in the <photon number current>, unlike ordinary <Compton scattering>. <Thermal bremsstrahlung> averages this emission over a thermal distribution of charged particles.

= Thermal radiation
{parent=Electromagnetic radiation}
{wiki}

Thermal radiation is <electromagnetic radiation> emitted by matter because of its temperature. Matter in <thermal equilibrium> emits a spectrum governed by its absorptivity, approaching the <Planck law> for a <blackbody>.

= Scattering efficiency
{parent=Electromagnetic radiation}
{title2=$Q_{\rm sca}=\sigma/(\pi a^2)$}

For a <sphere> of <radius> $a$, the <scattering efficiency> is its effective scattering <area> divided by the projected <geometric cross-section> $\pi a^2$. It is a <dimensionless quantity>. For <Rayleigh scattering by a small sphere> with relative <refractive index> $m$ and incident <wavenumber> $k$,
$$
Q_{\rm sca}=\frac{\sigma}{\pi a^2}=\frac83(ka)^4\left(\frac{m^2-1}{m^2+2}\right)^2.
$$
At fixed <wavelength> and fixed <refractive index>, the <Rayleigh scattering cross-section> scales as $a^6$, whereas the <scattering efficiency> scales as $a^4$. Doubling <radius> multiplies the former by $64$ and the latter by $16$. An efficiency here is an effective scattering <area> relative to a reference <area>, rather than a fraction of all light in an arbitrary beam.

For a worked toy example, take $a=10\,\mathrm{nm}$, $m=1.5$, and $\lambda=550\,\mathrm{nm}$. Then $ka=2\pi(10/550)\simeq0.1142$ and $(m^2-1)/(m^2+2)=5/17$. Substitution gives <Rayleigh scattering cross-section> $\sigma\simeq1.234\times10^{-20}\,\mathrm{m}^2$ and <scattering efficiency> $Q_{\rm sca}\simeq3.929\times10^{-5}$. At incident <light intensity> $1\,\mathrm{W\,m^{-2}}$, the scattered <power> is approximately $1.234\times10^{-20}\,\mathrm W$. These are leading small-particle estimates, not a measured air-<molecule> cross-section.

= Rayleigh scattering
{c}
{parent=Electromagnetic radiation}
{wiki}

<Rayleigh scattering> is <elastic scattering> of <electromagnetic radiation> by particles much smaller than the incident <wavelength>. In the stationary particle approximation, the scattered <electromagnetic radiation> keeps the incident <frequency>: its direction changes, rather than its color. The incident <electric field> induces an oscillating <electric dipole moment>, which produces <electric dipole radiation>. This is a useful example of how a microscopic response can explain a familiar large-scale sight, <why the sky is blue>. https://farside.ph.utexas.edu/teaching/jk1/lectures/node108.html[Richard Fitzpatrick, Rayleigh Scattering: bound-charge response and wavelength dependence]{ref}.

The smallness condition for <Rayleigh scattering by a small sphere> of <radius> $a$ is $ka\ll1$, where the <wavenumber> is $k=2\pi/\lambda$ and $\lambda$ is the surrounding medium's <wavelength>. For ordinary materials with modest <refractive index>, the incident <electric field> then varies little across the particle, making the <electric-dipole approximation for radiation> useful. For very large <refractive index>, also check the internal size $|m|ka\ll1$ before applying the simple <electric dipole> model. Once these conditions fail, use <Mie scattering> for a <sphere> instead of extrapolating the <Rayleigh scattering wavelength dependence>.

Historically, the 1871 paper https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Scientific_Papers/Volume_1/On_the_Light_from_the_Sky,_Its_Polarization_and_Colour[Rayleigh, On the Light from the Sky, Its Polarization and Colour: original-paper transcription]{ref} connected the color and <polarization of an electromagnetic wave> of scattered skylight with particles smaller than the <wavelength>. Its <dimensional analysis> anticipates scattered <light intensity> proportional to particle <volume> squared and to $\lambda^{-4}$.
