Here the mobile species is <electrons>. With the alternative $e^{+i\omega t}$ convention, the denominator is $1+i\omega\tau$ and the <imaginary part> changes sign; the physical response is identical.<Lecture notes on the electrodynamics of metals and superconductors>[p. 2, Eqs. (1)–(11), and pp. 5–6, Eqs. (23)–(27)]{ref}

For $E_x(t)=E_0\cos\omega t$ with $\omega>0$, the real-time <current density> is
$$
J_x(t)=\frac{\sigma_0E_0}{\sqrt{1+\omega^2\tau^2}}\cos(\omega t-\phi),\qquad
\phi=\arctan(\omega\tau).
$$
Thus a positive <argument> of <complex conductivity> in the $e^{-i\omega t}$ convention means that <current density> lags the <electric field>. At $\omega\tau=1$, its <real part> and <imaginary part> are both $\sigma_0/2$, its <modulus> is $\sigma_0/\sqrt2$, and the lag is $\pi/4$.

\Image[drude-complex-conductivity-frequency-response.png]
{title=<Drude frequency response>: dissipative component, amplitude, and <phase> lag}
{description=Exact dimensionless <Drude model> curves with $e^{-i\omega t}$ time dependence. The <real part> controls average electrical <power> absorption; the positive <imaginary part> describes delayed <current density>. The dotted line marks $\omega\tau=1$.}
{source=drude-complex-conductivity-frequency-response.py}

For $\omega\tau\ll1$, <complex conductivity> tends to the positive constant $\sigma_0$: <current density> nearly follows the <electric field>. For $\omega\tau\gg1$ within this model,
$$
\sigma(\omega)\sim\frac{i ne^2}{m\omega},\qquad
\operatorname{Re}\sigma\sim\frac{ne^2}{m\omega^2\tau},\qquad
\phi\longrightarrow\frac{\pi}{2}.
$$
The <current density> amplitude decreases and its response becomes predominantly inertial. Average <power> absorbed per volume is $\overline p=\tfrac12\operatorname{Re}\sigma\,|\widehat{\mathbf E}|^2\geq0$. These limits describe the material response to a specified local <electric field>, not the complete electromagnetic response of a finite specimen with contacts. The <Drude response> has its pole at $\omega=-i/\tau$, consistent with the decaying <causal response of a first-order relaxation equation>.

The simple <Drude model> does not predict the microscopic <momentum relaxation time>. It neglects <electronic band structure> and <Fermi-Dirac statistics>; a useful semiclassical extension replaces $m$ by an appropriate <band effective mass>, but neither a single scalar <mass> nor one constant <momentum relaxation time> describes every material. Several <charge carriers>, energy-dependent scattering, and transitions between electronic bands can spoil the simple <Drude frequency response>. A successful low-<angular-frequency> fit does not justify extrapolation to arbitrarily large <angular frequencies>.

= Drude conductivity and Hall experiment
{c}
{parent=Drude model}

A motivating laboratory realization is <The Hall Effect and Conductivity in Germanium by Rex Tayloe>. The handout specifies a germanium sample holder with <temperature> and <magnetic field> control, a low-voltage supply, digital meters, and a current-reversing switch. Measure the sample dimensions, map the contacts, and establish the directions of <electric current>, <magnetic field>, and transverse voltage. At fixed <temperature> and zero <magnetic field>, sweep positive and negative <electric currents> and record longitudinal <electric potential differences>. A straight voltage-versus-<electric-current> relation tests <Ohm's law>; sample geometry converts its <slope> to <electrical resistivity>. For the <Hall effect>, minimize the zero-field transverse offset and measure transverse voltage at all four combinations of $\pm I$ and $\pm B$, repeating for several <electric currents>. The readouts test longitudinal proportionality, transverse polarity, and transverse proportionality to <electric current>. These are protocol instructions, not reported measurement outcomes.<The Hall Effect and Conductivity in Germanium by Rex Tayloe>[pp. 1–3, Equipment, Fig. 1, and At room temperature: conductivity sweep and four polarity combinations]{ref}

For interpreting such measurements, an ideal uniform bar of <wire cross-sectional area> $A$ with longitudinal sensing contacts separated by $L$ gives $\sigma=L I/(A V_L)$. <Four-terminal sensing> suppresses voltage drops in current contacts when the voltage meter draws negligible <electric current>; it does not fix uncertain dimensions or a nonuniform <current density>. Reverse <electric current> and use $[V_L(+I)-V_L(-I)]/2$ to reject a constant voltage offset. Keep <temperature> stable, allow the <current density> to settle, and reduce <electric current> to test for <Joule heating>. Those are controls for interpreting the material coefficient, rather than reasons to assume every measured curve is linear.

For the transverse measurement define $\mathbf J=J_x\widehat{\mathbf x}$, $\mathbf B=B_z\widehat{\mathbf z}$, width $w$ along $y$, and thickness $d$ along $z$. Define $V_H=V(y=-w/2)-V(y=+w/2)=E_yw$, so $J_x=I/(wd)$ gives
$$
R_H=\frac{E_y}{J_xB_z}=\frac{dV_H}{IB_z}.
$$
Extract the part odd in both <electric current> and <magnetic field> using
$$
V_H^{\mathrm{odd}}=\frac{V(+I,+B)-V(-I,+B)-V(+I,-B)+V(-I,-B)}{4}.
$$
This combination rejects a constant offset and longitudinal pickup even in <magnetic field>. It cannot reject another signal with the same two reversal symmetries. Check field polarity and magnitude independently; a sign error in the contact map changes the inferred <Hall coefficient>.

For one <Drude model> species in transverse open circuit, the steady <Lorentz force> balance is $0=q(\mathbf E+\mathbf v_d\times\mathbf B)-m\mathbf v_d/\tau$ with $v_{d,y}=0$. Its $y$ component gives $E_y=v_{d,x}B_z$. Consequently
$$
R_H=\frac1{nq},\qquad
R_H=-\frac1{ne}\quad\text{for electrons},\qquad
n=\frac1{e|R_H|},\qquad
\mu=\sigma|R_H|=\frac{e\tau}{m},\qquad
\tau=\frac{m\sigma}{ne^2}.
$$
Longitudinal <electrical conductivity> and the <Hall coefficient> therefore separate <number density> from <charge carrier mobility> in this single-species model. Inferring a <momentum relaxation time> additionally requires an appropriate <mass>. A constant-field longitudinal measurement alone cannot supply all three.

A <Hall coefficient> is not universally the reciprocal of signed <number density>. For example, adding independent <electron> and <electron hole> contributions with densities $n,p$ and positive <charge carrier mobilities> $\mu_e,\mu_h$ in the weak-<magnetic-field> limit gives
$$
\sigma=e(n\mu_e+p\mu_h),\qquad
R_H=\frac{p\mu_h^2-n\mu_e^2}{e(n\mu_e+p\mu_h)^2}.
$$
The <Hall coefficient> can vanish even while <electrical conductivity> is positive, and its sign weights <charge carrier mobility> as well as <number density>. The germanium handout explicitly asks students to track possible transverse sign changes with <temperature>; it does not establish a universal single-carrier interpretation. Real <electronic band structure> and unequal scattering also limit that interpretation.<The Hall Effect and Conductivity in Germanium by Rex Tayloe>[p. 1, Experiment, and p. 3, As a function of temperature: tracking carrier sign and Hall-voltage sign changes]{ref}

= High-frequency Drude plasma cutoff
{parent=Drude model}
{title2=$\omega_p^2=ne^2/(m\epsilon_0)$}

The <Drude model> relative permittivity is $1-\omega_p^2/[\omega(\omega+i/\tau)]$. At frequencies large compared with the collision rate but below $\omega_p$, its leading real part is negative and the physical spatial wave-number branch has positive imaginary part. The field therefore decays into the conductor; close to the threshold the full complex response must be retained.

= Joule heating
{c}
{parent=Ohm's law}
{wiki}
{title2=$P=VI=I^2R=V^2/R$}

= Ohmic heating
{c}
{synonym}

= Electrical dissipation
{synonym}

<Joule heating> is the dissipative conversion of electrical <work> into <internal energy> in an <electrical conductor>. In a metal, the <electric field> supplies <energy> to <charge carriers>, which transfer it to the material. This makes an immersed <resistor> a useful calibrated heater and makes <electrical resistance> a source of losses in wiring. Wind-driven <electric currents> are a proposed source of <heating of an inflated giant planet>.

For a stationary, purely resistive <Ohmic conductor>, use the <passive sign convention>: $V$ is the terminal <electric potential difference> from the entry to the exit of positive <electric current> $I$. The absorbed electrical <power> and <Ohm's law> give
$$
P=VI=I^2R=\frac{V^2}{R}\geq0,\qquad R>0.
$$
The last two expressions assume $V=IR$ at the relevant material <temperature>. For constant <electrical resistance> and <electric current> over a duration $\Delta t$, the dissipated <energy> is $E_{\rm diss}=I^2R\Delta t$; otherwise use $E_{\rm diss}=\int V(t)I(t)\,dt$. In <SI>, $\mathrm{V\,A}=\mathrm W=\mathrm{J/s}$.<University Physics Volume 2 by Samuel J. Ling and others>[§9.5, Power Dissipated by a Resistor and Summary of Relationships: combining terminal power with Ohm's law]{ref}

Absorbed electrical <power> $VI$ is more general than <Joule heating>: a <capacitor> can store <energy>, a <battery> can change its chemical <energy>, and an <electrical circuit> can deliver mechanical <work> or emit <light>. Even a heated <resistor> need not show an indefinitely rising <temperature>: in a thermal <steady state>, its dissipated <power> leaves by <heat transfer>. With the heater inside a <calorimeter> boundary, electrical input crosses as <work>; the subsequent <heat transfer> to the room is a different term in the <first law of thermodynamics>.

= Resistor power under fixed drive
{parent=Joule heating}
{title2=$P_I=I_0^2R,\quad P_V=V_0^2/R$}

Whether increasing <electrical resistance> increases <Joule heating> depends on what the supply holds fixed. Consider separate ideal <resistor> experiments with $R=5\,\Omega$ and $R=10\,\Omega$, each maintained at its specified <temperature>. At fixed <electric current> $I_0=1\,\mathrm A$, doubling $R$ changes the required <electric potential difference> from $5$ to $10\,\mathrm V$ and doubles <power> from $5$ to $10\,\mathrm W$. At fixed <electric potential difference> $V_0=5\,\mathrm V$, doubling $R$ halves <electric current> from $1$ to $0.5\,\mathrm A$ and halves <power> from $5$ to $2.5\,\mathrm W$. These are stipulated model values, not measured data.

\Image[joule-heating-fixed-current-fixed-voltage.png]
{id=joule-heating-fixed-current-fixed-voltage}
{title=Increasing <electrical resistance> has opposite effects on <Joule heating> under fixed <electric current> and fixed <electric potential difference>}
{description=Original calculated comparisons, with $I_0=1\,\mathrm A$ on the left and $V_0=5\,\mathrm V$ on the right. The marked $5$ and $10\,\Omega$ cases are separate ideal experiments. Each lower panel shows the companion quantity that changes when the supply holds the other quantity fixed.}

The comparison also predicts feedback when a particular <resistor>'s <electrical resistance> rises with <temperature>: a supply maintaining <electric current> increases its heating <power>, whereas one maintaining terminal <electric potential difference> reduces it. Predicting the actual <temperature> still needs the material response $R(T)$ and its <heat transfer> to the surroundings; a nominal supply setting does not establish which drive is actually constant at the <resistor>.

= Local Joule heating rate
{parent=Joule heating}
{title2=$p_{\rm vol}=\mathbf J\cdot\mathbf E$}

The <electric field> does <work> on matter at local <power> per <volume>
$$
p_{\rm vol}=\mathbf J\cdot\mathbf E,\qquad
[p_{\rm vol}]=\frac{\mathrm A}{\mathrm{m^2}}\frac{\mathrm V}{\mathrm m}
=\mathrm{W\,m^{-3}}.
$$
Here $\mathbf J$ is <current density> and the <dot product> includes the relative direction. Positive $\mathbf J\cdot\mathbf E$ means <energy> passes from the field to matter; negative means matter supplies <energy> to the field. In general this is a <work> rate, not automatically an irreversible heating rate.<The Feynman Lectures on Physics, Volume II by Richard Feynman and others>[§27–2, equations 27.3–27.5 and the intervening charge-force argument: work on matter per unit volume and its sign in the field-energy balance]{ref}

For a stationary <isotropic> <Ohmic conductor> with real scalar <electrical conductivity> $\sigma>0$, local <Ohm's law> gives $\mathbf J=\sigma\mathbf E$. Thus the <local Joule heating rate> is
$$
p_{\rm vol}=\sigma|\mathbf E|^2
=\frac{|\mathbf J|^2}{\sigma}
=\rho_{\rm el}|\mathbf J|^2\geq0,\qquad
\rho_{\rm el}=\frac1\sigma.
$$
The <electrical resistivity> $\rho_{\rm el}$ has units $\Omega\,\mathrm m$, and <electrical conductivity> has units $(\Omega\,\mathrm m)^{-1}$. These formulas use the material's rest frame and dissipative conduction <current density>. Moving media, <complex conductivity>, and additional storage or transport processes require their appropriate <energy> balance rather than this scalar substitution.

For <uniform current in a straight homogeneous wire> of <length> $L$, <wire cross-sectional area> $A$ and constant <electrical conductivity>, $E=V/L$, $J=I/A$ and $R=L/(\sigma A)$. The <volume integral> recovers terminal <power>:
$$
P=\int_{\text{wire}}\mathbf J\cdot\mathbf E\,d^3x
=\frac IA\frac VL(AL)=IV=I^2R.
$$
As a numerical check, the wire already used in <electrical resistance> has $L=1\,\mathrm m$, $A=10^{-6}\,\mathrm{m^2}$, $\sigma=5\times10^5\,(\Omega\,\mathrm m)^{-1}$ and $V=5\,\mathrm V$. It gives $J=2.5\times10^6\,\mathrm{A/m^2}$, $p_{\rm vol}=1.25\times10^7\,\mathrm{W/m^3}$ and $P=12.5\,\mathrm W$, agreeing with $I=2.5\,\mathrm A$ and $R=2\,\Omega$. Concentrating the same <electric current> in a smaller <wire cross-sectional area> therefore raises the local heating rate if <electrical resistivity> stays fixed.

= Electrical calorimetry of a resistor
{parent=Joule heating}
{title2=$E_{\rm el}=\int VI\,dt$}

An electrical <calorimetry> experiment asks whether a measured electrical <work> input accounts for a measured warming. The University of Iowa's demonstration uses an insulated flask containing <water>, an immersed heater, a <thermometer>, electrical meters and a timer. Its procedure compares the duration, electrical readings and <temperature> rise. The following low-<power> measurement design and synthetic readout develop that idea; they are not measurements reported by the demonstration. <5F15.10 - Electrical Equivalent of Heat by University of Iowa Department of Physics and Astronomy>[Equipment and Procedure: insulated flask, immersed heater, thermometry, electrical meters and timed warming]{ref}

Use a covered <calorimeter> with weighed <water>, an immersed <resistor>, a calibrated <thermometer> and gentle stirring. Put an <ammeter> in series with the heater branch and a high-input-<electrical-resistance> <voltmeter> across the immersed <resistor>'s terminals. Record $V(t)$, $I(t)$, <temperature> $T(t)$ and room <temperature> $T_a(t)$ before, during and after switching the heater on. Measure the <electric potential difference> at the heater, rather than at the supply: lead and contact <electrical resistance> outside the receiving boundary can consume <power> that never reaches the <water>. Account for <voltmeter loading> if the <ammeter> also includes the sensing branch's <electric current>.<University Physics Volume 2 by Samuel J. Ling and others/10.4 Electrical Measuring Instruments>[Measuring Current with an Ammeter and Measuring Voltage with a Voltmeter, Figures 10.35–10.36: series current measurement, parallel voltage measurement and instrument resistance]{ref}

The electrical readout gives operating <electrical resistance> $R(t)=V(t)/I(t)$ when $I(t)\ne0$, and <power> $P(t)=V(t)I(t)$. To test <Ohm's law>, obtain several pairs at the same <temperature>; one ratio alone cannot establish a linear response. For small <electrical resistance>, use <four-terminal sensing> to exclude lead drops and reverse <electric current> to test stable offsets from the <thermoelectric effect>. Use brief low-<power> resistance measurements if the intended result is the unheated value: the test itself causes <Joule heating>.

For a covered assembly at approximately constant <pressure>, with negligible evaporation, negligible endpoint mechanical <energy> changes and no <phase transition>, take
$$
C_{\rm eff}=m_wc_w+C_{\rm cal},\qquad
E_{\rm el}=\int_{t_0}^{t_1}VI\,dt
=C_{\rm eff}(T_1-T_0)+Q_{\rm loss,out}-W_{\rm stir,on}.
$$
Here $c_w$ is the <water>'s <specific heat capacity at constant pressure>, $C_{\rm cal}$ is the participating vessel, heater and sensor <heat capacity>, $Q_{\rm loss,out}$ is signed outward <heat> leakage, and $W_{\rm stir,on}$ is stirring <work> entering the assembly. The storage term approximates its <enthalpy> change; for a rigid fixed-<volume (thermodynamics)> receiver use the corresponding <heat capacity at constant volume>. Calibrate $C_{\rm eff}$ independently if the purpose is to test electrical input against thermal storage. Using the same heating run to define $C_{\rm eff}=E_{\rm el}/\Delta T$ and then claiming it verifies the equality would be circular.

\Image[joule-heating-electrical-calorimetry-readout.png]
{id=joule-heating-electrical-calorimetry-readout}
{title=An immersed <resistor> connects electrical readings to a corrected <calorimeter> warming}
{description=Original apparatus schematic and synthetic <temperature> trace, not Joule's original apparatus or a measured dataset. Separate sensing leads indicate the heater-terminal <electric potential difference>; the <ammeter> is in the heater branch. Shading marks $120\,\mathrm s$ of input at $5\,\mathrm W$. The dashed trace suppresses room <heat> leakage but keeps the same <heat capacity>.}

For the synthetic trace, stipulate $V=5\,\mathrm V$, $I=1\,\mathrm A$, $R=5\,\Omega$, $C_{\rm eff}=500\,\mathrm{J/K}$, room <temperature> $20\,{}^\circ\mathrm C$, and loss coefficient $G=0.5\,\mathrm{W/K}$, with negligible stirring input. The heater runs for $120\,\mathrm s$, beginning at room <temperature>. The <calorimeter heat-loss correction> model is
$$
C_{\rm eff}\frac{dT}{dt}=VI-G(T-T_a),\qquad
\Delta T=\frac{VI}{G}\left(1-e^{-G\Delta t/C_{\rm eff}}\right).
$$
It predicts $E_{\rm el}=600\,\mathrm J$, $\Delta T\approx1.13080\,\mathrm K$, storage $565.398\,\mathrm J$ and outward loss $34.602\,\mathrm J$. Neglecting leakage undercounts deposited <energy> by about $5.77\%$; assuming no loss predicts $1.20\,\mathrm K$. After switching off, the excess <temperature> undergoes <exponential decay> with <time constant> $C_{\rm eff}/G=1000\,\mathrm s$. A cooling slope can estimate $G$ only with known $C_{\rm eff}$ and a credible single-<temperature> model.

Controls separate causes of a <temperature> rise. Check the electrical meters' zeros and <thermometer calibration>; keep <water> <mass>, immersion, lid and stirring fixed between runs. Record a heater-off stirred baseline to estimate sensor drift and stirring input. Repeat cooling runs over the same <temperature> range to estimate environmental <heat transfer>; a baseline taken only at room <temperature> cannot measure warming-dependent leakage. Vary <electric current> while keeping $R$ and initial thermal conditions comparable: corrected deposited <energy> should scale as $I^2$ for equal durations. An independent <calorimeter calibration> or known reference <heat capacity> makes this a quantitative test rather than a qualitative observation.

Finite sensor response, incomplete <thermal equilibrium>, changing $R(T)$ or <heat capacity>, evaporation, and imperfect loss corrections limit the inference. A final <temperature> alone cannot distinguish smaller electrical input from larger <heat> leakage or a larger participating <heat capacity>. Agreement tests the stated assembly and its <energy> accounting; it does not show that all electrical <work> in arbitrary devices becomes <heat>.

Historically, the Royal Society's archive description of <James Prescott Joule>'s manuscript dated 13 October 1840 records a coil of wire in a jar of <water>, a <thermometer> and a <galvanometer>, and summarizes his inference that warming depends on <electrical resistance> and the square of <electric current> intensity. This electrical experiment precedes <Joule's paddle-wheel experiment>; the input here is electrical <work>, whereas the paddle supplies mechanical <work>. The catalogue, rather than the inaccessible manuscript images, supports this account. <Archive catalogue record AP-23-34: On the production of heat by voltaic electricity by The Royal Society Archives>[Royal Society Archives, AP/23/34, Description and Date: apparatus, resistance dependence and duplicate-ratio conclusion; manuscript dated 13 October 1840]{ref}

= Watt
{parent=Joule heating}
{wiki}
{title2=$\mathrm W=\mathrm{J/s}$}

The <watt> is the <SI> unit of <power>, one <joule> per second. <Joule heating> at $12.5\,\mathrm W$ transfers $125\,\mathrm J$ of <energy> over a <time> of $10\,\mathrm s$ if that <power> stays constant. <University Physics Volume 2 by Samuel J. Ling and others/9.5 Electrical Energy and Power>[Ling, Sanny and Moebs, §9.5: power in watts]{ref}.

= Electric field
{parent=Electromagnetism}
{wiki}

The electric field is the <force> per unit charge on a stationary test particle.

= Electric force
{parent=Electric field}
{title2=$\mathbf F_E=q\mathbf E$}

The <electric force> on an <electric charge> $q$ is $\mathbf F_E=q\mathbf E$, using the <electric field> of the other sources and excluding the charge's own self-field. Positive <electric charge> gives <force> along the <electric field>; negative <electric charge> reverses it. A field $\mathbf E=400\,\mathrm{N/C}\,\widehat{\mathbf x}$ therefore gives $\mathbf F_E=-8\times10^{-7}\,\mathrm N\,\widehat{\mathbf x}$ on $q=-2\,\mathrm{nC}$. This is the electric part of the <Lorentz force>; the magnetic contribution is $q\mathbf v\times\mathbf B$. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-2/pages/5-4-electric-field[OpenStax, University Physics II, §5.4: electric field and force]{ref}.

= Electric flux
{parent=Electric field}
{title2=$\Phi_E=\int_S E\cdot n\,dS$}
{wiki}

<Electric flux> measures how much <electric field> crosses a chosen <oriented surface>, with a sign distinguishing the two crossing directions. A strong <electric field> running along a surface contributes nothing to its <electric flux>: only the <normal component> counts. This lets <Gauss's law> turn a potentially complicated <electric field> into one number recording the net enclosed <electric charge>.

For a surface $S$ with chosen <unit normal> $\mathbf n$, the <electric flux> is the <surface integral>
$$
\Phi_E(S)=\int_S\mathbf E\cdot\mathbf n\,dA=\int_S\mathbf E\cdot d\mathbf S,\qquad d\mathbf S=\mathbf n\,dA.
$$
Here $dA$ is positive <surface area>, while $d\mathbf S$ is the <vector area element>. The <dot product> makes <electric flux> a signed <scalar>, rather than a <vector> like the <electric field>. Its <SI> unit is $\mathrm{N\,m^2/C}$: <electric field> measured using the <newton (unit)> per <coulomb>, multiplied by <surface area> in square metres. Reversing the <orientation of a surface> replaces $\mathbf n$ by $-\mathbf n$ and reverses the <electric flux>. Calling it <electric flux> does not imply that <electric charge> is moving through the surface. https://www.feynmanlectures.caltech.edu/II_03.html#Ch3-S2[Feynman lectures II.3, §3–2: flux of a vector field]{ref}.

For an open patch in a <plane>, with <surface area> $A$ and a constant <electric field>, the <unit normal> is constant and the <surface integral> simplifies to
$$
\Phi_E=\mathbf E\cdot(A\mathbf n)=EA\cos\theta,\qquad E=|\mathbf E|.
$$
The <angle> $\theta$ is between the <electric field> and the chosen <unit normal>, not between the <electric field> and the <plane>. The <cosine> describes the signed projected <surface area> seen by the <electric field>. Thus <electric flux> is positive for $0\leq\theta<90^\circ$, zero at $90^\circ$, and negative for $90^\circ<\theta\leq180^\circ$. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-2/pages/6-1-electric-flux[Ling, Sanny and Moebs, University Physics II, §6.1: electric flux]{ref}.

For example, take $E=100\,\mathrm{N/C}$ and $A=0.02\,\mathrm{m^2}$, so $EA=2\,\mathrm{N\,m^2/C}$. Three choices of <angle> give
$$
\begin{aligned}
\Phi_E(0^\circ)&=2\cos0^\circ=2\,\mathrm{N\,m^2/C},\\
\Phi_E(60^\circ)&=2\cos60^\circ=1\,\mathrm{N\,m^2/C},\\
\Phi_E(120^\circ)&=2\cos120^\circ=-1\,\mathrm{N\,m^2/C}.
\end{aligned}
$$
Keeping the $60^\circ$ patch fixed and reversing its <unit normal> gives $120^\circ$ and changes $+1$ to $-1\,\mathrm{N\,m^2/C}$. The <electric field> and physical patch have not changed. If the <electric field> is instead tangent to the patch, its <dot product> with the <unit normal> vanishes: <electric flux> is zero even though $E=100\,\mathrm{N/C}$.

\Image[open-surface-electric-flux.png]
{title=Three open patches in a <plane> in a constant <electric field>: the <angle> to the chosen <unit normal> gives <electric fluxes> of 2, 1 and minus 1 newton square metres per <coulomb>}

For a <closed surface> enclosing a volume $V$, choose the outward <unit normal>. <Gauss's law> gives
$$
\Phi_E(\partial V)=\oint_{\partial V}\mathbf E\cdot\mathbf n\,dA=\frac{Q_{\mathrm{enclosed}}}{\varepsilon_0}.
$$
Here $\varepsilon_0$ is the <vacuum permittivity>, and $Q_{\mathrm{enclosed}}$ is the signed total <electric charge> inside. The <electric field> in the <surface integral> includes all sources, including external <electric charges>. Assume no ideal <point charge> lies on the integration surface. Zero enclosed <electric charge> implies zero total <electric flux>; it does **not** imply zero <electric field> everywhere on the <closed surface> or inside it. Positive and negative contributions to the <surface integral> can cancel. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-2/pages/6-2-explaining-gausss-law[Ling, Sanny and Moebs, University Physics II, §6.2: outward flux and enclosed charge]{ref}.

To see the cancellation with sources inside, place <point charges> $+q$ at $(-a,0,0)$ and $-q$ at $(a,0,0)$, where $q>0$, $a=0.30\,\mathrm m$, and both lie inside the <sphere> of radius $R=1\,\mathrm m$ centered at the origin. This separated pair has the <electric dipole> geometry. Its enclosed <electric charge> is $q-q=0$, so its total outward <electric flux> is zero. At the point $P=(0,0,R)$ on the <sphere>, <Coulomb's law> and the <superposition principle> give the individual <electric fields>
$$
\mathbf E_+(P)=\frac{kq(a,0,R)}{(R^2+a^2)^{3/2}},\qquad
\mathbf E_-(P)=\frac{kq(a,0,-R)}{(R^2+a^2)^{3/2}},\qquad k=\frac{1}{4\pi\varepsilon_0}.
$$
The $z$ components cancel and the positive $x$ components add:
$$
\mathbf E(P)=\frac{2kqa}{(R^2+a^2)^{3/2}}\widehat{\mathbf x}\approx0.527244\frac{kq}{R^2}\widehat{\mathbf x}\ne\mathbf0.
$$
For the stipulated approximation $k=8.99\times10^9\,\mathrm{N\,m^2/C^2}$ and $q=1\,\mathrm{nC}$, this is about $4.740\,\mathrm{N/C}$ along positive $x$. At $P$, the outward <unit normal> is $\widehat{\mathbf z}$, so this nonzero <electric field> is tangent to the <sphere>. At other points of the <sphere>, its <normal component> can be positive or negative. The source-field formula applies away from the two <point charges>, where the <electric field> has zero <divergence>; it is singular at the sources. https://www.feynmanlectures.caltech.edu/II_04.html#Ch4-S2[Feynman lectures II.4, §§4–1 and 4–2: electrostatic sources and superposition]{ref}.

\Image[zero-net-flux-nonzero-field.png]
{title=Cross-section of the three-dimensional <electric field> of two opposite <point charges> inside a unit-radius <sphere>: zero total <electric flux> coexists with a nonzero tangential <electric field> at the top}

The diagram shows <streamlines> of the three-dimensional <electric field> in the section $y=0$. The circle is a cross-section of the <sphere>, not the integration surface: <Gauss's law> uses the entire three-dimensional <surface integral>. A two-dimensional <line integral> of outward field around that circle would be a different quantity, with units $\mathrm{N\,m/C}$ rather than the <electric flux> units $\mathrm{N\,m^2/C}$.

= Electric potential
{parent=Electric field}
{title2=$\Phi$}
{wiki}

= Electrostatic potential
{synonym}

In <electrostatics>, the <electric potential> $\Phi$ tells us the interaction <potential energy> per unit test <electric charge> in the <electric field> of prescribed sources. Its most directly useful quantity is an <electric potential difference>: how much <work> per unit <electric charge> an external agent must supply to move that test <electric charge> slowly between two points. This replaces the bookkeeping of a <vector> <force> along a path with the difference of two <scalar> values. The test <electric charge> must be small enough not to rearrange the sources appreciably.

For motion from $A$ to $B$, write $\Delta\Phi=\Phi(B)-\Phi(A)$. With fixed sources and only the <electric force> doing <work>,
$$
\Delta\Phi=-\int_A^B\mathbf E\cdot d\mathbf l,\qquad
\Delta U=q\Delta\Phi,\qquad
W_{\mathrm{field}}=-q\Delta\Phi,\qquad
\Delta K=-\Delta U.
$$
Here $U$ is interaction <potential energy>, $K$ is <kinetic energy>, and the <line integral> uses the <electric field> produced by the other <electric charges>. For slow transport with unchanged <kinetic energy>, the external <work> is $W_{\mathrm{ext}}=\Delta U$, so $\Delta\Phi=W_{\mathrm{ext}}/q$ for nonzero $q$. This external <work> has the opposite sign to the <work> done by the <electric field>. The <SI> unit is the <volt>,
$$
1\,\mathrm V=1\,\mathrm{J/C}.
$$
A <volt> measures <energy> per unit <electric charge>, not <energy> itself. For example, crossing an <electric potential difference> of $+5\,\mathrm V$ changes the <potential energy> of $q=+2\,\mathrm{nC}$ by $+10\,\mathrm{nJ}$, but changes that of $q=-2\,\mathrm{nC}$ by $-10\,\mathrm{nJ}$. Both test <electric charges> encounter the same <electric potential difference>; their opposite signs reverse the <energy> change.<University Physics Volume 2 by Samuel J. Ling and others/7.2 Electric Potential and Potential Difference>[§7.2, equations 7.4–7.7: energy per charge, work and potential difference]{ref}

The <electrostatic potential> is a <scalar> <function> of position. The <electric field> is a <conservative vector field>, and all three components follow from the negative <gradient> of the <electric potential>:
$$
\mathbf E=-\nabla\Phi,\qquad E_x=-\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial x},\quad E_y=-\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial y},\quad E_z=-\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial z}.
$$
The <electric field> points toward decreasing <electric potential>. A positive test <electric charge> feels <electric force> in that direction; a negative test <electric charge> feels <electric force> in the opposite direction, since $\mathbf F=q\mathbf E$. A freely accelerated negative test <electric charge> therefore moves toward higher <electric potential> while lowering its <potential energy> $U=q\Phi$.<University Physics Volume 2 by Samuel J. Ling and others/7.4 Determining Field from Potential>[§7.4, equations 7.13–7.15: components and negative gradient]{ref}

Only differences fix physical <electrostatic potentials>: adding the same constant $C_0$ everywhere, $\Phi'=\Phi+C_0$, changes neither the <gradient> nor any <electric potential difference>. Choosing an <electrical reference potential> is this elementary <gauge transformation>. Setting zero at infinity is convenient for finitely many localized <point charges>; it is not available for an ideal uniform <electric field> extending to infinity. There we can choose a finite reference point. Assigning zero at ground is a convention, whereas physically grounding an <electrical conductor> also allows exchange of <electric charge> to impose a boundary value. Zero <electric potential> at a point never by itself implies zero <electric field>.<University Physics Volume 2 by Samuel J. Ling and others/7.3 Calculations of Electric Potential>[§7.3, equation 7.8 and Figure 7.18: infinity and ground references]{ref}

For a fixed <point charge> $Q$ at the origin in <vacuum>, choose zero at infinity. <Coulomb's law> gives
$$
\Phi(r)=\frac{kQ}{r},\qquad
\mathbf E(r)=\frac{kQ}{r^2}\widehat{\mathbf r},\qquad
k=\frac{1}{4\pi\varepsilon_0},\qquad r>0,
$$
where $\varepsilon_0$ is the <vacuum permittivity>. Use the stipulated approximation $k=8.99\times10^9\,\mathrm{N\,m^2/C^2}$ and $Q=+2\,\mathrm{nC}$. At $r=0.20\,\mathrm m$,
$$
\Phi=89.9\,\mathrm V,\qquad E_r=449.5\,\mathrm{N/C}.
$$
A test <electric charge> $q=-3\,\mathrm{nC}$ has $U=-2.697\times10^{-7}\,\mathrm J$ and radial <electric force> $F_r=-1.3485\times10^{-6}\,\mathrm N$, directed inward. Moving it inward from $r_A=0.20\,\mathrm m$ to $r_B=0.10\,\mathrm m$ raises the <electric potential> from $89.9$ to $179.8\,\mathrm V$ but lowers its <potential energy>:
$$
\Delta\Phi=+89.9\,\mathrm V,\qquad
\Delta U=-2.697\times10^{-7}\,\mathrm J,\qquad
W_{\mathrm{field}}=+2.697\times10^{-7}\,\mathrm J.
$$
This is the <Coulomb potential energy> calculation using <electric potentials>. The source's singular self-field at $r=0$ is excluded; when finding the <force> on a source <point charge>, use only the <electric field> of the other sources.<University Physics Volume 2 by Samuel J. Ling and others/7.3 Calculations of Electric Potential>[§7.3: point-charge potential and scalar superposition]{ref}

For a uniform <electric field> $\mathbf E=300\,\mathrm{V/m}\,\widehat{\mathbf x}$, choose $\Phi(0)=12\,\mathrm V$. Then
$$
\Phi(x)=12\,\mathrm V-(300\,\mathrm{V/m})x,\qquad
\Delta\Phi=-\mathbf E\cdot(\mathbf r_B-\mathbf r_A).
$$
Moving from $x_A=0$ to $x_B=0.04\,\mathrm m$ gives $\Delta\Phi=-12\,\mathrm V$. For $q=+2\,\mathrm{nC}$ the change in <potential energy> is $-24\,\mathrm{nJ}$ and the <electric field> does $+24\,\mathrm{nJ}$ of <work>. For $q=-2\,\mathrm{nC}$, the same prescribed displacement gives $\Delta U=+24\,\mathrm{nJ}$: the external agent must supply that <work> for slow transport. This uniform <electric field> approximates the interior of a <parallel-plate capacitor> sufficiently far from the edges; the <fringing field of a capacitor> limits the approximation.<University Physics Volume 2 by Samuel J. Ling and others/7.4 Determining Field from Potential>[§7.4: voltage gradient in a uniform field]{ref}

\Image[electric-potential-work-and-equipotentials.png]
{id=electric-potential-work-and-equipotentials}
{title=Analytic <electric potential> models: <point charge> <equipotentials>, a uniform <electric field>, and the sign of the change in <potential energy>}

An <equipotential> is a <level set> $\Phi=\text{constant}$. On a smooth <equipotential> with nonzero <gradient>, every tangent displacement has $d\Phi=\nabla\Phi\cdot d\mathbf l=0$, so the <electric field> is normal to the <equipotential>. Thus moving a test <electric charge> along an <equipotential> requires no net electrical <work>. Around a <point charge>, the <equipotentials> are <spheres>; the circles in the diagram are their sections, not an assumption of two-dimensional <Coulomb's law>. A uniform <electric field> has parallel <plane> <equipotentials>. For contours separated by the same small <electric potential difference>, smaller normal separation means a stronger <electric field>, approximately $|\mathbf E|\approx|\Delta\Phi|/\Delta n$. At a point where $\nabla\Phi=0$, the <electric field> is zero and has no direction to call normal.<University Physics Volume 2 by Samuel J. Ling and others/7.5 Equipotential Surfaces and Conductors>[§7.5, Figures 7.30–7.32: equipotentials, perpendicular field and zero work]{ref}

The <superposition principle> adds <electric potentials> as signed <scalars>. For finitely many stationary <point charges> $q_j$ at $\mathbf r_j$ in <vacuum>,
$$
\Phi(\mathbf r)=k\sum_j\frac{q_j}{|\mathbf r-\mathbf r_j|},\qquad
\mathbf E(\mathbf r)=k\sum_jq_j\frac{\mathbf r-\mathbf r_j}{|\mathbf r-\mathbf r_j|^3}.
$$
For example, the <electric dipole> with $+q$ at $(a,0,0)$ and $-q$ at $(-a,0,0)$ has zero <electric potential> everywhere in the midplane $x=0$, yet its <electric field> at the midpoint is $\mathbf E=-(2kq/a^2)\widehat{\mathbf x}$. With $q=2\,\mathrm{nC}$ and $a=0.10\,\mathrm m$, this is $-3596\,\mathrm{N/C}\,\widehat{\mathbf x}$. At $(2a,0,0)$, the same model gives $\Phi=2kq/(3a)\approx119.867\,\mathrm V$ and $E_x=8kq/(9a^2)\approx1598.222\,\mathrm{N/C}$. Cancellation of <electric potentials> is quite different from cancellation of <vector> <electric fields>.

\Image[electrostatic-dipole-potential.png]
{title=Computed dipole equipotentials and electric-field directions: zero potential on the vertical midline still permits a nonzero field}

A motivating measurement is <conductive-paper electric potential mapping>. A documented teaching experiment uses slightly conductive paper on an insulating board, two circular <electrodes> in highly conducting ink, a steady supply, and a digital <voltmeter>. Connect the supply to the two <electrodes>, keep the <voltmeter> common lead on the negative <electrode>, and use its other lead as a movable probe. Adjust the supply using the measured <electric potential difference> between <electrodes>; the published procedure uses $10\,\mathrm V$. The observable is the <voltmeter> reading $\Phi(P)-\Phi(\mathrm{COM})$ at the probe position $P$. Locate several points reading $2\,\mathrm V$, record their positions on a separate map, and join them into an <equipotential>; repeat at other readings. Lift the probe between contacts so it does not damage the paper. This procedure makes the <electrical reference potential> concrete: every reading compares two contacts.<Physics 2212 laboratory manual by Eric Murray/Physics 2212, Lab 5: Electric Potential and Field>{ref}

From neighboring readings infer an <electric field> component by a <finite difference>, $E_x\approx-[\Phi(x+h)-\Phi(x)]/h$. Reconstruct two in-plane components with displacements along two independent directions. Alternatively, draw the <electric field> normal to mapped <equipotentials>, toward lower <electric potential>. The second diagram illustrates the same measurement principle with bar <electrodes> spanning a uniform sheet, insulating top and bottom edges, and a synthetic model $\Phi(x)=(50\,\mathrm{V/m})x$. The model readings $4.0\,\mathrm V$ at $x=0.08\,\mathrm m$ and $5.0\,\mathrm V$ at $x=0.10\,\mathrm m$ imply $E_x=-50\,\mathrm{V/m}$. These values are calculated illustrations, not reported experimental data. The documented circular-<electrode> experiment similarly estimates the <electric field> from readings at successive centimetre marks.<Physics 2212 laboratory manual by Eric Murray/Physics 2212, Lab 5: Electric Potential and Field>{ref}

\Image[electric-potential-voltmeter-mapping.png]
{id=electric-potential-voltmeter-mapping}
{title=<Voltmeter> setup for <conductive-paper electric potential mapping> and a synthetic profile whose negative <derivative> gives the <electric field>}

Useful controls include checking the <voltmeter> zero, checking that the <electric potential difference> along each painted <electrode> is negligible, and repeating reference readings to detect supply drift. Reversing the supply should reverse the signs relative to the same reference contact. Contact resistance, leakage <electric currents>, and touching the sheet can distort the map. A <voltmeter> has finite input <electrical resistance> and draws a small <electric current>; <voltmeter loading> changes the quantity being measured if that <electrical resistance> is insufficiently large relative to the sheet's effective source <electrical resistance>.<Electricity and Magnetism laboratory manual/EC-2: Electric Fields>{ref}<University Physics Volume 2 by Samuel J. Ling and others/10.4 Electrical Measuring Instruments>[§10.4, Measuring Voltage with a Voltmeter: parallel connection and large input resistance]{ref}

The paper carries a steady <electric current>, so this is a planar analogue rather than a direct measurement of isolated <point charges> in three-dimensional <vacuum>. For constant <electrical conductivity>, <Ohm's law> and steady <charge conservation> give $\mathbf j=-\sigma\nabla\Phi$ and $\nabla\cdot\mathbf j=0$, hence the two-dimensional <Laplace equation> away from <electrodes>. Insulating sheet edges impose zero normal <electric current>, while finite <electrodes> fix boundary values. This derivation explains both the analogy and its limits: inhomogeneous paper, finite boundaries, and finite <electrodes> can change the contours. One should not fit the three-dimensional <point charge> formula $kQ/r$ to a planar map without testing that model. Small probe spacing resolves spatial variation better, but amplifies uncertainty in the inferred <finite difference>. The published laboratory instructions explicitly ask students to assess whether their proposed field fit is appropriate.<Electricity and Magnetism laboratory manual/EC-2: Electric Fields>{ref}<Physics 2212 laboratory manual by Eric Murray/Physics 2212, Lab 5: Electric Potential and Field>{ref}

The scalar description has a crucial limit. <Faraday's law of induction> for a stationary closed path $\Gamma$ says
$$
\oint_\Gamma\mathbf E\cdot d\mathbf l=-\frac{d\Phi_B}{dt},\qquad
\nabla\times\mathbf E=-\frac{\partial\mathbf B}{\partial t},
$$
where $\Phi_B$ is <magnetic flux>. Nonzero changing <magnetic flux> gives nonzero circulation, incompatible with a single-valued <electric potential> alone. More generally, a scalar <electric potential> and a <magnetic vector potential> together give
$$
\mathbf E=-\nabla\Phi-\frac{\partial\mathbf A}{\partial t},\qquad
\mathbf B=\nabla\times\mathbf A.
$$
Consequently, in the presence of induction, two <voltmeter> lead routes between the same contacts can produce different readings if the enclosed <magnetic flux> changes. This follows by subtracting the two path <line integrals>; it is a deduction from <Faraday's law of induction>, not a claimed outcome of the paper experiment. The steady setup and negligible induction justify interpreting its readings as <electric potential differences>. Merely introducing a scalar <electric potential> that depends on <time> cannot supply a nonzero <curl> of the <electric field>.<The Feynman Lectures on Physics, Volume II by Richard Feynman and others/Chapter 21: Solutions of Maxwell’s Equations with Currents and Charges>[§21–3]{ref}

A short mathematical justification connects the statements above. Along a smooth path, the <chain rule> gives $d\Phi=\nabla\Phi\cdot d\mathbf l$. Integrating and using $\mathbf E=-\nabla\Phi$ yields the endpoint formula by the <fundamental theorem for line integrals>, proving <path independence>. Conversely, vanishing circulation around every closed path lets us define $\Phi(P)=-\int_{P_0}^{P}\mathbf E\cdot d\mathbf l$ independently of the route, with $\Phi(P_0)=0$. Zero <curl> is a local test; for a continuously differentiable <curl-free vector field>, a <simply connected> open region suffices for a global potential. The explicit finite-source formula already gives a global <electric potential> away from its source points.<The Feynman Lectures on Physics, Volume II by Richard Feynman and others/Chapter 4: Electrostatics>{ref}

= Voltmeter
{parent=Electric potential}
{wiki}

A <voltmeter> measures an <electric potential difference> between two contacts; its finite input <electrical resistance> can cause <voltmeter loading>.

= Voltmeter loading
{parent=Voltmeter}

<Voltmeter loading> is the change in an <electric potential difference> caused by the <electric current> drawn by the measuring <voltmeter>.

= Conductive-paper electric potential mapping
{parent=Electric potential}

<Conductive-paper electric potential mapping> uses a <voltmeter> to locate <equipotentials> on a resistive sheet, then infers the in-plane <electric field> from the measured <electric potential differences>.

= Electrical reference potential
{parent=Electric potential}
{title2=$\Phi_{\mathrm{ref}}=0$}

An <electrical reference potential> fixes the arbitrary additive constant of an <electric potential>. Assigning zero to ground makes other potentials differences from that baseline; it does not assert that the <electric field> vanishes there. If a source calculation gives $\Phi(a)=89.9\,\mathrm V$ and $\Phi(b)=179.8\,\mathrm V$ relative to infinity, choosing $a$ as the zero instead gives $\Phi'(a)=0$ and $\Phi'(b)=89.9\,\mathrm V$, with the same <electric potential difference>. Grounding an <electrical conductor> additionally connects it to a reservoir that can exchange <electric charge> to maintain the prescribed <electric potential>; that physical boundary condition differs from merely relabeling an unchanged solution.<University Physics Volume 2 by Samuel J. Ling and others/7.3 Calculations of Electric Potential>[§7.3: potential references, including ground in Figure 7.18]{ref}; https://www.feynmanlectures.caltech.edu/II_06.html#Ch6-S8[Feynman lectures II.6, §6–8: a grounded conducting plane]{ref}.

= Constrained electrostatic equilibrium
{parent=Electric potential}

A particle constrained to a surface can have a stable minimum of <electrostatic potential> restricted to that surface even when the unrestricted equilibrium is unstable. Away from source charges, <Laplace's equation> makes the full potential <Hessian matrix> trace zero, which rules out a positive-definite three-dimensional Hessian but does not rule out a positive-definite restriction to two dimensions. Equal positive charges at the corners of a square give a planar minimum at its centre and an unstable perpendicular direction.

= Electrostatic reciprocity identity
{parent=Electric potential}
{title2=$\int_V(\phi_1\rho_2-\phi_2\rho_1)\,dV=\varepsilon_0\int_{\partial V}(\phi_2\nabla\phi_1-\phi_1\nabla\phi_2)\cdot d\mathbf S$}

For two <electrostatic potentials> satisfying the <Poisson equation> $\Delta\phi_i=-\rho_i/\varepsilon_0$, <Green's second identity> yields the displayed reciprocity formula. The <product rule for divergence> applied to $\phi_1\nabla\phi_2-\phi_2\nabla\phi_1$ cancels the cross-gradient terms. The normal points outward from the integration region; for an exterior region this means inward toward the excluded object on its inner boundary. Boundary terms may be removed only under suitable decay or boundary conditions.

= Image charge
{parent=Electric potential}

An image charge is a fictitious point charge placed outside the physical solution region so that its potential, combined with the real sources, enforces the required conductor boundary values. The <uniqueness of the Dirichlet problem> then identifies the constructed potential with the physical one. The image need not describe the actual induced surface-charge distribution.

= Fall time of a charge toward a grounded plane
{parent=Image charge}
{title2=$t_{\rm hit}=\pi\sqrt{2\pi\varepsilon_0 m d^3}/|q|$}

The image of a charge $q$ at height $z$ is $-q$ at height $-z$. Their separation is $2z$, giving force $-q^2/(16\pi\varepsilon_0z^2)$. Starting at rest at height $d$, energy conservation gives $\dot z^2=q^2(1/z-1/d)/(8\pi\varepsilon_0m)$. Integrating the reciprocal speed to the plane yields the stated finite time in the electrostatic Newtonian model. The induced-conductor potential energy is $-q^2/(16\pi\varepsilon_0z)$; using the full image-pair energy would double the force incorrectly.

= Falling charge above a grounded conducting plane
{parent=Image charge}
{title2=$T=\pi\sqrt{2\pi\varepsilon_0md^3}/|q|$}

A real charge of height $z$ has a reflected <image charge> at $-z$. Its acceleration is $\ddot z=-\kappa/z^2$, with $\kappa=q^2/(16\pi\varepsilon_0m)$. Released from rest at height $d$, its ideal-model arrival time is
$$
T=\frac{\pi d^{3/2}}{2\sqrt{2\kappa}}.
$$
The image follows the instantaneous reflected position. The induced-charge energy carries one-half the full real-image pair interaction factor, consistent with this force.

= Image charge for a grounded conducting sphere
{parent=Image charge}
{title2=$q'=-aq/R,\quad\mathbf p'=a^2\mathbf p/R^2$}

For a charge $q$ at $\mathbf p$, $R=|\mathbf p|>a$, outside a grounded sphere of radius $a$, the displayed image gives zero potential on the sphere and decay at infinity. On $|\mathbf x|=a$, one has $|\mathbf x-\mathbf p'|=(a/R)|\mathbf x-\mathbf p|$, proving boundary cancellation. The induced force is inward, of magnitude $q^2aR/[4\pi\varepsilon_0(R^2-a^2)^2]$, from <Coulomb's law> applied to the image field. The self-field is excluded.

= Electric potential difference
{parent=Electric potential}
{title2=$V_{AB}=\phi(A)-\phi(B)$}

An <electric potential difference> compares <electric potentials> at two points. For a move from $a$ to $b$, define $\Delta\Phi=\Phi(b)-\Phi(a)$; the terminal convention $V_{AB}=\Phi(A)-\Phi(B)$ instead means the potential at $A$ relative to $B$, so the order must always be stated. In <electrostatics>, the <line integral> of the <electric field> gives
$$
\Delta\Phi=-\int_a^b\mathbf E\cdot d\mathbf l,\qquad
\Delta U=q\Delta\Phi,\qquad
W_{\mathrm{field}}=q\int_a^b\mathbf E\cdot d\mathbf l=q\bigl(\Phi(a)-\Phi(b)\bigr)=-\Delta U.
$$
Here $\Delta U$ is the change in interaction <potential energy> of the test <electric charge> $q$ with fixed sources, while $W_{\mathrm{field}}$ is <work> done by the <electric field>. For an external agent moving the charge slowly without a change in <kinetic energy>, its <work> is $\Delta U$. A positive <electric charge> gains <kinetic energy> when accelerated toward lower <electric potential>; a negative <electric charge> gains <kinetic energy> when accelerated toward higher <electric potential>. Both motions lower $q\Phi$.<University Physics Volume 2 by Samuel J. Ling and others/7.2 Electric Potential and Potential Difference>[§7.2: potential difference, work, and energy]{ref}

For example, fix $Q=+2\,\mathrm{nC}$ at the origin and compare distances $r_a=0.20\,\mathrm m$ and $r_b=0.10\,\mathrm m$. With the stipulated approximation $k=8.99\times10^9\,\mathrm{N\,m^2/C^2}$ and zero at infinity,
$$
\Phi(a)=\frac{kQ}{r_a}=89.9\,\mathrm V,\qquad
\Phi(b)=\frac{kQ}{r_b}=179.8\,\mathrm V,\qquad
\Delta\Phi=89.9\,\mathrm V.
$$
Moving $q=-3\,\mathrm{nC}$ inward therefore gives
$$
U_a=-2.697\times10^{-7}\,\mathrm J,\qquad
U_b=-5.394\times10^{-7}\,\mathrm J,\qquad
\Delta U=-2.697\times10^{-7}\,\mathrm J,\qquad
W_{\mathrm{field}}=+2.697\times10^{-7}\,\mathrm J.
$$
If only the <electric force> does <work>, <conservation of energy> makes the gain in <kinetic energy> $2.697\times10^{-7}\,\mathrm J$. Direct integration confirms the signs: $\int_{r_a}^{r_b}kQ\,dr/r^2=kQ(1/r_a-1/r_b)=-89.9\,\mathrm V$, which multiplied by the negative test <electric charge> gives positive <work>. The <electric potential difference> is unchanged by choosing a different <electrical reference potential>.

In an <electrical circuit>, an <electric potential difference> is the voltage across a component's terminals. In an <electrical network>, a vertex <voltage> is specified relative to a reference vertex; its differences across edges drive <current flow>. For a uniform <electric field>, the integral reduces to $\Delta\Phi=-\mathbf E\cdot(\mathbf r_b-\mathbf r_a)$. A field $\mathbf E=300\,\mathrm{V/m}\,\widehat{\mathbf x}$ across a displacement $0.04\,\mathrm m\,\widehat{\mathbf x}$ therefore gives $\Delta\Phi=-12\,\mathrm V$, as in the interior approximation for a <parallel-plate capacitor>. The <volt>, measured in <joules> per <coulomb>, expresses <energy> transfer per unit <electric charge>, which makes it useful for comparing components without specifying the amount of charge transported.

= Point charge
{parent=Electric potential}
{wiki}

A point charge idealizes electric charge concentrated at one point. In vacuum, a charge $Q$ at the origin has potential $Q/(4\pi\epsilon_0r)$.

= Equipotential
{parent=Electric potential}
{wiki}

An equipotential is a level set of the electric potential. The electric field is normal to every smooth equipotential.

= Magnetic field
{parent=Electromagnetism}
{title2=$\mathbf B$}
{wiki}

= Magnetic flux density
{synonym}

A <magnetic field> is a <vector field> $\mathbf B(\mathbf r,t)$ whose local value determines the magnetic part of the <Lorentz force> on a body with <electric charge> $q$ and <velocity> $\mathbf v$. In <SI> units,
$$
\mathbf F_B=q\mathbf v\times\mathbf B,\qquad
|\mathbf F_B|=|q|vB\sin\alpha,
$$
where $v$ is the <speed>, $B=|\mathbf B|$, and $\alpha$ is the <angle> between the <velocity> and the <magnetic field>. The <cross product> makes a nonzero magnetic <force> perpendicular to both the <velocity> and the <magnetic field>. The <right-hand rule> gives its direction for positive <electric charge>; negative <electric charge> reverses it. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-2/pages/11-2-magnetic-fields-and-lines[OpenStax, magnetic fields and forces]{ref}.

The <SI> unit of <magnetic field> is the <tesla (unit)>:
$$
1\,\mathrm T=\frac{1\,\mathrm N}{(1\,\mathrm C)(1\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}})}.
$$
For a stipulated example in <SI> units, take <electric charge> $q=2\,\mathrm C$, <velocity> $\mathbf v=(3,0,0)\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$, and <magnetic field> $\mathbf B=(0,0,4)\,\mathrm T$. The <cross product> gives magnetic <force> $\mathbf F_B=(0,-24,0)\,\mathrm N$. With the same <speed> directed along $+z$, the magnetic <force> is zero: motion parallel to a <magnetic field> produces no magnetic <acceleration>.

<Magnetic field lines> visualize the direction of a <magnetic field> by being tangent to it at each point. They are not generally the path of a body with <electric charge>: in a uniform <magnetic field> along $+z$, the <magnetic field lines> are straight while transverse <velocity> produces <circular motion>, and adding parallel <velocity> produces <helical motion in a uniform magnetic field>. The worked <Lorentz force> example below illustrates this distinction.

A <magnetic field> need not be static. <Magnetostatics> is the restricted case with no dependence on <time>; an <electromagnetic wave> has changing <electric field> and <magnetic field> even in vacuum. A changing <magnetic field> is coupled to the <electric field> through <Faraday's law of induction>. https://www.feynmanlectures.caltech.edu/II_20.html[Feynman lectures, II.20: electromagnetic waves in free space]{ref}. The useful separation is between local bending, described by the <Lorentz force>, and the amount of <magnetic field> crossing a <surface>, described by <magnetic flux>.

= Tesla
{disambiguate=unit}
{parent=Magnetic field}
{title2=$\mathrm T$}
{wiki}

The <tesla (unit)> is the <SI> unit of <magnetic field>. From the magnetic <Lorentz force>, $1\,\mathrm T=1\,\mathrm{N\,s\,C^{-1}\,m^{-1}}$. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-2/pages/11-2-magnetic-fields-and-lines[OpenStax, magnetic-field units]{ref}.

= Solenoid
{parent=Magnetic field}
{title2=$B=\mu_0nI$}
{wiki}

A <solenoid> is a coil of <electrical conductor>, usually wire wound into a <helix> around a <circular cylinder>. The same <electric current> $I$ flows through its successive turns. Each turn contributes a <magnetic field>; the <superposition principle> makes their axial contributions reinforce inside the coil. Curl the fingers of your right hand in the direction of the <electric current> around the turns: the thumb gives the interior <magnetic field> direction by the <right-hand rule>. Throughout this branch, $N$ is the total number of turns, $\ell$ is the coil <length>, and $n=N/\ell$ is the <solenoid turn density>. https://www.feynmanlectures.caltech.edu/II_13.html#Ch13-S5[Feynman, Leighton and Sands, The Feynman Lectures on Physics, II.13–5: the field of a long coil]{ref}.

The attraction of a long <solenoid> is a controllable, nearly uniform <magnetic field> over an interior working region: change the <electric current> to tune its strength, or reverse the <electric current> to reverse its direction. This makes it a useful setting for studying the <Lorentz force>, measuring <magnetic flux>, and constructing an <inductor>. Its geometry turns the many contributions from individual turns into the simple <long-solenoid approximation>. A real finite coil has end fields and leakage; its exterior <magnetic field> is not zero. The <on-axis magnetic field of a finite solenoid> below quantifies that difference.

Historically, the coil helped connect <magnetic fields> to <electric currents>. In his memoir on the mathematical theory of electrodynamic phenomena, Ampère modeled the interactions of magnets using the ends of solenoids in place of microscopic magnetic constituents. This was a route toward explaining magnetism by <electric currents>, rather than a modern microscopic account of <magnetization>. https://www.e-rara.ch/zut/download/pdf/1038813[Ampère, Mémoire sur la théorie mathématique des phénomènes électro-dynamiques, concluding discussion on p. 289]{ref}. The historical setting is documented by the https://ampere-archives.fr/histoire-electricite/parcours-historique/lois-courants/ampere-loi[Ampère archives, investigations of current interactions in 1820–1826]{ref}.

= Solenoid turn density
{parent=Solenoid}
{title2=$n=N/\ell$}

The <solenoid turn density> $n$ counts turns per unit axial length in a uniformly wound <solenoid>. The total turn count $N$ is dimensionless, whereas $n$ has units $\mathrm{m^{-1}}$. A $1000$-turn coil of length $0.50\,\mathrm m$ has $n=2000\,\mathrm{m^{-1}}$; an axial interval of length $s=0.010\,\mathrm m$ then contains $ns=20$ turns. The effective circumferential <surface current density> in the closely spaced circular-turn model is $K=nI$, with units $\mathrm{A/m}$. https://web.mit.edu/8.02t/www/test/materials/StudyGuide/guide09_revised.pdf[MIT 8.02 course notes, §9.4, equations 9.4.3–9.4.4: turns per length and surface current]{ref}.

For a single-layer <helix>, the axial advance per turn is approximately $1/n$. The circular-turn model neglects this advance and the associated axial component of <electric current>, as well as the detailed <magnetic field> close to individual wires. The number of turns around the whole coil is always $N=n\ell$ here; it must not be substituted for $n$ in $B=\mu_0nI$.

= Long-solenoid approximation
{parent=Solenoid}
{title2=$\mathbf B_{\rm in}=\mu_0nI\,\widehat{\mathbf z},\quad\mathbf B_{\rm out}=0$}

In <magnetostatics>, an ideal infinitely long <solenoid> is a cylindrical sheet of circumferential <surface current density> $K=nI$. It has negligible winding advance, no magnetic core, and no imposed external <magnetic field>. Choose positive <electric current> to produce <magnetic field> along $+z$. In this ideal model the interior <magnetic field> is uniform and axial, $B=\mu_0nI$, and the exterior <magnetic field> is zero; $\mu_0$ is the <vacuum permeability>. For a finite, closely wound air-core coil, the same result is an approximation well inside the coil, several radii from either end. Air is treated as having the <magnetic permeability> of vacuum. https://www.feynmanlectures.caltech.edu/II_13.html#Ch13-S5[Feynman lectures, II.13–5: long-solenoid field and the return field near the ends]{ref}.

For a short proof, the translational and rotational <symmetries> of the ideal cylindrical <current sheet>, together with the <Biot-Savart law>, give an axial <magnetic field>. <Ampère's law> on rectangles wholly outside makes that axial exterior value independent of radius; the condition of no imposed <magnetic field> makes it zero at infinity and hence everywhere outside. Rectangles wholly inside similarly show that the interior value is independent of radius. Now choose an <Amperian loop> with axial length $s$, one long side inside and the other outside. The transverse sides are perpendicular to the <magnetic field>, so their contributions to the <line integral> vanish. The loop crosses $ns$ turns of the winding, giving
$$
\oint_\Gamma\mathbf B\cdot d\mathbf l
=Bs=\mu_0(ns)I,
\qquad B=\mu_0nI.
$$
Here $s$ is the integration-path length, not the whole coil <length> $\ell$. An <Amperian loop> surrounding both sides of the winding encloses equal and opposite <electric currents>; zero net circulation alone would not prove zero <magnetic field> at every exterior point.

\Image[solenoid-ampere-loop.png]
{title=<Ampère's law> for the ideal <long-solenoid approximation>}
{description=Axial cross-section of an infinite ideal winding: paired dots and crosses show <electric current> out of and into the page. The indicated <right-hand rule> produces interior <magnetic field> along $+z$. The counterclockwise <Amperian loop> crosses only the upper row, with $ns=6$ positive crossings; its interior side runs along $+z$, and its exterior return lies where the ideal <magnetic field> is zero.}

For the $1000$-turn, $0.50\,\mathrm m$ coil with <electric current> $I=0.50\,\mathrm A$, the <long-solenoid approximation> gives
$$
B\approx(1.256637\times10^{-6})(2000)(0.50)\,\mathrm T
=1.256637\times10^{-3}\,\mathrm T
\approx1.257\,\mathrm{mT}.
$$
This illustrative calculation uses <vacuum permeability> $\mu_0\approx1.256637\times10^{-6}\,\mathrm{H/m}$, a rounded value, not an exact constant. Since the 2019 redefinition of <SI>, <vacuum permeability> is determined experimentally.<The International System of Units (SI), ninth edition, SI Brochure>[§2.3.1: the ampere and vacuum permeability]{ref} Doubling $I$ doubles the ideal <magnetic field>; doubling $N$ at fixed $\ell$ does the same. Increasing $\ell$ while keeping $N$ fixed decreases $n$ and hence the ideal <magnetic field>.

= On-axis magnetic field of a finite solenoid
{parent=Solenoid}
{title2=$B_z(z)$}

Model a finite <solenoid> as a continuum of coaxial turns shaped as <circles> of <radius> $a>0$, uniformly distributed over length $\ell$ with <solenoid turn density> $n=N/\ell$. Put its centre at $z=0$, so its ends are $z=\pm\ell/2$. With the same <electric current> orientation as in the <long-solenoid approximation>, its axial <magnetic field> is
$$
B_z(z)=\frac{\mu_0nI}{2}
\left[
\frac{z+\ell/2}{\sqrt{a^2+(z+\ell/2)^2}}
-\frac{z-\ell/2}{\sqrt{a^2+(z-\ell/2)^2}}
\right].
$$
This formula applies only on the axis, where <symmetry> cancels the transverse <magnetic field>. It does not describe points off the axis. It is exact for the continuum circular-turn model, which neglects winding thickness, discrete wire spacing, and helical advance. https://web.mit.edu/8.02t/www/test/materials/StudyGuide/guide09_revised.pdf[MIT 8.02 course notes, §9.4, equations 9.4.5–9.4.6: the finite-solenoid axial field]{ref}.

The derivation is an especially direct use of the <superposition principle>. The <on-axis magnetic field of a circular current loop> at source position $z'$ is $\mu_0Ia^2/[2(a^2+(z-z')^2)^{3/2}]$. There are $n\,dz'$ turns in an axial slice, so
$$
B_z(z)=\frac{\mu_0nIa^2}{2}
\int_{-\ell/2}^{\ell/2}
\frac{dz'}{[a^2+(z-z')^2]^{3/2}}.
$$
Substitute $u=z-z'$ and use the <antiderivative> $u/[a^2\sqrt{a^2+u^2}]$ to obtain the displayed expression. If the centre is instead at $z=z_0$, replace every $z$ in that expression by $z-z_0$.

At the centre and at either end, respectively,
$$
\frac{B_z(0)}{\mu_0nI}=\frac{\ell}{\sqrt{\ell^2+4a^2}},
\qquad
\frac{B_z(\ell/2)}{\mu_0nI}
=\frac{\ell}{2\sqrt{\ell^2+a^2}}.
$$
For aspect ratios $\ell/a=2,6,20$, the centre ratios are approximately $0.7071,0.9487,0.9950$. The end ratio tends to $1/2$ as $\ell/a$ becomes large, even though the centre ratio tends to $1$. Thus a long coil can have an excellent central <magnetic field> while the ends still differ strongly from that value. Convergence to the <long-solenoid approximation> is not uniform across the end region.

\Image[solenoid-axial-field-profile.png]
{title=Finite <solenoid> axial <magnetic fields> compared with the ideal interior value}
{description=Original profiles for $\ell/a=2,6,20$ on the axis, normalized by $\mu_0nI$. The dashed step is the approximation that assigns the ideal interior value within the coil and zero outside; an infinite <solenoid> itself has no ends. The finite profiles stay smooth through the end planes and remain nonzero beyond them. These curves describe no off-axis points.}

For a concrete geometry, give the earlier coil <radius> $a=0.025\,\mathrm m$, so $\ell/a=20$. Its central <magnetic field> is approximately $1.2504\,\mathrm{mT}$, its end value is $0.6275\,\mathrm{mT}$, and at $z=\ell$ its axial <magnetic field> is still $0.00277\,\mathrm{mT}$. The last point is outside the coil: finite length does not permit the zero-exterior conclusion of the infinite model. Off the axis, the <magnetic field lines> bend around to form the return field. A central axial value alone also does not determine the total <magnetic flux> through every turn of a short coil.

= Solenoid inductance
{parent=Solenoid}
{title2=$L\approx\mu_0N^2A/\ell$}

= Self-inductance of a solenoid
{synonym}

For a long vacuum-core or air-core <solenoid> with fixed geometry and linear magnetic response, neglecting end effects gives <self-inductance>
$$
L\approx\frac{\mu_0N^2A}{\ell}
=\mu_0n^2A\ell,
\qquad A=\pi a^2.
$$
Here $N$ is the total number of turns, $n=N/\ell$ is the <solenoid turn density>, and $A$ is the interior cross-sectional <area>. The reason for the square of $N$ is physical: more turns produce more <magnetic field>, and more turns link the resulting <magnetic flux>. Approximately equal <magnetic flux> $\Phi\approx BA$ through each turn gives <magnetic flux linkage> $\Lambda\approx N\Phi\approx\mu_0N^2AI/\ell$; division by the <electric current> yields $L$. https://s3.amazonaws.com/edx-textbooks/mit_802_textbook/Chapter11.pdf[MIT 8.02 course notes, Example 11.2: self-inductance of a solenoid]{ref}.

For the same $N=1000$, $\ell=0.50\,\mathrm m$, and $a=0.025\,\mathrm m$, the <area> is $A=\pi(0.025)^2\,\mathrm{m^2}\approx1.9635\times10^{-3}\,\mathrm{m^2}$. The long-coil estimate is
$$
L\approx\frac{(1.256637\times10^{-6})(1000)^2\pi(0.025)^2}{0.50}\,\mathrm H
\approx4.9348\,\mathrm{mH}.
$$
At $I=0.50\,\mathrm A$, each turn links approximately $2.4674\times10^{-6}\,\mathrm{Wb}$ and the total <magnetic flux linkage> is approximately $2.4674\times10^{-3}\,\mathrm{Wb}$. Their ratio to $I$ is the stated <self-inductance>. A real finite coil need not give precisely these values: the axial centre correction above is not by itself a correction formula for <inductance>, which depends on <magnetic flux> across all the turns.

At fixed $A$ and $\ell$, doubling $N$ quadruples $L$. At fixed <solenoid turn density> $n$ and <area> $A$, doubling $\ell$ doubles both $N$ and $L$. These are different changes to the geometry, so there is no contradiction between $L\propto1/\ell$ at fixed $N$ and $L\propto\ell$ at fixed $n$. A magnetic core requires its own <magnetic permeability> model; nonlinear <magnetization> can make a constant-$L$ description inadequate.

= Stored energy of a solenoid
{parent=Solenoid inductance}
{title2=$U=\tfrac12LI^2$}

The <magnetic energy> stored in a linear <solenoid> of constant <self-inductance> $L$ is
$$
U=\frac12LI^2,
\qquad
U\approx\frac{B^2}{2\mu_0}A\ell
$$
in the <long-solenoid approximation>. The first formula is an <inductor> relation; the second identifies its storage with <magnetic energy> in the interior <magnetic field>, under the vacuum-core approximation. This agreement joins the <electrical circuit> description to the spatial <magnetic field> description. The expression $B^2/(2\mu_0)$ is the vacuum magnetic <energy density>. https://s3.amazonaws.com/edx-textbooks/mit_802_textbook/Chapter11.pdf[MIT 8.02 course notes, §11.3: magnetic energy and its density]{ref}.

A short derivation starts from the terminal <electric potential difference> $v_L=L\,dI/dt$, with positive <electric current> entering the terminal at higher potential. The absorbed <power> is $v_LI$, and integrating from zero <electric current> gives
$$
U=\int_0^I Li\,di=\frac12LI^2.
$$
The result describes recoverable storage in the <inductor>; <Joule heating> in the wire is a separate expenditure of <energy>. https://www.feynmanlectures.caltech.edu/II_17.html#Ch17-S8[Feynman lectures, II.17–8: inductance and magnetic energy]{ref}.

For the long-coil example, $L\approx4.9348\,\mathrm{mH}$ and $I=0.50\,\mathrm A$ give $U\approx6.1685\times10^{-4}\,\mathrm J=0.61685\,\mathrm{mJ}$. Substituting the ideal $B\approx1.256637\,\mathrm{mT}$ and volume $A\ell\approx9.8175\times10^{-4}\,\mathrm{m^3}$ into $B^2A\ell/(2\mu_0)$ gives the same value. Doubling <electric current> quadruples stored <magnetic energy>. In a real coil, reducing <electric current> can return this <energy> to an <electrical circuit>, or release it as <Joule heating> in a closed <RL circuit>.

= Solenoidal magnetic-field constraint
{parent=Magnetic field}
{title2=$\nabla\cdot\mathbf B=0$}

The <magnetic field> has zero divergence. The <ideal magnetohydrodynamic induction equation> preserves this constraint from initial data, since the divergence of its curl is zero.

= Magnetic field line
{parent=Magnetic field}

A magnetic field line is an integral curve tangent to the <magnetic field>. In coordinates $x^i$, it obeys $dx^i/B^i=dx^j/B^j$ wherever the relevant components are nonzero.

= Flux tube
{parent=Magnetic field line}
{wiki}

A flux tube is a bundle of <magnetic field lines> bounded by a surface everywhere tangent to the <magnetic field>. <Gauss's law for magnetism> makes the <magnetic flux> through any transverse cross-section constant along the tube. For a narrow tube with nearly uniform field magnitude $B$ and transverse area $A$, this gives $BA=\mathrm{constant}$.

= Flux-tube area
{parent=Flux tube}
{title2=$A$}

The cross-sectional area of a narrow <flux tube>, measured normal to its <magnetic field>. Constancy of the <magnetic flux> gives $A|\mathbf B|=\mathrm{constant}$ along the tube. This area controls the mass transport when flow follows the field.

= Magnetic-field-line equation
{parent=Magnetic field line}

The magnetic-field-line equation states that the tangent to a field line is parallel to $\mathbf B$. In spherical coordinates for an axisymmetric poloidal field, it can be written $dr/(r\,d\theta)=B_r/B_\theta$.

= Magnetic dipole field
{parent=Magnetic field line}

Outside a localized magnetic dipole $\boldsymbol\mu$, the vacuum field in Gaussian conventions is
$$
\mathbf B(\mathbf r)=\frac{3(\boldsymbol\mu\mathbin\cdot\mathbf r)\mathbf r}{r^5}-\frac{\boldsymbol\mu}{r^3}.
$$
For a dipole along the polar axis, $B_r=2\mu\cos\theta/r^3$ and $B_\theta=\mu\sin\theta/r^3$.

= Dipole magnetic-field line
{parent=Magnetic dipole field}

An axisymmetric dipole field line satisfies $r=C\sin^2\theta$, where $C$ is its equatorial crossing radius. This follows by integrating $dr/(r\,d\theta)=B_r/B_\theta=2\cot\theta$.

= Dipolar flux-tube area
{parent=Dipole magnetic-field line}

Near the polar axis of a <magnetic dipole field>, $B\propto r^{-3}$. Conservation of <magnetic flux> in a <flux tube> consequently gives $A(r)\propto r^3$. A <dipole magnetic-field line> intersecting a sphere of radius $R_\star$ at small polar angle $\theta_\star$ obeys
$$
\theta(r)^2\simeq\theta_\star^2\frac r{R_\star},
\qquad
A(r)\simeq\pi r^2\theta(r)^2
=\pi\theta_\star^2\frac{r^3}{R_\star}.
$$
This area is for one polar tube. The approximation requires $\theta_\star^2r/R_\star\ll1$; the full <magnetic dipole field> does not remain a narrow polar tube at arbitrarily large radius.

= Magnetostatic equilibrium
{parent=Magnetic field}
{wiki}

A magnetostatic equilibrium is a time-independent configuration in which magnetic, pressure, gravitational, and any other body-force densities balance.

= Spherical magnetostatic pressure balance
{parent=Magnetostatic equilibrium}
{title2=$\Delta_*\chi+\psi\psi'=-\mu_0r^2\sin^2\theta\,p'$}

For an axisymmetric <magnetic field> $\mathbf B=\psi\mathbf e_\phi/(r\sin\theta)+\nabla\times[\chi\mathbf e_\phi/(r\sin\theta)]$, the <poloidal magnetic flux function> gives $B_r=\chi_\theta/(r^2\sin\theta)$ and $B_\theta=-\chi_r/(r\sin\theta)$. Force balance implies locally $p=p(\chi)$ and $\psi=\psi(\chi)$ on regular connected flux surfaces. Direct calculation gives $(\nabla\times\mathbf B)\times\mathbf B=-(\Delta_*\chi+\psi\psi')\nabla\chi/(r^2\sin^2\theta)$, with $\Delta_*\chi=\chi_{rr}+\sin\theta\,\partial_\theta(\chi_\theta/\sin\theta)/r^2$. Equating this to $\mu_0\nabla p$ proves the displayed <magnetostatic equilibrium> equation, including its sign and dimensions.

= Confined linear-pressure spherical magnetic equilibrium
{parent=Spherical magnetostatic pressure balance}
{title2=$ (x^2-3)\sin x+3x\cos x=0$}

Set $\psi=\alpha\chi$, $p=p_0+P\chi$ and $\chi=F(r)\sin^2\theta$. A regular family is $F=A[\cos(\alpha r)-\sin(\alpha r)/(\alpha r)]+cr^2$, where $P=-\alpha^2c/\mu_0$. A zero <magnetic field> at radius a requires $F(a)=F'(a)=0$. For nonzero A this yields the displayed condition with $x=\alpha a$ and $c=-A[\cos x-\sin x/x]/a^2$. The boundary pressure is $p_0$, independent of angle. The trigonometric pair is tied by regularity and the radial differential equation; its coefficients are not independent.

= Magnetized isothermal atmosphere
{parent=Magnetostatic equilibrium}

A plane-parallel <magnetostatic equilibrium> with constant downward <gravitational acceleration> $g$, horizontal <magnetic field>, constant <isothermal sound speed> $c_s$, and constant <Alfvén speed> $v_a$ has
$$
H=\frac{c_s^2+v_a^2/2}{g},\qquad \rho=\rho_0e^{-z/H},\qquad p=c_s^2\rho,\qquad B=B_0e^{-z/(2H)}.
$$
Indeed the <magnetohydrodynamic total pressure> is $(c_s^2+v_a^2/2)\rho$, and its vertical derivative is $-g\rho$. The <magnetic pressure> therefore increases the <scale height> above that of an unmagnetized atmosphere.

= Cylindrical magnetostatic equilibrium
{parent=Magnetostatic equilibrium}

A cylindrical magnetostatic equilibrium is an <axisymmetric vector field> and <pressure> configuration independent of the axial coordinate. Its radial <Lorentz force density> balances the <pressure gradient>, as expressed by <cylindrical magnetostatic pressure balance>.

= Z-pinch
{c}
{parent=Cylindrical magnetostatic equilibrium}
{wiki}

In a Z-pinch, an axial <electric current> produces a <toroidal magnetic field> whose inward <magnetic tension> confines a plasma. A cylindrical <magnetostatic equilibrium> balances the resulting <Lorentz force density> against a <pressure gradient>.

= Cylindrical magnetostatic pressure balance
{parent=Cylindrical magnetostatic equilibrium}

For an <axisymmetric vector field> independent of the axial coordinate and regular on the axis, <Gauss's law for magnetism> gives $B_R=0$. The magnetostatic <Ampère-Maxwell equation> then gives $j_R=0$, $j_\phi=-cB_z'/(4\pi)$, and $j_z=c(RB_\phi)'/(4\pi R)$ in Gaussian units. Radial <magnetostatic equilibrium> is
$$
\frac d{dR}\left(p+\frac{B_z^2+B_\phi^2}{8\pi}\right)+\frac{B_\phi^2}{4\pi R}=0.
$$
For enclosed axial <electric current> $I(R)$, the magnetostatic <Ampère-Maxwell equation> gives $B_\phi=2I/(cR)$, hence
$$
\frac d{dR}\left(p+\frac{B_z^2}{8\pi}\right)
+\frac1{2\pi c^2R^2}\frac{dI^2}{dR}=0.
$$
The first term contains <pressure> and axial <magnetic pressure>; the second combines toroidal <magnetic pressure> with the hoop force from <magnetic tension>.

= Flux-tube axial field virial identity
{parent=Cylindrical magnetostatic pressure balance}
{title2=$\langle B_z^2\rangle=B_p^2-2\mu_0\langle p\rangle$}

For a regular cylindrical <flux tube> with $\mathbf B=(0,B_\phi(s),B_z(s))$, vanishing field beyond radius $s_0$, and exterior <pressure> $p_e$, define $\langle h\rangle=2s_0^{-2}\int_0^{s_0}sh(s)\,ds$ and $B_p^2=2\mu_0p_e$. Integrating <cylindrical magnetostatic pressure balance> against $s^2$ gives the displayed identity. The toroidal <magnetic pressure> and hoop <magnetic tension> cancel in this cross-sectional axial-field average. Nonnegative gas <pressure> gives $\langle B_z^2\rangle\leq B_p^2$, with strict inequality precisely when $\langle p\rangle>0$.

= Uniform-twist central axial field bound
{parent=Flux-tube axial field virial identity}
{title2=$B_z(0)^2\leq(1+\gamma^2)B_p^2$}

For a <flux tube> with $B_\phi=\gamma(s/s_0)B_z$, regularity at the axis and <cylindrical magnetostatic pressure balance> give $B_z(0)^2=B_p^2-2\mu_0p(0)+\gamma^2\langle B_z^2\rangle$. The <flux-tube axial field virial identity> and $p\geq0$ give the bound. A twisted <toroidal magnetic field> can support a central axial field above the untwisted <magnetic pressure> limit even though its cross-sectional mean obeys that limit.

= Power-law pressure-supported axial current
{parent=Cylindrical magnetostatic pressure balance}

For constant axial <magnetic field> and $p=p_0(1+R^2/a^2)^{-k}$ with $p_0>0$ and $k>0$, <cylindrical magnetostatic pressure balance> gives, with $x=R^2/a^2$,
$$
I^2(R)=2\pi c^2p_0a^2
\begin{cases}
\displaystyle\frac{1-(1+kx)(1+x)^{-k}}{k-1},&k\ne1,\\
\log(1+x)+(1+x)^{-1}-1,&k=1.
\end{cases}
$$
The nontrivial <electric current> is finite at infinity exactly when $k>1$, in which case $I^2(\infty)=2\pi c^2p_0a^2/(k-1)$. Either sign of $I$ is possible because the <pressure> balance fixes its square.

= Magnetic pressure support with vanishing axial field
{parent=Cylindrical magnetostatic pressure balance}

With no axial <electric current>, <cylindrical magnetostatic pressure balance> reduces to constant $p+B_z^2/(8\pi)$. For $p=p_0e^{-R^2/a^2}$ and $B_z(0)=0$, its radial solutions are
$$
B_z=\sigma\sqrt{8\pi p_0}\sqrt{1-e^{-R^2/a^2}},
\qquad
j_\phi=-\sigma\frac{c\sqrt{8\pi p_0}}{4\pi a^2}
\frac{R e^{-R^2/a^2}}{\sqrt{1-e^{-R^2/a^2}}},
\qquad \sigma\in\{-1,1\}.
$$
As $R\downarrow0$, $B_z\sim\sigma\sqrt{8\pi p_0}R/a$ and $j_\phi\to-\sigma c\sqrt{8\pi p_0}/(4\pi a)$. These finite scalar limits do not give a <smooth function> of Cartesian position: $R=\sqrt{x^2+y^2}$ has a cusp at the axis, and the azimuthal unit vector has no unique limit there. Thus these data describe radial equilibrium away from the axis, but cannot satisfy smooth vector-field regularity on the axis without modifying the data.

= Motional electric field
{parent=Magnetic field}

A conductor or plasma moving with nonrelativistic velocity $\mathbf u$ through a magnetic field experiences the motional electric field $\mathbf E=-\mathbf u\times\mathbf B/c$ in Gaussian units when the electric field vanishes in its rest frame.

= London equations
{c}
{parent=Magnetic field}
{wiki}

= London equation
{c}
{synonym}

The London equations relate the superconducting <electric current> to the <electromagnetic field>: $\partial_t\mathbf J=\mathbf E/(\mu_0\lambda_L^2)$ and $\nabla\times\mathbf J=-\mathbf B/(\mu_0\lambda_L^2)$. In a gauge with constant condensate phase the second follows from $\mathbf J=-\mathbf A/(\mu_0\lambda_L^2)$; it implies exponential <magnetic field> screening.

= London penetration depth
{c}
{parent=London equations}
{title2=$\lambda_L=\sqrt{m/(\mu_0n_sq^2)}$}
{wiki}

For uniform vortex-free <superconductivity>, the <London equation> and stationary Maxwell equations give $\nabla^2\mathbf B=\mathbf B/\lambda_L^2$. A field tangential to a planar superconducting half-space decays as $\mathbf B(z)=\mathbf B(0)e^{-z/\lambda_L}$. A nonzero normal boundary field is incompatible with this translation-invariant decaying Meissner solution, because divergence-free magnetic flux would require that component to remain constant.

= Meissner effect
{c}
{parent=London equations}
{wiki}

= Meissner-Ochsenfeld effect
{c}
{synonym}

The <Meissner effect> is the equilibrium expulsion of a sufficiently weak, static <magnetic field> from the bulk of a material exhibiting <superconductivity>. Expulsion includes a <magnetic field> that was already present before cooling: zero <electrical resistance> alone does not predict this. The <field-cooled Meissner experiment> tests that distinction.
