A finite <London penetration depth> means that the <magnetic field> and screening <electric current> occupy a surface layer, rather than disappearing at a mathematical boundary.<Lecture notes on the electrodynamics of metals and superconductors>[§2, pp. 12–13, Eqs. (64)–(74): acceleration, additional magnetic constraint and penetration length]{ref}

This makes <superconductivity> useful for shielding weak <magnetic fields>, and makes its magnetic response an independent test of the phase. The striking result is that the same screened equilibrium can be reached by two different preparation histories. The assumptions matter: <finite-thickness Meissner screening> can leave an appreciable <magnetic field> throughout a small sample, and the <limits of Meissner screening> distinguish <quantum vortex> penetration and retained <magnetic flux> from this equilibrium state.

= Meissner state
{c}
{parent=Meissner effect}

A <Meissner state> is an equilibrium state of <superconductivity> without <quantum vortex> penetration, with screening <electric currents> responding to the applied <magnetic field>. In a solid, <simply connected> sample much thicker than the <London penetration depth>, the deep interior <magnetic field> is approximately zero. This is a statement about a phase and a geometry, not a rule that every sample exhibiting <superconductivity> always has $\mathbf B=0$. In particular, the <Meissner state> exists for sufficiently weak <magnetic fields> in both <type-I superconductors> and <type-II superconductors>. <University Physics Volume 3 by Samuel J. Ling and others/9.8 Superconductivity>[§9.8, Properties of Superconductors, paragraphs distinguishing type I, type II and their two critical fields]{ref}

= Planar London screening
{c}
{parent=Meissner state}
{title2=$B(z)=B_0e^{-z/\lambda_L}$}

Take a homogeneous material exhibiting <superconductivity> in the <half-space> $z\geq0$, bounded by vacuum at $z=0$. Fix the <temperature> below the <superconducting transition temperature>, take a constant positive <London penetration depth> $\lambda_L$, and assume a local, linear <London equation> description in a <Meissner state>. The applied <magnetic field> is weak enough that it neither nucleates a <quantum vortex> nor appreciably suppresses the material's <superconductivity>. Assume translation invariance in $x,y$, no imposed transport <electric current>, and a tangential boundary <magnetic field> $\mathbf B(0)=B_0\widehat{\mathbf x}$. Here $B_0$ is the local surface <magnetic flux density>, equal to the imposed vacuum <magnetic field> in this infinite planar geometry; it is not automatically the far-away applied value for a finite object.

For a bounded, decaying interior solution, the <London equations>, <magnetostatic Ampère law> and <Gauss's law for magnetism> give
$$
\nabla^2\mathbf B=\frac{\mathbf B}{\lambda_L^2},\qquad
\frac{d^2B_x}{dz^2}=\frac{B_x}{\lambda_L^2},\qquad
B_x(0)=B_0,\quad B_x(\infty)=0.
$$
The solution is
$$
\mathbf B(z)=B_0e^{-z/\lambda_L}\widehat{\mathbf x}.
$$
At one <London penetration depth> the <magnetic field> is $e^{-1}\simeq0.368$ of its surface value; at five it is $e^{-5}\simeq0.00674$. There is no finite depth where this exponential is exactly zero. The <differential equation> excludes the growing exponential through the deep-interior boundary condition. A constant normal component cannot decay in this geometry: <Gauss's law for magnetism> would require $\partial_zB_z=0$. A perpendicular applied <magnetic field> on a finite plate therefore requires a different spatial problem.<Lecture notes on the electrodynamics of metals and superconductors>[§2, p. 13, Eqs. (68)–(74): London screening equation; the planar boundary-value solution here follows directly]{ref}

\Image[meissner-field-and-screening-current-profiles.png]
{title=<Magnetic field> and signed <Meissner screening current> profiles with finite <London penetration depth>}
{description=Original analytic profiles, not measured data. Left: the <planar London screening> solution, with vacuum at $z<0$ and the material at $z\geq0$. Right: <finite-thickness Meissner screening> in a slab of thickness $2\lambda_L$, with equal tangential surface <magnetic fields>. Blue is $B_x/B_0$; orange is the signed <current density> $\mu_0\lambda_LJ_y/B_0$. Different horizontal origins are labeled explicitly.}

= Meissner screening current
{c}
{parent=Planar London screening}
{title2=$J_y(z)=-B_0e^{-z/\lambda_L}/(\mu_0\lambda_L)$}

In <SI> units, the <magnetostatic Ampère law> gives the <current density> supporting <planar London screening>:
$$
\mathbf J=\frac{1}{\mu_0}\nabla\times\mathbf B
=-\frac{B_0}{\mu_0\lambda_L}e^{-z/\lambda_L}\widehat{\mathbf y}.
$$
Here $\mu_0$ is the <vacuum permeability>. The sign follows from $(\nabla\times\mathbf B)_y=\partial_zB_x$ in the chosen coordinates. Thus $B_0>0$ requires <electric current> along $-\widehat{\mathbf y}$. Its own <magnetic field> opposes the imposed <magnetic field> inside the material. The <electric current> is distributed over a finite layer; its depth <integral> is the effective <surface current density>
$$
\mathbf K=\int_0^\infty\mathbf J(z)\,dz
=-\frac{B_0}{\mu_0}\widehat{\mathbf y}.
$$
The units are $\mathrm{A\,m^{-2}}$ for $\mathbf J$ and $\mathrm{A\,m^{-1}}$ for $\mathbf K$. Shrinking $\lambda_L$ at fixed $B_0$ increases the surface <current density>, while this integrated <surface current density> stays fixed. The <current sheet> approximation is the large-length-scale limit of this layer, not a claim that an actual sample carries an infinitely thin <electric current>. It reproduces the <magnetic-field jump across a surface current> between the exterior $B_0\widehat{\mathbf x}$ and a field-free interior, with outward <unit normal> $-\widehat{\mathbf z}$.

= Finite-thickness Meissner screening
{c}
{parent=Meissner state}
{title2=$B_x(z)=B_0\cosh(z/\lambda_L)/\cosh(d/(2\lambda_L))$}

To isolate finite size from <quantum vortex> penetration, keep the same weak-field <London equations> but replace the <half-space> by an infinite slab $-d/2\leq z\leq d/2$. Put equal tangential <magnetic fields> $B_0\widehat{\mathbf x}$ at its two faces. With $z=0$ now at the slab center, the even solution and its signed <current density> are
$$
B_x(z)=B_0\frac{\cosh(z/\lambda_L)}{\cosh(d/(2\lambda_L))},\qquad
J_y(z)=\frac{B_0}{\mu_0\lambda_L}\frac{\sinh(z/\lambda_L)}{\cosh(d/(2\lambda_L))}.
$$
The <hyperbolic cosine> profile has equal boundary values, while the <electric currents> on the two sides have opposite signs. In particular,
$$
\frac{B_x(0)}{B_0}=\frac{1}{\cosh(d/(2\lambda_L))},\qquad
\frac{\overline B_x}{B_0}=\frac{1}{dB_0}\int_{-d/2}^{d/2}B_x(z)\,dz
=\frac{2\lambda_L}{d}\tanh\!\left(\frac{d}{2\lambda_L}\right).
$$
For the stipulated thickness $d=2\lambda_L$, the center retains about $0.648B_0$ and the mean is about $0.762B_0$, even though this is a <Meissner state>. For $d\gg\lambda_L$, the center <magnetic field> is exponentially small and the mean is about $2\lambda_LB_0/d$, reflecting the two surface layers. For $d\ll\lambda_L$, the mean tends to $B_0$: little screening is possible across such a short distance. These are analytic model examples, not material measurements. Real finite objects also need their <demagnetizing field> and edge geometry accounted for; the slab calculation only isolates the effect of thickness.

= Meissner effect and flux history
{c}
{parent=Meissner effect}
{title2=$\partial_t\mathbf B=0\text{ when }\mathbf E=0$}

Compare two ideal models for a stationary, solid, <simply connected> sample. In the <perfect conductor> flux-history model the interior <electric field> is constrained to zero from the moment perfect conduction starts. <Faraday's law of induction> then gives
$$
\partial_t\mathbf B=-\nabla\times\mathbf E=0.
$$
Consequently the interior <magnetic field> retains its value at that moment. Cooling in an already applied <magnetic field> freezes a nonzero interior value; starting perfect conduction in zero <magnetic field> and then applying a <magnetic field> preserves zero. The zero initial interior <magnetic field> often assumed in <perfect conductor> wave-reflection problems is one particular preparation, not a general prediction of expulsion. <University Physics Volume 3 by Samuel J. Ling and others/9.8 Superconductivity>[§9.8, Eq. (9.38) and the argument that zero resistivity conserves existing flux rather than expelling it]{ref}

\Image[meissner-cooling-and-field-history.png]
{title=Two preparation histories distinguish the <Meissner state> from <perfect conductor> flux memory}
{description=Original schematic end states for a solid sample much thicker than its <London penetration depth>, not measured data or transition kinetics. Both rows end at the same low <temperature> and applied weak <magnetic field>. Blue arrows indicate interior <magnetic field>; orange edges represent screening <electric currents>. The near-zero interior in the <Meissner state> omits its finite penetration layer. The <perfect conductor> panel uses the ideal zero-interior-<electric field> constraint.}

The <Meissner state> instead selects the screened equilibrium after either preparation, provided that equilibrium is accessible and the applied <magnetic field> lies in the <Meissner state> regime. The first <London equation> alone illustrates the missing information: at fixed $\lambda_L$, combining it with <Faraday's law of induction> gives
$$
\partial_t\!\left(\nabla\times\mathbf J+\frac{\mathbf B}{\mu_0\lambda_L^2}\right)=0.
$$
This permits an arbitrary time-independent integration field. The second <London equation> imposes the additional zero value of that bracket in the vortex-free local model.<Lecture notes on the electrodynamics of metals and superconductors>[§2, pp. 12–13, Eqs. (64), (66)–(68): time-independent freedom and the second London equation]{ref} Establishing <superconductivity> during cooling is not the fixed-parameter, zero-<electric field> model: transient <electric fields> accompany changing <magnetic flux>, and $\lambda_L$ itself depends on <temperature>. The equilibrium <London equations> predict final spatial screening, not the cooling rate or the time-resolved expulsion pulse.

= Meissner effect discovery
{c}
{parent=Meissner effect}

The 1933 report https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/BF01504252[Ein neuer Effekt bei Eintritt der Supraleitfähigkeit by W. Meissner and R. Ochsenfeld] describes cooling tin and <lead> cylinders in a fixed transverse <magnetic field>. Two parallel cylinders were about $140\,\mathrm{mm}$ long and $3\,\mathrm{mm}$ thick, separated by $1.5\,\mathrm{mm}$. A roughly $10\,\mathrm{mm}$ rotating search coil between them drove a ballistic <galvanometer>. At about $5\,\mathrm{G}$, the reported below/above-transition induction ratios were $1.70$ for tin single crystals and $1.77$ for polycrystalline <lead>. The increased gap <magnetic field> indicates redistribution outside the cylinders, rather than an unchanged frozen field. Coil-winding geometry and noncircular tin specimens limited comparison with the ideal prediction. <Ein neuer Effekt bei Eintritt der Supraleitfähigkeit by Walther Meissner and others>[1933 report, first arrangement; English translation PDF pp. 1–2, printed p. 119, alongside the German text]{ref}

Their second arrangement used a <lead> tube, about $130\,\mathrm{mm}$ long with $3\,\mathrm{mm}$ outer and $2\,\mathrm{mm}$ inner diameters. A rotating search coil could sit inside or alongside it. The bore retained <magnetic flux> after removing the applied <magnetic field>; the coil nearly filled the bore, preventing a test of interior field uniformity. A hole containing <magnetic field> is distinct from <magnetic field> penetrating the surrounding material exhibiting <superconductivity>. <Ein neuer Effekt bei Eintritt der Supraleitfähigkeit by Walther Meissner and others>[1933 report, second arrangement; translation PDF p. 2, printed p. 119, bore retention and explicitly stated spatial-resolution limitation]{ref}

The modern <field-cooled Meissner experiment> provides a complementary readout. MIT's probe I places a sample in a brass holder, with an $810$-turn test coil of $7.1\,\mathrm{mm}$ inner diameter, $10.5\,\mathrm{mm}$ outer diameter and $12.3\,\mathrm{mm}$ length, inside a longer <solenoid>. A nearby silicon-diode <thermometer> tracks cooling by helium gas. With <lead> still normal, the guide applies about $200\,\mathrm{mA}$ to the <solenoid> and connects the test coil to a current-integrator box and <oscilloscope>. Cooling produces a transient; warming gives the opposite sign. Switching off the <solenoid> while the sample is normal checks the sign. <Superconductivity: The Meissner Effect, Persistent Currents and the Josephson Effects by MIT Department of Physics>[Probe I, §4.2 and Fig. 6, printed pp. 5–6; §4.3 temperature control; §5.2.2, printed p. 10, DC field cooling and sign control]{ref}

The inferred change is in linked <magnetic flux>, not a direct scan of $B(z)$. For a stationary test coil the pulse obeys $\int V\,dt=-\Delta\Psi$, where $\Psi$ is its <magnetic flux linkage>. An integrator or AC-coupled <oscilloscope> needs its transfer response accounted for before extracting this pulse area. Keep the source <electric current> fixed during cooling, monitor <temperature>, and compare warming and cooling slowly enough to reduce sample–<thermometer> lag. An AC <inductance> change alone can reflect changed normal-state <electrical conductivity>; the fixed-field expulsion test distinguishes that alternative. <Superconductivity: The Meissner Effect, Persistent Currents and the Josephson Effects by MIT Department of Physics>[§5.1, printed pp. 8–9, thermometer lag; §5.2.2, printed p. 10, limitation of AC mutual-inductance evidence]{ref} Neither this coil readout nor the historical gap-field measurement resolves the microscopic <London penetration depth> by itself.

= Limits of Meissner screening
{c}
{parent=Meissner effect}

For a bulk <type-II superconductor> with negligible <demagnetizing field>, raising the applied <magnetic field intensity> past the lower critical value $H_{c1}$ favors a <superconducting mixed state> containing <quantum vortex> lines; <superconductivity> survives until the upper critical value $H_{c2}$. The <magnetic field> extends around each vortex over a penetration scale, not solely inside its core. Thus a nonzero average <magnetic flux density> can coexist with <superconductivity>. Below $H_{c1}$, a <type-II superconductor> also supports a <Meissner state>; a vortex-free state above the equilibrium entry field may instead be <metastable>. <University Physics Volume 3 by Samuel J. Ling and others/9.8 Superconductivity>[§9.8, type-II critical-field and mixed-state paragraphs; the source uses B_c1 and B_c2 for applied-field scales]{ref}

Real preparation histories may leave <quantum vortex> lines held in place by material inhomogeneities, retaining <magnetic flux> and producing <hysteresis>. This <flux pinning> restricts access to equilibrium: it is different from the arbitrary frozen interior <magnetic field> of the ideal <perfect conductor> model. Harvard's vortex-imaging work illustrates strong pinning to bulk disorder in a <type-II superconductor>. <Scanning Tunneling Microscopy Research by Hoffman Laboratory>[Vortex Imaging in Iron Pnictide Superconductor, Fig. 2 and the discussion of disordered vortices and bulk pinning]{ref} Separately, <flux conservation in a superconducting loop> and <fluxoid and flux quantization in a superconductor> explain why a hole can retain <magnetic flux>. Neither bore retention, vortex penetration nor weak screening in a thin sample should be silently identified with the equilibrium <planar London screening> calculation.

= Magnetic flux
{parent=Magnetic field}
{title2=$\Phi_B$}
{wiki}

<Magnetic flux> measures the signed amount of <magnetic field> crossing an <oriented surface> $S$. Choose a <unit normal> $\widehat{\mathbf n}$ and use the <oriented surface element> $d\mathbf S=\widehat{\mathbf n}\,dA$. The defining <surface integral> is
$$
\Phi_B=\int_S\mathbf B\cdot d\mathbf S
=\int_S\mathbf B\cdot\widehat{\mathbf n}\,dA.
$$
For a uniform <magnetic field> across a flat <surface> of <area> $A$, the <dot product> reduces this to
$$
\Phi_B=BA\cos\theta,
$$
where $\theta$ is the <angle> between the <magnetic field> and the chosen <unit normal>, rather than the <plane> of the <surface>. The <SI> unit is the <weber (unit)>, $1\,\mathrm{Wb}=1\,\mathrm{T\,m^2}$. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-2/pages/13-1-faradays-law[OpenStax, magnetic flux and its orientation]{ref}.

For a stipulated <magnetic field> $B=2\,\mathrm T$ and <area> $A=3\,\mathrm{m^2}$, the <magnetic flux> is $6\,\mathrm{Wb}$ at $\theta=0$, $3\,\mathrm{Wb}$ at $\theta=\pi/3$, and zero at $\theta=\pi/2$. In the last case the <magnetic field> lies in the <plane> of the <surface>, so it does not cross it. Reversing the chosen <unit normal> reverses the sign of <magnetic flux> without changing the physical <magnetic field>; at $\theta=\pi$ the same example gives $-6\,\mathrm{Wb}$.

\Image[magnetic-flux-orientation.png]
{title=<Magnetic flux> as a flat <surface> rotates in a fixed <magnetic field>}
{description=Left: an edge-on <surface> and its <unit normal> at three <angles> to the fixed <magnetic field>. Right: the signed <magnetic flux> $\Phi_B=6\cos\theta\,\mathrm{Wb}$, including reversal of the <unit normal>. The stipulated <magnetic field> is $2\,\mathrm T$ and the <area> is $3\,\mathrm{m^2}$.}

The geometric idea is projected <area>: tilting a flat <surface> reduces the <area> seen perpendicular to the <magnetic field> by the factor $\cos\theta$. This explains how rotation alone can change <magnetic flux> even when the <magnetic field> is constant; <Faraday's law of induction> turns such changes into an <electromotive force>. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-2/pages/13-1-faradays-law[OpenStax, induction from changing magnetic flux]{ref}.

= Weber
{disambiguate=unit}
{parent=Magnetic flux}
{title2=$\mathrm{Wb}$}
{wiki}

The <weber (unit)> is the <SI> unit of <magnetic flux>, with $1\,\mathrm{Wb}=1\,\mathrm{T\,m^2}$. A uniform <magnetic field> of $1\,\mathrm T$ crossing a flat <surface> of <area> $1\,\mathrm{m^2}$ along its chosen <unit normal> gives $1\,\mathrm{Wb}$. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-2/pages/13-1-faradays-law[OpenStax, magnetic-flux units]{ref}.

= Capacitance
{parent=Electromagnetism}
{title2=$C$}
{wiki}

The <capacitance> of a linear two-terminal <capacitor> is the constant relating its separated <electric charge> to its terminal <electric potential difference>:
$$
Q=CV,\qquad C=\frac QV\quad(V\ne0).
$$
Here $Q$ is the <electric charge> on one <electrical conductor>, the other carries $-Q$, and $V$ is their <electric potential difference> with the same ordering. Reversing the terminal ordering reverses both $Q$ and $V$ and leaves <capacitance> unchanged. An ideal passive <capacitor> has $C\ge0$; an ordinary pair of separated <electrical conductors> with positive <permittivity> has $C>0$. At $V=0$, use $Q=CV$ rather than the undefined ratio $0/0$.

For example, <electric charge> magnitude $Q=0.8854\,\mathrm{nC}$ at terminal <electric potential difference> $V=10\,\mathrm V$ gives <capacitance> $C=88.54\,\mathrm{pF}$. At $20\,\mathrm V$, the same linear <capacitor> has $Q=1.7708\,\mathrm{nC}$ and the same <capacitance>. The plots in <capacitor energy> show that <capacitance> is the <slope> of the <electric charge> versus terminal <electric potential difference> graph.

For fixed geometry and linear material response, <capacitance> depends on dimensions and <permittivity>, rather than the source's terminal <electric potential difference>. This is why <capacitance> is a useful property of a component: once it is known, $Q=CV$ predicts the separated <electric charge> for each operating terminal <electric potential difference>. The <SI> unit is the <farad>, $1\,\mathrm F=1\,\mathrm{C/V}$. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-2/pages/8-1-capacitors-and-capacitance[OpenStax University Physics 2, §8.1: capacitance and its unit]{ref}.

For combinations of <capacitors>, the same terminal definition gives an equivalent <capacitance>. Two identical <capacitors> of <capacitance> $C$ connected in parallel share the same terminal <electric potential difference>, and their <electric charges> add, giving $C_{\rm eq}=2C$. Connected in series with an initially neutral isolated junction, they carry equal plate <electric charge> magnitudes and their terminal <electric potential differences> add, giving $C_{\rm eq}=C/2$. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-2/pages/8-2-capacitors-in-series-and-in-parallel[OpenStax University Physics 2, §8.2: capacitors in series and in parallel]{ref}.

= Farad
{parent=Capacitance}
{title2=$\mathrm F$}
{wiki}

The <farad>, symbol $\mathrm F$, is the <SI> unit of <capacitance>:
$$
1\,\mathrm F=1\,\frac{\mathrm C}{\mathrm V}.
$$
A <capacitor> of <capacitance> $1\,\mathrm F$ carries separated <electric charge> magnitude $1\,\mathrm C$ at terminal <electric potential difference> $1\,\mathrm V$. A picofarad is $10^{-12}\,\mathrm F$, so the worked <parallel-plate capacitor>'s <capacitance> $88.54\,\mathrm{pF}$ is $8.854\times10^{-11}\,\mathrm F$. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-2/pages/8-1-capacitors-and-capacitance[OpenStax University Physics 2, §8.1: the farad]{ref}.

= Capacitance of an isolated conducting sphere
{parent=Capacitance}
{title2=$C=4\pi\epsilon R$}

An isolated conducting sphere of radius $R$ in a medium of <permittivity> $\epsilon$ has surface <electrostatic potential> $V=Q/(4\pi\epsilon R)$ relative to infinity. Thus its <capacitance> is $C=Q/V=4\pi\epsilon R$.

= Sphere-plane capacitance
{parent=Capacitance}
{title2=$C(R,h)$}

For a conducting sphere of radius $R$ at center height $h>R$ above a grounded infinite plane,
$$
C(R,h)=4\pi\epsilon R\sinh\alpha\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac1{\sinh(n\alpha)},
\qquad\cosh\alpha=\frac hR.
$$
The distance $h$ is measured to the center, so the surface gap is $h-R$. Relative to the <capacitance of an isolated conducting sphere>, the enhancement is $c(R/h)=C(R,h)/(4\pi\epsilon R)$.

The <method of images> gives a constructive proof. At fixed sphere potential $V_s$, start with $q_0=4\pi\epsilon RV_s$ at height $z_0=h$ and place its opposite image below the plane. Cancel that image's nonconstant sphere potential by the successive charges
$$
q_{n+1}=\frac R{h+z_n}q_n,
\qquad z_{n+1}=h-\frac{R^2}{h+z_n},
$$
with opposite mirror charges below the plane at every step. The resulting sphere potential is $V_s$ and the plane potential is zero. Since $q_n=4\pi\epsilon RV_s\sinh\alpha/\sinh((n+1)\alpha)$, summing the sphere charges gives the displayed <capacitance>. In particular $c(y)=1+y/2+O(y^2)$ for small $y$.

The exact sphere-plane boundary-value problem is also treated in https://arxiv.org/abs/1206.6034[Behunin and colleagues' analysis of sphere-plane electrostatics].

= Capacitance per unit length
{parent=Capacitance}

Capacitance per unit length is the charge per unit length divided by the potential difference. A vacuum coaxial cable with radii $a<b$ has
$$
C'=\frac{2\pi\varepsilon_0}{\log(b/a)}.
$$

= Inductance
{parent=Electromagnetism}
{title2=$L$}
{wiki}

<Inductance> relates <electric currents> to the <magnetic flux linkage> they produce. For a single linear coil with fixed geometry and magnetic response, its <self-inductance> $L$ is the constant in
$$
\Lambda=LI.
$$
Here $\Lambda$ is <magnetic flux linkage> from the coil's own <electric current> $I$. The <SI> unit of <inductance> is the <henry (unit)>. Knowing <inductance> lets us predict the induced <electromotive force> when <electric current> changes, and the <magnetic energy> stored by an <inductor>. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-2/pages/14-2-self-inductance-and-inductors[OpenStax University Physics 2, §14.2: self-inductance and inductors]{ref}.

For example, a coil of <self-inductance> $0.5\,\mathrm H$ carrying <electric current> $3\,\mathrm A$ has <magnetic flux linkage> $1.5\,\mathrm{Wb}$. If its $100$ turns link the same <magnetic flux>, each turn has <magnetic flux> $0.015\,\mathrm{Wb}$. Keeping the geometry and magnetic response fixed while doubling <electric current> doubles <magnetic flux linkage> and quadruples the <inductor>'s <magnetic energy>.

The comparison with <capacitance> is useful: a linear <capacitor> has $Q=CV$ and stores <capacitor energy> $CV^2/2$ in its <electric field>, whereas a linear <inductor> has $\Lambda=LI$ and stores <magnetic energy> $LI^2/2$ in its <magnetic field>. The <RLC circuit> brings both forms of <energy> storage together.

= Magnetic flux linkage
{parent=Inductance}
{title2=$\Lambda=\sum_{k=1}^N\Phi_k$}

= Flux linkage
{synonym}

<Magnetic flux linkage> adds the signed <magnetic fluxes> through the turns of a coil:
$$
\Lambda=\sum_{k=1}^N\Phi_k,\qquad
\Phi_k=\int_{S_k}\mathbf B\cdot d\mathbf S.
$$
Choose each <oriented surface> $S_k$ consistently with positive <electric current> around its turn using the <right-hand rule>. If all $N$ turns link approximately the same <magnetic flux> $\Phi$, then $\Lambda\approx N\Phi$; equality holds when every turn links exactly the same <magnetic flux>. Uniform <magnetic field> perpendicular to identical flat turns of <area> $A$ gives $\Phi=BA$. For a stipulated $100$-turn coil with <magnetic flux> $0.015\,\mathrm{Wb}$ per turn, <magnetic flux linkage> is $1.5\,\mathrm{Wb}$, not $0.015\,\mathrm{Wb}$. The turn count is dimensionless. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-2/pages/14-2-self-inductance-and-inductors[OpenStax University Physics 2, §14.2: flux through the turns of a coil]{ref}.

= Henry
{disambiguate=unit}
{parent=Inductance}
{title2=$\mathrm H$}
{wiki}

The <henry (unit)> is the <SI> unit of <inductance>:
$$
1\,\mathrm H=1\,\frac{\mathrm{Wb}}{\mathrm A}
=1\,\frac{\mathrm{V\,s}}{\mathrm A}.
$$
A coil with <self-inductance> $1\,\mathrm H$ has <magnetic flux linkage> $1\,\mathrm{Wb}$ at <electric current> $1\,\mathrm A$. A rate of change of <electric current> $1\,\mathrm{A/s}$ produces induced <electromotive force> of magnitude $1\,\mathrm V$. The <weber (unit)> measures <magnetic flux> and <magnetic flux linkage>; the <henry (unit)> measures their ratio to <electric current>. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-2/pages/14-2-self-inductance-and-inductors[OpenStax University Physics 2, §14.2: the unit of self-inductance]{ref}.

= Self-inductance
{parent=Inductance}
{title2=$\Lambda=LI,\quad\mathcal E_{\rm ind}=-L\dot I$}

<Self-inductance> is <inductance> associated with a coil's own <electric current>, rather than <magnetic flux> supplied by another coil. In the linear model, fixed geometry and magnetic response give constant $L$ in $\Lambda=LI$. With the turn orientations defined in <magnetic flux linkage>, <Faraday's law of induction> gives the self-induced <electromotive force>
$$
\mathcal E_{\rm ind}=-\frac{d\Lambda}{dt}=-L\frac{dI}{dt}.
$$
The final equality requires constant $L$; if the geometry changes, use $-d\Lambda/dt$. The minus sign expresses <Lenz's law>: the induced <electromotive force> opposes the change in <electric current>. It does not always oppose the <electric current> itself. When positive <electric current> decreases, the induced <electromotive force> acts in its direction. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-2/pages/14-2-self-inductance-and-inductors[OpenStax University Physics 2, §14.2: self-induced emf and its direction]{ref}.

For terminal sign bookkeeping, label the ideal <inductor>'s terminals $a,b$, choose positive <electric current> entering $a$ and leaving $b$, and define $v_L$ as the terminal <electric potential difference> drop from $a$ to $b$ in the lumped <electrical circuit> model. This passive convention gives
$$
v_L=+L\frac{dI}{dt}=-\mathcal E_{\rm ind}.
$$
Thus a coil with <self-inductance> $0.5\,\mathrm H$ and increasing <electric current> $dI/dt=12\,\mathrm{A/s}$ has terminal drop $v_L=6\,\mathrm V$ and induced <electromotive force> $\mathcal E_{\rm ind}=-6\,\mathrm V$. These signs describe the same process with different reference conventions. The <RL circuit> uses the terminal-drop convention throughout.

A finite terminal <electric potential difference> cannot make the <electric current> of an ideal <inductor> with constant $L>0$ jump. Integrating its <differential equation> gives
$$
I(t_2)-I(t_1)=\frac1L\int_{t_1}^{t_2}v_L(t)\,dt.
$$
If $v_L$ stays bounded, the <integral> tends to zero as the interval shrinks, ensuring <continuity> of <electric current>. A step in terminal <electric potential difference> changes $dI/dt$ immediately, while <electric current> remains continuous. At constant <electric current>, the self-induced <electromotive force> is zero, although the <inductor>'s <magnetic energy> can remain nonzero.

= Inductor
{parent=Self-inductance}
{wiki}

An <inductor> is an <electrical circuit> component designed to provide <self-inductance>, often a coil such as a <solenoid>. An ideal linear <inductor> has constant $L>0$ and zero <electrical resistance>. Its stored <magnetic energy>, with zero chosen at zero <electric current>, is
$$
U=\frac12LI^2.
$$
For <self-inductance> $0.5\,\mathrm H$ and <electric current> $3\,\mathrm A$, this gives $U=2.25\,\mathrm J$. Unlike <Joule heating>, ideal storage is recoverable: the <inductor> can return <energy> to an <electrical circuit> when its <electric current> decreases. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-2/pages/14-3-energy-in-a-magnetic-field[OpenStax University Physics 2, §14.3: energy stored by an inductor]{ref}.

The short derivation uses the terminal convention in <self-inductance>. The <power> absorbed by an ideal <inductor> is
$$
P_L=v_L I=LI\frac{dI}{dt}=\frac{d}{dt}\left(\frac12LI^2\right).
$$
Starting from zero <electric current>, the <integral> of this <power> gives
$$
U=\int_0^t P_L(t')\,dt'=\int_0^I Li\,di=\frac12LI^2.
$$
Positive $P_L$ increases stored <magnetic energy>; negative $P_L$ returns <energy>. At constant <electric current> $3\,\mathrm A$ in the example, $P_L=0$ and induced <electromotive force> is zero, but $U=2.25\,\mathrm J$ still resides in the <magnetic field>. The <battery> in the steady <RL circuit> supplies <Joule heating> in the <electrical resistance>, rather than further increasing the <inductor>'s <magnetic energy>.

\Image[inductor-energy-storage-and-release.png]
{title=Stored <magnetic energy> and its release as <Joule heating> in an <RL circuit>}
{description=Left: the <inductor>'s <magnetic energy> during buildup and a separate decay experiment starting at $3\,\mathrm A$. Right: the <power> of <Joule heating> during decay; the shaded <integral> over all time is $2.25\,\mathrm J$. The decay path stays closed through <electrical resistance> $2\,\Omega$ and <self-inductance> $0.5\,\mathrm H$.}

During the closed-loop release in <RL circuit>, stored <magnetic energy> plus accumulated <Joule heating> stays $2.25\,\mathrm J$. A real coil also has <electrical resistance>, so its <Joule heating> must be included separately from ideal <inductor> storage. The constant-$L$ formulas assume a fixed linear magnetic response.

= Bound charge and bound current
{parent=Electromagnetism}

Polarization and magnetization produce the effective sources
$$
\rho_{\rm b}=-\nabla\cdot P,
\qquad
J_{\rm b}=\partial_tP+\nabla\times M.
$$
Defining $D=\epsilon_0E+P$ and $H=B/\mu_0-M$ moves these sources into the macroscopic fields.

= Bound current
{parent=Bound charge and bound current}
{title2=$\mathbf J_b=\partial_t\mathbf P+\nabla\times\mathbf M$}

The current associated with <electric polarization> and <magnetization> in matter. If the magnetic response is neglected, it is $\mathbf J_b=\partial_t\mathbf P$. It is distinguished from a free conduction current in the <Maxwell equations in matter>.

= Polarization density
{title2=$\mathbf P$}
{parent=Bound charge and bound current}
{wiki}

= Electric polarization
{synonym}

Electric polarization is electric dipole moment per unit volume. It produces bound volume and surface charge
$$
\rho_{\rm b}=-\nabla\cdot\mathbf P,
\qquad
\sigma_{\rm b}=\mathbf P\cdot\mathbf n.
$$
Across an interface, the net bound sheet charge is $(\mathbf P_1-\mathbf P_2)\cdot\mathbf n$, where $\mathbf n$ points from medium one to medium two.

= Electric displacement field
{title2=$\mathbf D$}
{parent=Polarization density}
{wiki}

The electric displacement is $\mathbf D=\epsilon_0\mathbf E+\mathbf P$ and obeys $\nabla\cdot\mathbf D=\rho_{\rm free}$. In a linear isotropic dielectric, $\mathbf D=\epsilon\mathbf E$.

= Magnetization
{title2=$\mathbf M$}
{parent=Bound charge and bound current}
{wiki}

Magnetization is magnetic dipole moment per unit volume. It produces bound volume and surface currents
$$
\mathbf J_{\rm b}=\nabla\times\mathbf M,
\qquad
\mathbf K_{\rm b}=\mathbf M\times\mathbf n.
$$
Across an interface, the net bound sheet current is $(\mathbf M_1-\mathbf M_2)\times\mathbf n$.

= Paramagnetism
{parent=Magnetization}
{wiki}
{title2=$\chi>0$}

In a linear isotropic regime, a paramagnetic material has positive <magnetic susceptibility>: its induced <magnetization> is aligned with the applied <magnetic field intensity>. <Molecular oxygen> supplies an example discussed through <molecular orbital theory> in the <covalent bond> article.

= Curie temperature
{c}
{parent=Magnetization}
{title2=$T_C$}
{wiki}

The Curie temperature is the transition temperature above which a ferromagnetic material loses spontaneous <magnetization>. A hot conducting planetary core can generate a <magnetic field> through <dynamo action> without behaving as a permanent magnet. The blocking temperature at which a mineral records crustal remanence also depends on its relaxation timescale and need not equal its Curie temperature.

= Remanent magnetization
{parent=Magnetization}

= Remanence
{synonym}

Remanent magnetization is <magnetization> retained after the applied <magnetic field> has been removed. Magnetic minerals cooling in an ambient field can preserve information about that field in a planetary crust. Such a record may outlive the <planetary dynamo> which originally produced it. Crustal remanence therefore differs from a present field maintained by conducting-fluid motion.

= Magnetization current
{title2=$J_{\mathrm{mag}}^\mu$}
{parent=Magnetization}

A relativistic magnetization current has the form $J_{\mathrm{mag}}^\mu=\partial_\nu M^{\nu\mu}$ for an antisymmetric polarization-magnetization tensor. It is identically conserved because commuting derivatives contract an antisymmetric tensor.

= Magnetic field intensity
{title2=$\mathbf H$}
{parent=Magnetization}

The magnetic field intensity is $\mathbf H=\mathbf B/\mu_0-\mathbf M$ and obeys $\nabla\times\mathbf H=\mathbf J_{\rm free}$ in magnetostatics. In a linear isotropic medium, $\mathbf B=\mu\mathbf H$.

= Magnetic spherical-shell matching
{parent=Electromagnetism}

Across an interface with no free surface current, the normal component of $B$ and tangential component of $H$ are continuous. For an axisymmetric spherical shell, harmonic scalar potentials have dipole form $(ar+b/r^2)\cos\theta$ in each radial region.

= Maxwell stress tensor
{c}
{parent=Electromagnetism}
{wiki}

With electromagnetic momentum density $\mathbf g=\epsilon_0\mathbf E\times\mathbf B$, the momentum-flux convention
$$
\sigma_{ij}=-\epsilon_0E_iE_j+\frac{\epsilon_0}{2}E^2\delta_{ij}
-\frac1{\mu_0}B_iB_j+\frac1{2\mu_0}B^2\delta_{ij}
$$
satisfies
$$
\partial_tg_j+\partial_i\sigma_{ij}=-(\rho\mathbf E+\mathbf J\times\mathbf B)_j.
$$

= Electromagnetic momentum density
{title2=$\mathbf g=\epsilon_0\mathbf E\times\mathbf B=\mathbf S/c^2$}
{parent=Maxwell stress tensor}

The electromagnetic momentum density in vacuum is $\mathbf g=\epsilon_0\mathbf E\times\mathbf B$. It equals the <Poynting vector> divided by $c^2$, expressing the relativistic relation between energy flux and momentum density.

= Local conservation of electromagnetic momentum
{parent=Maxwell stress tensor}

With the momentum-flux sign convention used for the <Maxwell stress tensor>, Maxwell's equations imply
$$
\nabla\mathbin{\cdot}\boldsymbol\sigma
+\partial_t\mathbf g=-\mathbf f_{\rm L},
$$
where $\mathbf g$ is the <electromagnetic momentum density> and $\mathbf f_{\rm L}$ is the <Lorentz force density> exerted by the field on matter. Thus total field-plus-matter momentum is locally conserved.

= Radiation pressure on a perfectly absorbing surface
{parent=Maxwell stress tensor}

A normally incident vacuum wave of time-averaged intensity $I$ transfers momentum $I/c$ per unit area and time to a perfectly absorbing surface, so its radiation pressure is
$$
p=\frac Ic.
$$
A perfect reflector reverses the normal momentum and gives $2I/c$.

= Proca action
{c}
{parent=Electromagnetism}
{wiki}

The Proca action adds $m^2A_\mu A^\mu/2$ to the Maxwell Lagrangian. It describes a massive spin-one field and explicitly breaks the Abelian gauge symmetry of the massless theory.

= Proca equation
{parent=Electromagnetism}
{c}

A massive vector field coupled to a conserved current obeys
$$
\partial_\mu F^{\mu\nu}-m^2A^\nu=-\mu_0J^\nu.
$$
The mass term breaks electromagnetic gauge invariance.

= Proca field
{c}
{parent=Proca equation}

A Proca field is a massive spin-one vector field. Its field equation enforces transversality and leaves three physical polarizations.

= Proca propagator
{c}
{parent=Proca field}

For metric signature $(+---)$, the momentum-space Feynman propagator of a Proca field is
$$
\widetilde\Delta_{\mu\nu}(p)=\frac{-i}{p^2-m^2+i\epsilon}\left(\eta_{\mu\nu}-\frac{p_\mu p_\nu}{m^2}\right).
$$
The longitudinal numerator term inverts the massive vector kinetic operator without gauge fixing.

= High-energy obstruction for a hard vector mass
{parent=Proca propagator}
{title2=$D_{\mu\nu}(k)=-i(g_{\mu\nu}-k_\mu k_\nu/m^2)/(k^2-m^2+i0)$}

A free <Proca field> is consistent and has three physical <particle polarizations>. The difficulty for generic interactions is the ultraviolet behavior of its <Proca propagator>: the longitudinal numerator makes part of it approach order $1/m^2$, rather than decay as $1/k^2$. Longitudinal external <polarization vectors> also behave as $\varepsilon_L^\mu=k^\mu/m+O(m/E)$. Without suitable identities and cancellations, amplitudes grow with energy and perturbative counterterms of increasing dimension are needed. Conserved-current Abelian models and the <Stueckelberg mechanism> are important exceptions; a massive interacting vector is not intrinsically inconsistent as an <effective field theory>.

= Proca time-ordering contact term
{c}
{parent=Proca propagator}
{title2=$\Delta^{\rm cov}_{ab}-\Delta^{T}_{ab}=i\delta_a^0\delta_b^0\delta^{(4)}/m^2$}

For a free massive <Proca field> in the mostly-plus <Minkowski metric>, the covariant inverse of the quadratic <action> and the canonical <time-ordered product> of vector potentials differ locally. With <Fourier transform> $e^{iq\cdot(x-y)}$, the covariant <Proca propagator> is $-i(\eta_{ab}+q_aq_b/m^2)/(q^2+m^2-i0)$, while the literal canonical <two-point correlation function> subtracts $i\delta_a^0\delta_b^0/m^2$ in momentum space. The time component is nondynamical; its physical-mode numerator is $|\boldsymbol q|^2/m^2$, rather than $-1+(q^0)^2/m^2$. The difference is an instantaneous <Dirac delta function>, not another propagating state. Both expressions agree away from coincidence. This distinction matters when identifying a covariant <Green function> with a canonical correlator in a constrained field theory.

= Transverse vector field
{parent=Proca field}

A vector field is transverse when $\partial_\mu V^\mu=0$ in spacetime, or $\mathbf k\cdot\mathbf V=0$ for a spatial Fourier mode.

= Lorenz constraint in Proca theory
{parent=Proca equation}
{c}

Taking a divergence and using antisymmetry of $F^{\mu\nu}$ and current conservation gives $m^2\partial_\mu A^\mu=0$. For nonzero mass, the Lorenz condition follows from the field equation rather than from a gauge choice.

= Yukawa potential
{parent=Proca equation}
{c}
{wiki}

The static Green function of $\nabla^2-m^2$ in three dimensions is proportional to $e^{-mr}/r$. It describes an interaction screened beyond range $m^{-1}$ and tends to the Coulomb potential as $m\to0$.

= Relativistic Lorentz force
{parent=Electromagnetism}

$m\,du^\mu/d\tau=qF^\mu{}_\nu u^\nu$ gives $d\mathbf p/dt=q(\mathbf E+\mathbf v\times\mathbf B)$ and $d(\gamma mc^2)/dt=q\mathbf E\cdot\mathbf v$.

= Constant-field relativistic Lorentz-force solution
{parent=Relativistic Lorentz force}

For a constant <electromagnetic field tensor>, the <relativistic Lorentz force> is a constant-coefficient linear system on <four-velocity>, with solution
$$
u(\tau)=\exp\!\left(\frac qmF\tau\right)u(0).
$$
The electric-type blocks of $F$ generate hyperbolic rotations, while magnetic-type blocks generate ordinary rotations.

= Relativistic motion in parallel electric and magnetic fields
{parent=Constant-field relativistic Lorentz-force solution}

When uniform $\mathbf E$ and $\mathbf B$ are parallel, the <relativistic Lorentz force> splits into independent hyperbolic motion along the fields and circular motion in the perpendicular plane. The circle is periodic in <proper time>, while electric acceleration makes the helix's longitudinal pitch grow exponentially from one proper-time cyclotron period to the next.

= Relativistic hyperbolic motion in a uniform electric field
{parent=Relativistic Lorentz force}

A particle released from rest in a constant electric field parallel to its motion follows a spacetime hyperbola and has rapidity proportional to proper time.

This force law realizes <hyperbolic motion> with constant <proper acceleration>.

= Null crossed electromagnetic field
{parent=Relativistic Lorentz force}

Perpendicular fields with $\mathbf E\cdot\mathbf B=0$ and $E^2-c^2B^2=0$ form a null electromagnetic field. The constant-field Lorentz-force generator is nilpotent, so its exponential terminates as a polynomial.

Both invariants of the <electromagnetic field tensor> vanish for this field class.

= Relativistic trajectory in a constant null crossed field
{parent=Null crossed electromagnetic field}

Choose
$$
\mathbf B=B\,\mathbf e_z,\qquad
\mathbf E=cB\,\mathbf e_x,
\qquad
\Omega=\frac{qB}{m}.
$$
A particle released from rest at the origin has the proper-time trajectory
$$
t(\tau)=\tau+\frac{\Omega^2\tau^3}{6},
\qquad
x(\tau)=\frac{c\Omega\tau^2}{2},
\qquad
y(\tau)=-\frac{c\Omega^2\tau^3}{6},
\qquad z(\tau)=0.
$$
Eliminating <proper time> gives the semicubical parabola
$$
\boxed{y^2=\frac{2qB}{9mc}x^3.}
$$
This signed coefficient assumes that the positive $x$ direction is chosen along $\mathbf E$; reversing that axis changes the signs of both $x^3$ and its coefficient.

= Charged particle in a uniform magnetic field
{parent=Electromagnetism}
{wiki}

A velocity cross magnetic-field force bends perpendicular motion into a circle while doing no work.

= Magnetic angular-momentum invariant
{parent=Charged particle in a uniform magnetic field}
{title2=$\mathbf L\cdot\mathbf B+\frac q2|\mathbf r\times\mathbf B|^2$}

For a particle obeying $m\dot{\mathbf v}=q\mathbf v\times\mathbf B$ in a constant uniform <magnetic field>, the displayed combination is conserved. The derivative of mechanical <angular momentum> projected onto the field is canceled by the quadratic position term. This is the rotational analogue of the distinction between mechanical and <canonical momentum> in an electromagnetic field.

= Helical motion in a uniform magnetic field
{parent=Charged particle in a uniform magnetic field}

In a uniform <magnetic field>, the <Lorentz force> leaves the component of <velocity> parallel to the field constant and rotates the perpendicular component at the nonrelativistic <cyclotron frequency> $|q|B/m$. The resulting path is a <helix> around a field line.

= Guiding center
{parent=Charged particle in a uniform magnetic field}
{wiki}

= Guiding centre
{synonym}

The guiding center is the center of a charged particle's circular cyclotron orbit. In a nonuniform field it drifts slowly compared with the rapid <cyclotron frequency>.

= Damped cyclotron motion
{parent=Charged particle in a uniform magnetic field}
{wiki}

Adding linear drag to cyclotron motion produces an exponentially contracting spiral.

= Electrostatics
{parent=Electromagnetism}
{wiki}

<Electrostatics> studies time-independent <electric charge> distributions and their <electric fields>. In <vacuum>, its local equations in <SI> units are
$$
\nabla\cdot\mathbf E=\frac{\rho}{\epsilon_0},\qquad \nabla\times\mathbf E=\mathbf0,\qquad \mathbf E=-\nabla\Phi,\qquad \nabla^2\Phi=-\frac{\rho}{\epsilon_0}.
$$
Here $\rho$ is <charge density>, $\epsilon_0$ is <vacuum permittivity>, and $\Phi$ is <electric potential>. <Gauss's law> supplies the <divergence> equation; the static form of <Faraday's law of induction> supplies the zero <curl>. The <Poisson equation> becomes <Laplace's equation> where there is no <electric charge>. Prescribed <electric charges> give <electric fields> through <Coulomb's law> and the <superposition principle>; mobile <electric charges> on an <electrical conductor> instead require solving for their distribution together with the <electric potential>. https://farside.ph.utexas.edu/teaching/em/lectures/node40.html[Richard Fitzpatrick, Electrostatics and magnetostatics: field equations and boundary data]{ref}.

The useful distinction is between prescribed sources and prescribed <boundary conditions>. On a grounded <electrical conductor>, an external connection fixes its <electric potential> relative to an <electrical reference potential> and allows <electric charge> to enter or leave. On an isolated <electrical conductor>, total <electric charge> is fixed and its constant <electric potential> is an unknown. A neutral isolated <electrical conductor> can therefore have a nonzero local <surface charge density> through <electrostatic induction>. Equal <electric potentials> also do not imply equal <electric charges>: <charge sharing between distant connected spheres> gives a simple geometric example. https://www.feynmanlectures.caltech.edu/II_06.html#Ch6-S6[Feynman, Leighton and Sands, II.6: charged conductors and connected-sphere estimates]{ref}.

<Electrostatics> is useful because a spatial <boundary value problem> replaces the need to follow every moving <charge carrier> during <charge relaxation>. It describes the settled state of a <capacitor> and explains <electrostatic shielding> of sensitive equipment. Its assumptions must match the apparatus: stationary boundaries and sources, negligible time variation of the <magnetic field>, and enough time for <charge relaxation>. A metal carrying a maintained <electric current> through finite <electrical resistance> generally needs a nonzero internal <electric field> by <Ohm's law>; it is not a <conductor in electrostatic equilibrium>. Static macroscopic <electric charge> also does not mean that every <electron> is motionless. https://www.feynmanlectures.caltech.edu/II_07.html#Ch7-S1[Feynman, Leighton and Sands, II.7.1: conductor potentials as boundary-value problems]{ref}.

= Faraday's ice pail experiment
{c}
{parent=Electrostatics}

<Faraday's ice pail experiment> distinguishes **redistribution** of <electric charge> by <electrostatic induction> from **transfer** of <electric charge> by contact. Lower a charged body into an initially neutral isolated metal pail: an externally connected <electrometer> responds even though the body never touches the metal. Withdraw the body without contact and the response disappears. Repeat with conductive contact before withdrawal and a residual response can remain. The striking point is that an exterior response does not establish that the pail has gained net <electric charge>.

**Apparatus and readout.** A modern implementation uses two concentric wire-mesh cylinders: a 10 cm diameter, 15 cm high inner pail on insulating rods and a grounded outer shield. Connect the <electrometer>'s signal lead to the inner pail and its reference lead to the shield. Momentarily ground and zero the signal assembly, then isolate it; the shield remains grounded. A charged disk on an insulating handle goes into the lower half without touching. The <electrometer> measures <electric potential difference> in <volts>, with sign determined by the lead connections. It does not directly measure <electric charge> in <coulombs>.<Basic Electrostatics System (ES-9080B), Instruction Manual 012-07227G by Cecilia Hernandez>[Basic Electrostatics System, instruction manual 012-07227G, printed pp. 9–10, 13–15, Figure 1.1 and Procedure 1A]{ref}

\Image[faraday-ice-pail-apparatus-and-enclosed-charge.png]
{title=<Faraday's ice pail experiment>: apparatus and isolated versus grounded <electric charge> accounting}
{description=Original qualitative schematics. The open pail approximates enclosure; the two closed <cavity in a conductor> panels stipulate exact enclosure, <electrostatic equilibrium>, no exterior sources and zero <electric potential> at infinity. Signs denote total <electric charges>, not a measured or uniform <surface charge density>.}

**A charge ledger before contact.** To separate exact reasoning from the open apparatus, first idealize the pail as a closed <cavity in a conductor> with one internal body of total <electric charge> $q$. Let the isolated metal have total <electric charge> $Q_0$. The metal's <electric field> is zero at <electrostatic equilibrium>.<Intermediate College Physics by Richard Fitzpatrick/Ideal Conductors>[Richard Fitzpatrick, Ideal Conductors, equations 2.124–2.126: zero field and constant potential in metal]{ref} A <Gaussian surface> enclosing the <cavity in a conductor> but lying wholly in the metal therefore has zero <electric flux>. <Gauss's law> and <charge conservation> give
$$
q+Q_{\rm inner}=0,\qquad Q_{\rm inner}+Q_{\rm outer}=Q_0,\qquad Q_{\rm outer}=Q_0+q.
$$
For $Q_0=0$ and $q>0$, the inner-wall total is $-q$ and the outer-wall total is $+q$: the metal remains neutral. The exterior <electric field> can therefore be nonzero without any transfer from the body. The connected <electrometer> and signal cable belong to the same isolated conducting assembly and share its exterior <electric charge>. The zero-<electric field> assertion concerns metal, not the occupied <cavity in a conductor>.

This ledger fixes totals, not the local <surface charge density>. Moving an off-center body changes the inner-wall distribution while its <integral> remains $-q$. With fixed outer geometry, fixed $Q_0+q$ and unchanged exterior sources, <uniqueness of the Dirichlet problem> together with the fixed total outer <electric charge> determines the same exterior solution: the outside cannot reveal the body's position or detailed internal distribution. For example, internal bodies with stipulated <electric charges> $+2q$ and $-q$ give the same total exterior response as a single body with $+q$. Internal bodies with $+q$ and $-q$ can give a null exterior response while retaining a nonzero internal <electric field>. This is why a null reading cannot establish an empty <cavity in a conductor>.

**Withdrawal is the control.** Record a baseline, insert without contact, withdraw, and check return to baseline. After a fresh zero, repeat with the charged disk touching the inner wall, then withdraw and record the residual response. Finally zero the pail and reinsert the disk without contact to test remaining <electric charge>. Keep the operator grounded, discharge unwanted handle <electric charge>, and keep the unused charged disk distant. Dirty supports and humidity promote leakage. These controls separate transfer from <electrostatic induction> and stray coupling.<Basic Electrostatics System (ES-9080B), Instruction Manual 012-07227G by Cecilia Hernandez>[Basic Electrostatics System, instruction manual 012-07227G, printed pp. 4–5 and 14–15, Procedure 1A steps 3–8: handle charge, withdrawal, contact and disk retest]{ref}

\Image[faraday-ice-pail-withdrawal-contact-readout.png]
{title=<Faraday's ice pail experiment>: qualitative withdrawal, contact and grounding sequences}
{description=Stipulated ideal-model outcomes for a body initially carrying positive <electric charge> $q$, with a separate neutral initial state for each row. No measured <electric potential differences> or time scale are depicted. The grounding sequence disconnects ground before withdrawal; reversing that order removes its residual <electric charge>.}

In the ideal contact test, the internal body is itself an <electrical conductor> and makes a conducting connection to the wall. After <charge relaxation>, body and enclosure become a single <equipotential>; the remaining charge-free void has zero <electric field>. The body then carries no excess <electric charge> on surfaces facing that void. Withdrawal without subsequent charging leaves total metal <electric charge> $Q_0+q$. A coated paddle can retain <electric charge> on insulating patches, so merely touching it does not guarantee complete transfer. A residual reading establishes some transfer under the controls; the disk retest checks the stronger claim of complete transfer. The ideal argument assumes no leakage and no relevant contact-generated charging.

**Grounding changes the ledger.** With no exterior sources and zero <electric potential> at infinity, a closed grounded enclosure has zero exterior <electric field>, inner-wall <electric charge> $-q$ and zero outer-wall <electric charge>.<The Feynman Lectures on Physics, Volume II by Richard Feynman and others>[II.5, §5–10, final shielding argument: a closed grounded conducting shell separates the two electrostatic problems]{ref} Consequently, disconnecting ground before withdrawal retains total metal <electric charge> $-q$, which moves to the exterior after withdrawal. Keeping ground connected during withdrawal instead restores zero <electric charge>. A lasting opposite-sign response can therefore arise through grounding without body–wall contact.

For the real open pail, no <Gaussian surface> lying wholly in metal closes over the mouth. Increasing insertion depth and seeking a stable response tests whether enclosure is a good approximation; mesh openings, rim proximity and nearby objects limit it. In a fixed-geometry approximation, the positive insertion response obeys $\Delta V\simeq q/C_{\rm eff}$, where $C_{\rm eff}$ is the effective <capacitance> of the pail, shield, cable and <electrometer> input. Changed geometry changes that conversion. Wait for <charge relaxation> and instrument settling at each step, while keeping the observation short compared with leakage. The experiment probes settled <electrostatics>; it does not establish shielding against arbitrary changing <electromagnetic fields>.

= Electrostatic interface boundary conditions
{parent=Electrostatics}
{title2=$[D_n]=\sigma_{\rm fixed}$}

Across a charged dielectric interface the tangential <electric field> is continuous and the normal displacement jump is the fixed <surface charge density>. Without a dipole sheet the <electrostatic potential> is continuous. For a charge sheet with equal <permittivity> on both sides, its normal potential derivative has jump $-\sigma_{\rm fixed}/\varepsilon$. Setting the field to zero on one side is an additional physical boundary assumption, as in a conductor or a negligible-exterior-admittance idealization.

= Debye–Hückel approximation
{c}
{parent=Electrostatics}
{title2=$(\nabla^2-\kappa_D^2)\phi=-\rho_{\rm fixed}/\varepsilon$}

The Debye–Hückel approximation linearizes mobile-ion Boltzmann densities about a neutral bulk electrolyte. It is valid for ionic potential energies small compared with $k_BT$. The inverse <Debye–Hückel screening length> satisfies $\kappa_D^2=\sum_i n_i z_i^2e^2/(\varepsilon k_BT)$. The quadratic screened charging free energy includes both <electric field> energy and the linearized ionic-response term $\varepsilon\kappa_D^2\phi^2/2$.

= Screened sinusoidal surface-charge mode
{parent=Debye–Hückel approximation}
{title2=$p=\sqrt{k^2+\kappa_D^2}$}

A lateral sinusoidal <surface charge density> with wave number $k$ in the <Debye–Hückel approximation> generates vertical exponential or hyperbolic functions with inverse length $p=\sqrt{k^2+\kappa_D^2}$. In a homogeneous electrolyte filling all space, an isolated sheet at $z_s$ produces $\phi=\sigma_k(x)e^{-p|z-z_s|}/(2\varepsilon p)$. Boundary conditions determine the normalization when the electrolyte occupies only a gap.

= Phase registration of screened charged sheets
{parent=Screened sinusoidal surface-charge mode}
{title2=$\theta_{\min}=\pi\pmod{2\pi}$}

Two equally modulated charge sheets in identical full-space electrolyte have charging energy per area $\alpha^2[1+e^{-pd}\cos\theta]/(4\varepsilon p)$, where $p=\sqrt{k^2+\kappa_D^2}$. Its phase-dependent interaction favors a half-wavelength offset, with opposite charge patches facing each other. For an electrolyte gap with negligible exterior displacement response, the energy is instead $\alpha^2[\coth(pd)+\cos\theta/\sinh(pd)]/(2\varepsilon p)$, with the same minimizing phase. Absolute energies require specified exterior <electrostatic interface boundary conditions>.

= Bjerrum length
{parent=Electrostatics}
{c}
{title2=$\ell_B$}
{wiki}

For a chosen reference charge $q$, the separation at which its Coulomb interaction energy equals $k_BT$. The charge-$q$ convention is $\ell_B=q^2/(4\pi\epsilon_{\rm abs}k_BT)$ in SI, or $\ell_B=\beta q^2/\epsilon$ in <Gaussian units>. The commonly tabulated value uses the elementary charge; changing <counterion> valence requires tracking that reference convention. It sets the strength of <counterion condensation>.

= Poisson-Boltzmann equation
{parent=Electrostatics}
{c}
{wiki=Poisson–Boltzmann_equation}

A mean-field coupling of the <Poisson equation> to an ideal <Boltzmann distribution> of mobile <ions>. In <Gaussian units> with uniform dielectric constant $\epsilon$, $\nabla^2\phi=-(4\pi/\epsilon)[\rho_f+\sum_i q_i n_{i,0}e^{-\beta q_i\phi}]$. The potential reference and chemical potentials determine the normalization factors. It neglects discrete-ion correlations and assumes local <thermal equilibrium>. A counterion-only model is not the same equation as a symmetric salt reservoir.

= Neutral cylindrical cell model
{parent=Poisson-Boltzmann equation}
{title2=$E(R)=0$}

Confine the <counterions> of a uniformly charged infinite cylinder within a coaxial cylinder of radius $R$, with total mobile line charge $\lambda$. The <electric field> vanishes at $R$, while its surface value at $a$ is fixed by the bare line charge. This gives $a\phi'(a)=2\lambda/\epsilon$ and $R\phi'(R)=0$ in <Gaussian units>. Sending $R/a\to\infty$ defines the <infinite-dilution limit of cylindrical counterions>.

= Infinite-dilution limit of cylindrical counterions
{parent=Neutral cylindrical cell model}

In a neutral counterion-only cylindrical cell, send the outer radius to infinity at fixed cylinder charge. Below the <Manning parameter> threshold, number density tends to zero at each fixed radius. Above threshold, the <cylindrical counterion-only Poisson-Boltzmann profile> retains line charge $\lambda-q/\ell_B$ locally, while the rest recedes to infinity. Therefore the limit of the full finite-cell charge integral is not the charge integral of the pointwise limiting density.

= Cylindrical counterion-only Poisson-Boltzmann profile
{parent=Poisson-Boltzmann equation}
{title2=$\Phi=2\log[1+(\eta-1)u]$}

For $u=\log(r/a)$ and $\Phi=\beta q\phi-2u$, a counterion-only <Poisson-Boltzmann equation> reduces to $\Phi''=-Ce^{-\Phi}$. Conditions $\Phi(0)=0$, $\Phi\to\infty$ and $\Phi'\to0$ give $\Phi=2\log(1+bu)$, $b=\sqrt{C/2}>0$. Matching surface <Gauss's law> gives $b=\eta-1$, so this branch requires the <Manning parameter> $\eta>1$. Its number density is $n(r)=(\eta-1)^2(a/r)^2/[2\pi\ell_Ba^2(1+(\eta-1)\log(r/a))^2]$. It leaves residual line-charge magnitude $q/\ell_B$.

= Counterion condensation
{parent=Poisson-Boltzmann equation}
{wiki}

= Manning condensation
{c}
{synonym}

Localization of a finite fraction of oppositely charged <ions> near a charged object even as the confining volume grows without bound. In the salt-free infinite-cylinder mean-field model, the <Manning parameter> must exceed one for a nonzero limiting local density. The condensed charge does not include <ions> that escape to infinity; finite-cell charge neutrality and the local infinite-volume limit must not be interchanged.

= Manning condensed fraction
{parent=Counterion condensation}
{c}
{title2=$f_c=\max(1-1/\eta,0)$}

In the mean-field infinite-dilution cylinder model, the fraction of neutralizing charge that remains localized is $f_c=\max(1-1/\eta,0)$, where $\eta$ is the <Manning parameter>. For $\eta>1$, integrating the <cylindrical counterion-only Poisson-Boltzmann profile> gives $Q_c=\lambda-q/\ell_B$. Below threshold, the local limiting density vanishes even though every finite neutral cell contains the full <counterion> charge.

= Cylindrical screening charge
{parent=Counterion condensation}
{title2=$Q(r)=2\pi\int_a^r s\rho(s)\,ds$}

The positive mobile charge per unit axial length accumulated from cylinder radius $a$ to $r$ is $Q(r)=2\pi\int_a^r s\rho(s)\,ds$. For fixed negative line charge $-\lambda$, <Gauss's law> gives signed radial <electric field> $E_r=2[-\lambda+Q(r)]/(\epsilon r)$ in <Gaussian units>. Its absolute value is the field magnitude. An integral over a local infinite-dilution profile measures condensed charge, rather than every <ion> in a finite neutral cell.

= Manning parameter
{parent=Counterion condensation}
{c}
{title2=$\eta=\lambda\ell_B/q$}

The dimensionless line-charge coupling $\eta=\lambda\ell_B/q$, with negative cylinder line charge $-\lambda$ and <counterions> of charge $q>0$. Here the <Bjerrum length> uses the same reference charge $q$. The mean-field infinite-dilution threshold is $\eta=1$. If $\ell_B$ is instead tabulated for elementary charge $e$ and <counterions> have charge $ze$, the corresponding coupling is $z\lambda\ell_B/e$.

= Coulomb's law
{parent=Electrostatics}
{c}
{wiki}
{title2=$\mathbf F_{2\leftarrow1}=kq_1q_2\mathbf r/|\mathbf r|^3$}

<Coulomb's law> gives the mutual <electric force> between stationary <point charges> in <vacuum>. Put <electric charges> $q_1$ and $q_2$ at $\mathbf r_1$ and $\mathbf r_2$, and define the <displacement> **from charge 1 to charge 2** by $\mathbf r=\mathbf r_2-\mathbf r_1$, with separation $r=|\mathbf r|>0$ and <unit vector> $\widehat{\mathbf r}=\mathbf r/r$. In <SI> units, the <force> on charge 2 due to charge 1 is
$$
\boxed{\mathbf F_{2\leftarrow1}=kq_1q_2\frac{\mathbf r}{|\mathbf r|^3}=\frac{kq_1q_2}{r^2}\widehat{\mathbf r},\qquad k=\frac{1}{4\pi\varepsilon_0}.}
$$
Here $\varepsilon_0$ is the <vacuum permittivity>. The magnitude is $k|q_1q_2|/r^2$. If $q_1q_2>0$, the <force> on charge 2 points away from charge 1; if $q_1q_2<0$, it points toward charge 1. Interchanging the charges gives $\mathbf F_{1\leftarrow2}=-\mathbf F_{2\leftarrow1}$, consistent with <Newton's third law> in this electrostatic setting. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-2/pages/5-3-coulombs-law[OpenStax, University Physics II, §5.3: Coulomb's law]{ref}.

For a fully computed example, stipulate $q_1=+2\,\mathrm{nC}$, $q_2=-3\,\mathrm{nC}$, $\mathbf r_1=\mathbf0$, $\mathbf r_2=0.20\,\mathrm m\,\widehat{\mathbf x}$ and the approximate value $k=8.99\times10^9\,\mathrm{N\,m^2/C^2}$. Then
$$
\mathbf F_{2\leftarrow1}=\frac{(8.99\times10^9)(2\times10^{-9})(-3\times10^{-9})}{(0.20)^2}\,\mathrm N\,\widehat{\mathbf x}=-1.3485\times10^{-6}\,\mathrm N\,\widehat{\mathbf x}.
$$
The <force> is attractive, of magnitude $1.3485\times10^{-6}\,\mathrm N$. The source <electric field> at charge 2 is $\mathbf E_1(\mathbf r_2)=449.5\,\mathrm{N/C}\,\widehat{\mathbf x}$, pointing away from the positive source. Multiplication by the negative $q_2$ reverses the direction to give the attractive <force>. This field excludes charge 2's own self-field.

Holding the <electric charges> fixed while changing the separation from $R$ to $2R$ or $3R$ gives the normalized <inverse-square law> values
$$
\frac{|\mathbf F(R)|}{|\mathbf F(R)|}=1,\qquad \frac{|\mathbf F(2R)|}{|\mathbf F(R)|}=\frac14,\qquad \frac{|\mathbf F(3R)|}{|\mathbf F(R)|}=\frac19.
$$
For the stipulated example, separations $0.20$, $0.40$ and $0.60\,\mathrm m$ give magnitudes $1.3485\times10^{-6}$, $3.37125\times10^{-7}$ and $1.498333\ldots\times10^{-7}\,\mathrm N$. The diagram computes these ratios and uses one common arrow-length scale for both sign choices.

\Image[coulomb-force-distance.png]
{title=Equal and opposite Coulomb force vectors: like charges repel, unlike charges attract, and doubling or tripling separation gives one quarter or one ninth of the force}

The <inverse-square law> has a geometric explanation through <Gauss's law>: a source <point charge> gives fixed total <electric flux> through a concentric <sphere>, whose <surface area> is $4\pi r^2$. Dividing that flux by the area gives a radial <electric field> proportional to $1/r^2$. This connects a pairwise <force> calculation to a global conservation-like accounting of field sources. For several stationary <point charges>, the <superposition principle> adds the individual <electric fields> as <vectors>, and $\mathbf F_i=q_i\mathbf E_{\mathrm{other}}(\mathbf r_i)$ uses all sources except charge $i$ itself. The <Coulomb potential energy> gives an alternative route when the desired quantity is <work> rather than instantaneous <force>.

= Coulomb potential energy
{parent=Coulomb's law}
{c}
{title2=$U(r)=kq_1q_2/r$}

= Coulomb potential
{synonym}
{c}

The <Coulomb potential energy> is the interaction <potential energy> of two <point charges> in <vacuum>. With separation $r>0$, <electric charges> $q_1,q_2$, $k=1/(4\pi\varepsilon_0)$ and zero <potential energy> at infinite separation,
$$
\boxed{U(r)=\frac{kq_1q_2}{r}.}
$$
This is mutual interaction <energy>; it does not include the divergent self-energies of ideal <point charges>. Unlike <electric potential>, which has units of <volts>, $U$ has <SI> units of joules. With charge 1 fixed and positive radial direction away from it, the <conservative force> in <Coulomb's law> gives
$$
U(r)-U(\infty)=-\int_\infty^r\frac{kq_1q_2}{s^2}\,ds=\frac{kq_1q_2}{r},\qquad
F_r=-\frac{dU}{dr}=\frac{kq_1q_2}{r^2}.
$$
Thus the $1/r$ <potential energy> and the <inverse-square law> for <force> are two descriptions of the same interaction. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-2/pages/7-1-electric-potential-energy[OpenStax, University Physics II, §7.1: electric potential energy]{ref}.

For $q_1=+2\,\mathrm{nC}$, $q_2=-3\,\mathrm{nC}$ and $r=0.20\,\mathrm m$, using the stipulated approximation $k=8.99\times10^9\,\mathrm{N\,m^2/C^2}$,
$$
U=\frac{(8.99\times10^9)(2\times10^{-9})(-3\times10^{-9})}{0.20}\,\mathrm J=-2.697\times10^{-7}\,\mathrm J.
$$
At $r=0.40\,\mathrm m$, $U=-1.3485\times10^{-7}\,\mathrm J$. Separating the pair from $0.20$ to $0.40\,\mathrm m$ therefore requires external <work> $\Delta U=+1.3485\times10^{-7}\,\mathrm J$ if the initial and final <kinetic energies> agree; the <electric force> does $-1.3485\times10^{-7}\,\mathrm J$ of <work>. Separating them all the way to infinity requires $+2.697\times10^{-7}\,\mathrm J$. The negative initial <potential energy> means that the pair lies below the chosen separated reference; it does not mean a negative <kinetic energy>.

Like-signed <electric charges> have positive $U$, which decreases as they separate, so their <electric force> is repulsive. Opposite-signed <electric charges> have negative $U$, which decreases as they approach, so their <electric force> is attractive. This attractive interaction supplies the <Coulomb potential> used in the <hydrogen atom>; determining its stationary quantum states also requires <quantum mechanics>. https://www.feynmanlectures.caltech.edu/III_19.html#Ch19-S1[Feynman lectures III.19, §19–1: the Schrödinger equation for hydrogen]{ref}. Dividing $U$ by a nonzero test <electric charge> $q_2$ gives the source's <electric potential> $\Phi_1=kq_1/r$. More generally, the <electrostatic potential energy of point charges> sums each unordered charge pair once. This <energy> description is useful because <work> between two separations follows from endpoint values, without integrating a complicated trajectory.

= Electrostatic equilibrium
{parent=Electrostatics}

A state with a stationary <electric charge> distribution and no net drift of mobile charge driven by the <electric field>. A <conductor in electrostatic equilibrium> has zero interior <electric field>, while bound charges in an insulating material need not move to eliminate its interior field.

= Conductor in electrostatic equilibrium
{parent=Electrostatics}

A <conductor in electrostatic equilibrium> is an <electrical conductor> at rest whose mobile <charge carriers> have finished redistributing, with no maintained bulk <electric current>. In the ideal macroscopic model,
$$
\mathbf E_{\rm metal}=\mathbf0,\qquad \Phi=V_c\ \text{on each connected conductor},\qquad \rho_{\rm metal}=0.
$$
A residual bulk <electric field> would drive further <electric charge> redistribution. The <electric potential difference> between any two points joined through the metal is $-\int\mathbf E\cdot d\mathbf l=0$, so each connected piece is an <equipotential>. <Gauss's law> then gives zero net bulk <charge density>. This is a statement about net macroscopic <electric charge>, not the absence of positive <atomic nuclei> and negative <electrons>. Excess <electric charge> is represented by <surface charge density>; atomic surface structure is outside this continuum model. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-2/pages/6-4-conductors-in-electrostatic-equilibrium[Samuel Ling, William Moebs and Jeff Sanny, Conductors in electrostatic equilibrium]{ref}.

The word **inside** must distinguish metal from a <cavity in a conductor>. A completely enclosed, charge-free <cavity in a conductor> has constant boundary <electric potential> $V_c$. The constant solution of <Laplace's equation> and <uniqueness of the Dirichlet problem> give $\Phi=V_c$ and $\mathbf E=\mathbf0$ throughout the <cavity in a conductor>, regardless of its shape or external <electric charges>. Grounding is unnecessary for this inward <electrostatic shielding>; an isolated enclosure can change its overall <electric potential> without producing an internal <electric field>. Zero enclosed net <electric charge> alone would only give zero <electric flux>. A positive and negative pair inside a <cavity in a conductor> can have zero total <electric charge> and a nonzero <electric field>. <Intermediate College Physics by Richard Fitzpatrick/Ideal Conductors>[Richard Fitzpatrick, Ideal Conductors: charge-free cavities and uniqueness]{ref}.

If fixed <electric charges> inside a <cavity in a conductor> sum to $q$, a <Gaussian surface> lying entirely in the metal and enclosing that <cavity in a conductor> has zero <electric flux>. Thus the total induced inner-wall <electric charge> is $-q$. For one such <cavity in a conductor> and an isolated enclosure with total metal <electric charge> $Q_c$, the outer-wall <electric charge> is $Q_c+q$. In particular, an initially neutral isolated enclosure generally has an exterior <electric field> when $q\ne0$. For fixed internal sources, external changes only shift the constant <electric potential> inside an isolated enclosure, leaving the internal <electric field> unchanged. https://arxiv.org/abs/1609.04248[Aritro Pathak, On the electrostatic equilibrium of charges and cavities in a conductor]{ref}.

A grounded enclosure fixes $V_c=0$ and can acquire metal <electric charge> $-q$; with no exterior sources and $\Phi\to0$ at infinity, <uniqueness of the Dirichlet problem> gives zero exterior <electric field>. With external sources present, the exterior <electric field> need not vanish, but it is independent of the internal sources at fixed grounded boundary <electric potential>. https://www.feynmanlectures.caltech.edu/II_05.html#Ch5-S10[Feynman, Leighton and Sands, II.5.10: shielding by a closed grounded enclosure]{ref}.

<Faraday's ice pail experiment> makes the distinction between redistribution and transfer visible. In his letter of 4 February 1843, Faraday described an insulated pewter pail connected by wire to a gold-leaf <electrometer>, and a brass ball suspended on a long dry silk thread to reduce the hand's influence. He discharged the pail and charged the ball away from it. Lowering the ball without contact produced leaf divergence, which grew during entry and became approximately independent of further depth; withdrawing it collapsed the leaves. Contact with the bottom transferred <electric charge>, and the withdrawn ball was discharged. The readout was leaf divergence, not a direct map of the internal <electric field>. These observations distinguish <electrostatic induction> from contact transfer of <electric charge>. https://physics.kenyon.edu/EarlyApparatus/Static_Electricity/Faradays_Ice_Pail/Faraday_Ice_Pail.html[Thomas B. Greenslade Jr., Faraday Ice Pail, reproducing Faraday's 1843 letter]{ref}.

For a modern version of <Faraday's ice pail experiment>, connect an insulated inner metal pail to the signal input of a high-input-impedance <electrometer>, with its reference lead and an outer shield grounded. Zero and momentarily ground the pail, then isolate the signal side. Lower a charged paddle deeply without touching and record the <electric potential difference>; withdraw it and check return to zero. Repeat with contact, then withdrawal: a residual reading indicates transferred <electric charge>. Reverse the paddle's sign and repeat. The <electrometer> reads <volts>; inferring <electric charge> requires the effective <capacitance> of pail, cable and input, so a stable reading at fixed geometry is a relative measure, not automatically a value in <coulombs>. Keep supports clean, nearby charged objects distant, and the operator grounded; humidity can cause leakage. https://cdn.pasco.com/product_document/Basic-Electrostatics-Sys-Manual-ES-9080B1.pdf#page=13[PASCO, Basic Electrostatics System manual, equipment description and demonstration 1A]{ref}.

\Image[electrostatic-conductor-equilibrium-ice-pail.png]
{title=<Faraday's ice pail experiment>: insertion without contact versus transfer by contact}
{description=Original procedural schematic, not measured data or a reproduction of historical apparatus. The deep-insertion charge accounting idealizes a nearly enclosing pail; positive exterior <electric charge> is shared over the pail and connected signal assembly. The grounded outer shield and reference connection are shown in the insertion panel. Exact closed-enclosure predictions apply to a fully enclosed <cavity in a conductor>, not automatically to an open pail near its rim.}

A complementary <electrostatic shielding> demonstration uses a charged hollow metal enclosure, a small metal sampling ball on an insulating handle, and an <electrometer>. Discharge the sampling ball between trials; touch it to the exterior, withdraw it, and transfer its collected <electric charge> to the <electrometer>. Repeat by touching the inner wall without touching the exterior during withdrawal. The exterior sample produces a response, whereas the inner sample gives a null within instrument sensitivity. This compares accessible surface responses rather than assuming a perfectly spherical enclosure. https://www.feynmanlectures.caltech.edu/II_05.html#Ch5-S8[Feynman, Leighton and Sands, II.5.8: inner and outer sampling of a charged conductor]{ref}. Useful additional controls are an uncharged enclosure baseline, a repeat exterior sample to verify sensitivity, and repeated trials at different depths. An access hole, charged handle, leakage or disturbed <surface charge density> limits the inference; a null bounds detectable transfer, rather than proving an exactly zero <electric field>. An open pail's depth plateau tests good enclosure empirically. Neither demonstration establishes shielding of arbitrary time-dependent <electromagnetic fields>.

= Cavity in a conductor
{parent=Conductor in electrostatic equilibrium}

A <cavity in a conductor> is a void surrounded by an <electrical conductor>; **completely enclosed** is the essential qualification in exact <electrostatic shielding> arguments. At <electrostatic equilibrium>, the surrounding connected metal is an <equipotential>. An empty closed <cavity in a conductor> has constant <electric potential> and zero <electric field>, even if the metal is isolated and charged. An occupied closed <cavity in a conductor> generally has a nonzero <electric field>; its inner-wall total <electric charge> cancels the total <electric charge> of the internal sources. These are distinct statements, illustrated by <Faraday's ice pail experiment>.<Intermediate College Physics by Richard Fitzpatrick/Ideal Conductors>[Richard Fitzpatrick, Ideal Conductors, equations 2.127 and the following uniqueness argument: a charge-free enclosed cavity]{ref}

= Electrostatic shielding
{parent=Conductor in electrostatic equilibrium}

<Electrostatic shielding> decouples interior and exterior <electric fields> using a <conductor in electrostatic equilibrium>. Exterior sources cannot produce an <electric field> in an empty closed <cavity in a conductor>, whether grounded or isolated. Outward independence requires grounding: internal sources can affect an isolated enclosure's exterior, as in <Faraday's ice pail experiment>. Grounding makes the exterior solution independent of internal sources, while allowing exterior sources' own response. Openings and changing <electromagnetic fields> need additional analysis.<The Feynman Lectures on Physics, Volume II by Richard Feynman and others>[II.5, §5–10, concluding paragraphs on inward shielding and closed grounded enclosures]{ref}

= Electrostatic boundary conditions at a conductor
{parent=Conductor in electrostatic equilibrium}

= Electrostatic boundary condition
{synonym}

The <electrostatic boundary conditions at a conductor> relate the one-sided total <electric fields> at a smooth metal–<vacuum> interface. Choose a <unit normal> $\mathbf n$ **from metal into the adjacent vacuum**, let $\mathbf E_{\rm metal}$ and $\mathbf E_{\rm vac}$ be the two limiting <electric fields>, and let $\sigma$ be the signed <surface charge density>. A thin <Gaussian pillbox> with cap <area> $A$ has outward cap normals $+\mathbf n$ on the <vacuum> side and $-\mathbf n$ on the metal side. As its height tends to zero, its side <electric flux> tends to zero for bounded local <electric fields>, leaving
$$
A\mathbf E_{\rm vac}\cdot\mathbf n-A\mathbf E_{\rm metal}\cdot\mathbf n=\frac{\sigma A}{\epsilon_0},\qquad \mathbf n\cdot(\mathbf E_{\rm vac}-\mathbf E_{\rm metal})=\frac{\sigma}{\epsilon_0}.
$$
The minus sign comes from the second outward cap normal, not from the sign of the <electric charge>. A shrinking rectangular loop across the interface and the zero closed <line integral> in <electrostatics> give continuity of the <tangential component>:
$$
\mathbf n\times(\mathbf E_{\rm vac}-\mathbf E_{\rm metal})=\mathbf0.
$$
For a <conductor in electrostatic equilibrium>, $\mathbf E_{\rm metal}=\mathbf0$, hence
$$
\boxed{\mathbf E_{\rm vac}=\frac{\sigma}{\epsilon_0}\mathbf n,\qquad \Phi|_S=V_c,\qquad \sigma=-\epsilon_0\left.\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial n}\right|_{\rm vac}.}
$$
