Thus positive <surface charge density> gives an <electric field> away from metal and negative <surface charge density> gives an <electric field> toward metal. The derivative uses the same chosen <unit normal>, from the <vacuum> side. The ideal surface layer has no unique <electric field> value on the layer itself; these are limiting values. Ordinary surface <electric charge> preserves continuity of <electric potential>; an ideal <electric dipole> sheet would require a different model. https://farside.ph.utexas.edu/teaching/em/lectures/node70.html[Richard Fitzpatrick, Boundary conditions for E and D: pillbox and loop conditions]{ref}.

Here “exterior” means just outside the **metal**, including the <vacuum> within a <cavity in a conductor>. On a spherical inner wall of radius $a$, its <unit normal> from metal into the <cavity in a conductor> is $-\widehat{\mathbf r}$. A central positive <point charge> $q$ gives $\mathbf E_{\rm vac}=q\widehat{\mathbf r}/(4\pi\epsilon_0a^2)$ there, so
$$
\sigma_{\rm inner}=-\frac{q}{4\pi a^2},\qquad \int_{S_{\rm inner}}\sigma_{\rm inner}\,dA=-q.
$$
The outward normal of the <cavity in a conductor> as a volume is instead $+\widehat{\mathbf r}$; confusing these two <unit normals> reverses the inferred sign. For a stipulated spherical enclosure with inner radius $a$, outer radius $b$, no external sources and total isolated metal <electric charge> $Q_c$, <spherical symmetry> and <Gauss's law> give the analytical model
$$
E_r(r)=\begin{cases}
q/(4\pi\epsilon_0r^2),&0<r<a,\\
0,&a<r<b,\\
(Q_c+q)/(4\pi\epsilon_0r^2),&r>b.
\end{cases}
$$
Its outer <surface charge density> is $(Q_c+q)/(4\pi b^2)$. Grounding to $V_c=0$ with zero <electric potential> at infinity instead gives $Q_c=-q$ and zero exterior <electric field>, while preserving the same nonzero <electric field> inside the charged <cavity in a conductor>.

\Image[electrostatic-conductor-equilibrium-pillbox-cavity.png]
{title=Signed <Gaussian pillbox> accounting and an analytical charged <cavity in a conductor>}
{description=Original schematic and analytical model, not measured data. Left: the two cap normals give the normal <electric field> jump at a positive metal–<vacuum> surface. Right: a central positive <point charge> in a spherical enclosure with $b=2a$; solid blue is an isolated initially neutral enclosure, and dashed orange is a grounded enclosure. Both have zero <electric field> in the metal and the same nonzero <electric field> in the <cavity in a conductor>. The jumps show one-sided limits, with the point-source singularity excluded.}

As a numerical sign check, stipulate $q=1\,\mathrm{nC}$, $a=0.10\,\mathrm m$, $b=0.20\,\mathrm m$ and $1/(4\pi\epsilon_0)\simeq8.99\times10^9\,\mathrm{N\,m^2/C^2}$. The inner <electric field> is approximately $899\,\mathrm{N/C}$ along $+\widehat{\mathbf r}$, but $\mathbf E_{\rm vac}\cdot\mathbf n=-899\,\mathrm{N/C}$ and $\sigma_{\rm inner}\simeq-7.96\,\mathrm{nC/m^2}$. An isolated neutral enclosure has $+1\,\mathrm{nC}$ on the outer wall, approximately $+1.99\,\mathrm{nC/m^2}$ and $225\,\mathrm{N/C}$ just outside. Multiplying each <surface charge density> by its spherical <surface area> recovers the signed wall <electric charges>.

The local result uses the total <electric field>, including all induced and imposed sources. The factor $\sigma/\epsilon_0$ follows from the zero metal-side <electric field>; a symmetric isolated sheet in <vacuum> instead has magnitude $|\sigma|/(2\epsilon_0)$ on each side. https://www.feynmanlectures.caltech.edu/II_05.html#Ch5-S9[Feynman, Leighton and Sands, II.5.9: why a conductor differs from an isolated charge sheet]{ref}. At sharp corners, limiting <electric fields> can be singular and the smooth local pillbox argument needs care. In material outside the metal, use the normal free-charge jump of the <electric displacement field> and the material's <permittivity>, as in <electrostatic interface boundary conditions>. https://farside.ph.utexas.edu/teaching/em/lectures/node70.html[Richard Fitzpatrick, Boundary conditions for E and D: free and bound surface charge]{ref}.

= Surface charge density
{title2=$\sigma$}
{parent=Electrostatic boundary conditions at a conductor}
{wiki}

Surface charge density is electric charge per unit area. At a conductor in electrostatic equilibrium it equals $\epsilon_0\mathbf E_{\rm out}\mathbin{\cdot}\mathbf n$ for the normal pointing out of the conductor.

= Charge sharing between distant connected spheres
{parent=Conductor in electrostatic equilibrium}

Two widely separated conducting spheres of radii $R_1,R_2$ connected by a thin wire have equal potentials, so a total charge $Q$ divides as
$$
Q_i=\frac{R_i}{R_1+R_2}Q.
$$

= Charge on connected concentric spherical shells
{parent=Conductor in electrostatic equilibrium}

Two connected concentric spherical shells form one equipotential conductor. In the absence of charge in the inner cavity, all net charge lies on the exterior surface of the outer shell.

= Induced charge on a conducting sphere in a uniform electric field
{parent=Conductor in electrostatic equilibrium}

A neutral conducting sphere of radius $R$ in the field $E\widehat z$ has exterior potential and induced surface charge
$$
\Phi=-E\left(r-\frac{R^3}{r^2}\right)\cos\theta,
\qquad
\sigma=3\epsilon_0E\cos\theta.
$$
The surface integral of $\sigma$ is zero.

= Electrostatic energy
{parent=Electrostatics}
{wiki}

Electrostatic energy can be written as one half the charge-potential integral or epsilon-zero over two times the electric-field energy integral.

= Electrostatic energy of a three-plate capacitor
{parent=Electrostatic energy}
{title2=$W=\frac{\epsilon_0AV^2H}{H^2-a^2}$}

In the parallel-plate approximation, a central plate at potential $V$ between grounded plates at $z=\pm H$ forms two parallel capacitors of gaps $H+a$ and $H-a$. Summing their field energies gives the displayed formula. At fixed voltage it is minimized at $a=0$. Finite circular plates have fringe fields; the uniform-gap expression assumes those edge corrections are neglected.

= Electrostatic energy of a uniformly charged solid sphere
{parent=Electrostatic energy}
{title2=$U=\frac{3Q^2}{20\pi\epsilon_0R}$}

A solid sphere of radius $R$ and uniform <charge density> has total <electric charge> $Q$. <Gauss law> gives the radial <electric field> $E_r=Qr/(4\pi\epsilon_0R^3)$ inside and $E_r=Q/(4\pi\epsilon_0r^2)$ outside. The <electric potential> vanishing at infinity is $Q(3-r^2/R^2)/(8\pi\epsilon_0R)$ inside and $Q/(4\pi\epsilon_0r)$ outside. Integrating the <electrostatic energy> density $\epsilon_0E^2/2$ over both regions gives $U=3Q^2/(20\pi\epsilon_0R)$. In a fixed-density family with $Q\propto Z$ and $R^3\propto Z$, the <electrostatic energy> scales as $Z^{5/3}$.

= Electrostatic energy of alternating-charge concentric shells
{parent=Electrostatic energy}
{title2=$W=\frac{q^2}{8\pi\epsilon_0r_1}$}

A first spherical shell with charge $q$ followed by shells of charges $2(-1)^{n+1}q$ leaves enclosed charge $(-1)^{n+1}q$ after each shell. <Gauss's law> therefore gives the same squared <electric field> $q^2/(16\pi^2\epsilon_0^2r^4)$ in every gap outside the first radius. Integrating the <electrostatic energy> density over all space gives $W=q^2/(8\pi\epsilon_0r_1)$, independent of the other shell radii.

= Electrostatic energy of a uniformly charged spherical shell
{parent=Electrostatic energy}

A thin spherical shell of radius $a$ carrying total charge $Q$ has surface potential
$$
\Phi(a)=\frac{Q}{4\pi\epsilon_0a}
$$
and electrostatic self-energy
$$
U=\frac12Q\Phi(a)=\frac{Q^2}{8\pi\epsilon_0a}.
$$

= Electrostatic potential energy of point charges
{parent=Electrostatic energy}

For distinct <point charges> $q_i$ at positions $r_i$, the electrostatic <potential energy> is
$$
U=\frac1{4\pi\epsilon_0}\sum_{i<j}\frac{q_iq_j}{|r_i-r_j|}.
$$
When some charges are fixed, the terms depending on a movable charge form its potential energy in their <electric potential>.

= Coaxial cylindrical capacitor
{parent=Electrostatic energy}

For coaxial cylinders of radii $a<b$ and length $L\gg b$, carrying charges per unit length $\pm\lambda$,
$$
E(r)=\frac{\lambda}{2\pi\epsilon_0r}\widehat r,
\qquad
V=\frac{\lambda}{2\pi\epsilon_0}\log\frac ba,
\qquad
C=\frac{2\pi\epsilon_0L}{\log(b/a)}.
$$
The field energy is $U=\lambda^2L\log(b/a)/(4\pi\epsilon_0)=QV/2$.

= Coaxial capacitor with grounded inner and outer cylinders
{parent=Coaxial cylindrical capacitor}
{title2=$C'=2\pi\epsilon_0(1/\log\lambda+1/\log(2/\lambda))$}

A driven cylindrical conductor between two grounded coaxial conductors forms two <coaxial cylindrical capacitors> in parallel. For radii $a,\lambda a,2a$, the <capacitance per unit length> is the sum $C'=2\pi\epsilon_0[1/\log\lambda+1/\log(2/\lambda)]$. Writing $u=\log\lambda$ and $L=\log2$, the function $1/u+1/(L-u)$ is strictly <convex> on $(0,L)$ and symmetric about $L/2$. Hence $C'$ has its unique minimum at $\lambda=\sqrt2$, with value $8\pi\epsilon_0/\log2$. As $\lambda=1+\delta$ approaches the inner conductor, $C'\sim2\pi\epsilon_0/\delta$, agreeing with the local <parallel-plate capacitor> approximation.

= Electric dipole moment
{parent=Electrostatics}
{wiki}

The <electric dipole moment> measures the first spatial moment of signed <electric charge>. For localized <point charges> $q_i$ at positions $\mathbf r_i$, or an integrable <charge density> $\rho(\mathbf r)$ with a finite first moment, define
$$
\mathbf p=\sum_i q_i\mathbf r_i,
\qquad
\mathbf p=\int\mathbf r\rho(\mathbf r)\,d^3r,
\qquad
Q=\sum_iq_i=\int\rho(\mathbf r)\,d^3r.
$$
https://farside.ph.utexas.edu/teaching/jk1/lectures/node20.html[Richard Fitzpatrick, Electric Dipoles: continuous definition]{ref}.

The <electric dipole moment> is a <vector> with <SI> units $\mathrm{C\,m}$: <coulombs> times <length> in metres. Opposite <electric charges> can cancel in $Q$ while contributing in the same direction to $\mathbf p$. For a neutral pair with positive <electric charge> $q>0$ at $\mathbf r_+$ and negative <electric charge> $-q$ at $\mathbf r_-$,
$$
\mathbf d=\mathbf r_+-\mathbf r_-,\qquad \mathbf p=q\mathbf d.
$$
Thus the <electric dipole moment> points from negative to positive <electric charge>, not along the attraction between the two <point charges>. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-2/pages/5-7-electric-dipoles[Ling, Moebs and Sanny, University Physics 2, 5.7: charge-pair geometry and dipole moment]{ref}.

Changing the coordinate origin changes the position vectors, not the physical <electric charge> distribution. If the new origin is at $\mathbf a$ relative to the old origin, $\mathbf r_i'=\mathbf r_i-\mathbf a$, so direct substitution gives
$$
\mathbf p'=\sum_iq_i(\mathbf r_i-\mathbf a)=\mathbf p-Q\mathbf a.
$$
Consequently <charge neutrality> makes the <electric dipole moment> independent of the origin. For a system with nonzero total <electric charge>, always specify the origin: a single <point charge> at the old origin has $\mathbf p=0$ but $\mathbf p'=-Q\mathbf a$. This is coordinate bookkeeping, not a newly created <electric dipole>. The full <electric potential> stays the same at the same physical point; the terms of its <electric multipole expansion> and the observation coordinates change together.

As a stipulated numerical example, put $+2\,\mathrm{nC}$ at $(1,0,0)\,\mathrm{mm}$ and $-2\,\mathrm{nC}$ at $(-1,0,0)\,\mathrm{mm}$. Their total <electric charge> is zero and $\mathbf p=(4\times10^{-12},0,0)\,\mathrm{C\,m}$. Translating the origin by $(3,-2,0)\,\mathrm{mm}$ leaves this <electric dipole moment> unchanged. Adding $+1\,\mathrm{nC}$ at the old origin instead gives $Q=1\,\mathrm{nC}$ and $\mathbf p'=(1,2,0)\times10^{-12}\,\mathrm{C\,m}$. Distinguishing these two cases prevents the mistaken claim that every <electric dipole moment> is independent of the origin.

The usefulness of the <electric dipole moment> is compression: many internal <electric charges> determine one <vector> governing the leading distant <electric potential> of a neutral localized object when $\mathbf p\ne0$. https://www.feynmanlectures.caltech.edu/II_06.html#Ch6-S2[Feynman, Leighton and Sands, II.6.2: distant dipole fields]{ref}. A <polar molecule> need not literally contain two <point charges>; its <atomic nuclei> and <electron density> collectively give an <electric dipole moment>. For example, the <chemical polarity> of <water> reflects its <bent molecular geometry>, whereas the symmetric linear <molecular geometry> of <carbon dioxide> makes its permanent <electric dipole moment> vanish. https://openstax.org/books/chemistry-2e/pages/7-6-molecular-structure-and-polarity[Flowers, Theopold, Langley and Robinson, Chemistry 2e, 7.6: molecular geometry and polarity]{ref}. A collection of such <electric dipole moments> per unit volume gives <electric polarization>. https://farside.ph.utexas.edu/teaching/jk1/Electromagnetism/node40.html[Richard Fitzpatrick, Polarization: dipole moment per unit volume]{ref}.

= Time derivative of the electric dipole moment
{parent=Electric dipole moment}

For a localized charge and current distribution satisfying <charge conservation from Maxwell equations>,
$$
\dot{\mathbf p}=\int\mathbf J(x,t)\,d^3x.
$$

= Electric dipole
{parent=Electric dipole moment}
{wiki}

A finite <electric dipole> consists of equal and opposite <electric charges> separated by a nonzero <displacement>. It has <charge neutrality> but a nonzero <electric dipole moment> $\mathbf p=q\mathbf d$. An ideal point <electric dipole> is the mathematical limit $d=|\mathbf d|\to0$ with $\mathbf p$ fixed, so $q$ must increase as $1/d$. Shrinking the separation while holding $q$ fixed instead makes the <electric dipole moment> vanish. The ideal <electric dipole> describes an exterior <electric field>; it does not reproduce the two near-charge singularities or the internal structure of a <molecule>. https://farside.ph.utexas.edu/teaching/jk1/lectures/node20.html[Richard Fitzpatrick, Electric Dipoles: the fixed-moment point limit]{ref}.

Place the pair's midpoint at the origin and write $k_e=1/(4\pi\epsilon_0)$, where $\epsilon_0$ is <vacuum permittivity>. By <superposition principle> and <Coulomb's law>, its exact <electric potential> away from the two <point charges> is
$$
\Phi_{\rm pair}(\mathbf r)=k_eq\left(\frac1{|\mathbf r-\mathbf d/2|}-\frac1{|\mathbf r+\mathbf d/2|}\right).
$$
For $r=|\mathbf r|\gg d$, retain the first nonzero term of a <Taylor series>. The <electric potential> and its negative <gradient> become
$$
\Phi_{\rm dip}(\mathbf r)=k_e\frac{\mathbf p\cdot\widehat{\mathbf r}}{r^2},
\qquad
\mathbf E_{\rm dip}(\mathbf r)=\frac{k_e}{r^3}\left[3(\mathbf p\cdot\widehat{\mathbf r})\widehat{\mathbf r}-\mathbf p\right],\qquad r>0.
$$
Here $\widehat{\mathbf r}=\mathbf r/r$ and the products in parentheses are <dot products>. These are exact exterior formulas for the ideal <electric dipole> and distant approximations for a finite pair. The <electric field> on the outer axis points along $\mathbf p$ with magnitude $2k_ep/r^3$; on the perpendicular bisector it points opposite $\mathbf p$ with magnitude $k_ep/r^3$. This directional structure is what a scalar total <electric charge> misses. https://www.feynmanlectures.caltech.edu/II_06.html#Ch6-S2[Feynman, Leighton and Sands, II.6.2: exact pair potential and dipole expansion]{ref}.

In a prescribed uniform external <electric field> $\mathbf E_{\rm ext}$, a rigid pair has opposite <forces> $\mathbf F_\pm=\pm q\mathbf E_{\rm ext}$. Its net <force> is zero, but summing their moments about the midpoint gives the <torque>
$$
\boldsymbol\tau=\frac{\mathbf d}{2}\times q\mathbf E_{\rm ext}+\left(-\frac{\mathbf d}{2}\right)\times\left(-q\mathbf E_{\rm ext}\right)=\mathbf p\times\mathbf E_{\rm ext}.
$$
The <cross product> makes the <torque> perpendicular to both the <electric dipole moment> and the external <electric field>. If $\theta$ is the angle from $\mathbf E_{\rm ext}$ to $\mathbf p$ in their plane, the signed turning <torque> is $\tau_\theta=-pE_{\rm ext}\sin\theta$. It tends to reduce a small positive $\theta$. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-2/pages/5-7-electric-dipoles[Ling, Moebs and Sanny, University Physics 2, 5.7: forces and torque on a dipole]{ref}.

The external interaction <potential energy>, with zero chosen at $\theta=\pi/2$, is
$$
U=-\mathbf p\cdot\mathbf E_{\rm ext}=-pE_{\rm ext}\cos\theta,\qquad \tau_\theta=-\frac{dU}{d\theta}.
$$
Parallel alignment minimizes this <potential energy>; antiparallel alignment maximizes it even though the <torque> is zero in both positions. This omits the pair's constant internal binding <potential energy> and assumes a fixed permanent <electric dipole moment>. https://farside.ph.utexas.edu/teaching/jk1/Electromagnetism.pdf[Richard Fitzpatrick, Classical Electromagnetism, exercise 2.10: dipole torque and energy]{ref}.

\Image[electric-dipoles-pair-torque.png]
{title=Neutral charge pair and orientation energy in a uniform electric field}
{description=Original analytical illustration for a rigid <electric dipole>. Equal opposite <forces> cancel in translation but give a clockwise <torque> at the depicted positive angle. The <potential energy> curve is the dimensionless model $U/(pE_{\rm ext})=-\cos\theta$, not measured data.}

A nonuniform external <electric field> can exert a net <force> on a neutral <electric dipole>. Before taking the small-pair approximation, the exact external <force> is $\mathbf F=q[\mathbf E_{\rm ext}(\mathbf R+\mathbf d/2)-\mathbf E_{\rm ext}(\mathbf R-\mathbf d/2)]$, where $\mathbf R$ is the midpoint. A first-order <Taylor series> gives, for a fixed <electric dipole moment> and an external <electric field> varying slowly across $d$,
$$
\mathbf F\simeq(\mathbf p\cdot\nabla)\mathbf E_{\rm ext}=\nabla(\mathbf p\cdot\mathbf E_{\rm ext})
$$
in <electrostatics>. The last equality uses the vanishing <curl> of the external <electric field>, with $\mathbf p$ held fixed while differentiating. Thus uniform external <electric fields> turn a misaligned <electric dipole>, whereas gradients can also move its midpoint. For example, the local source-free <electric field> $\mathbf E_{\rm ext}=Gx\widehat{\mathbf x}+(E_0-Gy)\widehat{\mathbf y}$ gives $\mathbf F=(Gp_x,-Gp_y,0)$, so reversing the <electric dipole moment> reverses the net <force>. This stipulated local model has zero <divergence> and zero <curl>.

A motivating experiment is Filsinger and colleagues' study of electrostatic deflection of <polar molecules>. Benzonitrile ($\mathrm{C_7H_5N}$) in <helium> expanded from a pulsed valve into <vacuum>; two 1-mm skimmers narrowed the beam before a 15-cm deflector. A grounded trough and a high-voltage rod produced a vertical <electric field> gradient. Scanning a focused <laser> vertically downstream and recording benzonitrile ions from <photoionization> measured the beam's relative density. With voltage off, the profile spanned about 2 mm; applying voltage shifted and broadened it upwards. At 10 kV, many <molecules> lay outside the original profile. https://pure.mpg.de/pubman/item/item_736019_2/component/file_736018/433451%28filsinger%29.pdf[Filsinger et al., Quantum state selection, alignment, and orientation of large molecules using static electric and laser fields, sections II and III A, figure 3]{ref}.

Interpretation uses a <quantum state>'s <Stark effect> <energy> $W(E)$: $\mu_{\rm eff}=-dW/dE$ gives $F_y=\mu_{\rm eff}\,\partial_yE$. States whose <energy> falls with increasing <electric field> seek stronger fields. The voltage-off profile controls the mechanical aperture. Inferring state populations needs calculated trajectories, <speed> distributions and a rotational <temperature> model; profile displacement alone does not measure a permanent <electric dipole moment> uniquely. This is an electric <Stark effect> experiment, with <photoionization> occurring after deflection. https://pure.mpg.de/pubman/item/item_736019_2/component/file_736018/433451%28filsinger%29.pdf[Filsinger et al., section III A: Stark slopes, trajectory modelling and state selection]{ref}.

For the symmetric finite pair, the first omitted <electric multipole expansion> term after the <electric dipole> is third order, so typical relative errors scale as $(d/r)^2$. This stronger error estimate need not hold for an arbitrary neutral <molecule> with a nonzero <electric quadrupole moment>. A concrete check for a pair on the $z$ axis compares the exact and ideal <electric fields> at $r>d/2$ on the positive axis and at $r>0$ on the perpendicular bisector:
$$
\frac{E_{z,\rm pair}(0,0,r)}{E_{z,\rm dip}(0,0,r)}=\left(1-\frac{d^2}{4r^2}\right)^{-2},
\qquad
\frac{E_{z,\rm pair}(r,0,0)}{E_{z,\rm dip}(r,0,0)}=\left(1+\frac{d^2}{4r^2}\right)^{-3/2}.
$$
Both perpendicular-bisector components are negative. At $r=5d$, the ratios are approximately $1.0203$ and $0.9852$: errors of $+2.03\%$ and $-1.48\%$ relative to the ideal <electric field>. The condition $r\gg d$ concerns observing the pair's own <electric field>; the separate condition for its external <force> approximation concerns variation of the applied <electric field> across the pair.

\Image[electric-dipoles-finite-field.png]
{title=Finite charge pair approaching its ideal electric dipole field}
{description=Original geometry and analytical field-ratio curves for two <point charges> $\pm q$ separated by $d$. The outer axial <electric field> points along the <electric dipole moment>; the perpendicular-bisector <electric field> points against it. The curves compare exact <Coulomb's law> sums with the ideal <electric dipole> prediction and are models, not experimental measurements.}

= Electric dipole-dipole interaction
{parent=Electric dipole}

Two point dipoles separated by $\mathbf r$ have interaction proportional to
$$
\frac{\mathbf p_1\cdot\mathbf p_2}{r^3}
-\frac{3(\mathbf p_1\cdot\mathbf r)(\mathbf p_2\cdot\mathbf r)}{r^5}.
$$

The formula combines the source <electric dipole> field with the second dipole's coupling to that field.

= Electric multipole expansion
{parent=Electrostatics}

= Multipole expansion
{synonym}

= Electrostatic multipole expansion
{synonym}

For a localized charge distribution, the far <electric potential> is an inverse-radius expansion whose first terms are the total charge monopole, the electric dipole moment, and the electric quadrupole moment.

= Electric quadrupole moment
{parent=Electric multipole expansion}
{title2=$Q_{ij}=\sum_a q_a(3r_{a,i}r_{a,j}-|r_a|^2\delta_{ij})$}

The trace-free <electric quadrupole moment> is a symmetric tensor measuring the second-order term in an <electric multipole expansion>. For a localized charge distribution its contribution to the far-field <electrostatic potential> is $Q_{ij}\widehat r_i\widehat r_j/(8\pi\epsilon_0r^3)$, with summation over indices. The moment may be nonzero even when the total <electric charge> or <electric dipole moment> is nonzero; it should not be confused with an <electric quadrupole> whose lower moments vanish. Changing the origin generally changes the tensor when lower moments are nonzero.

= Electric quadrupole
{parent=Electric multipole expansion}
{wiki}

An electric quadrupole is a charge distribution whose total charge and <electric dipole moment> vanish while its second charge moment does not. Its potential decays as $r^{-3}$.

= Multipole expansion of three collinear charges
{parent=Electric multipole expansion}

For charges $-Q,NQ,-MQ$ at $z=d,0,-d$, respectively,
$$
\Phi(r,\theta)=\frac{Q}{4\pi\epsilon_0}\left[
\frac{N-M-1}{r}+\frac{(M-1)d\cos\theta}{r^2}
-\frac{(M+1)d^2P_2(\cos\theta)}{r^3}+O(r^{-4})\right].
$$
The monopole vanishes when $N=M+1$, the dipole vanishes when $M=1$, and both vanish when $(N,M)=(2,1)$.

= Dipole and quadrupole scaling limits of three collinear charges
{parent=Multipole expansion of three collinear charges}

Subject to $N=M+1$, keeping $Qd$ fixed as $d\to0$ gives a finite point-dipole limit when $M\ne1$. Keeping $Qd^2$ fixed gives a finite pure-quadrupole limit only when $M=1$; otherwise the dipole diverges.

= Transverse force from a collinear electric quadrupole
{parent=Multipole expansion of three collinear charges}

For charges $-Q,2Q,-Q$ at $z=d,0,-d$, the exact force on a charge $-Q$ at $(x,0,0)$ is
$$
F=-\frac{Q^2x}{2\pi\epsilon_0}
\left(\frac1{|x|^3}-\frac1{(x^2+d^2)^{3/2}}\right)\widehat x.
$$
It points toward the origin for every nonzero $x$.

= Magnetostatics
{parent=Electromagnetism}
{wiki}

Magnetostatics studies time-independent magnetic fields and steady currents. In vacuum its field equations are $\nabla\mathbin\cdot\mathbf B=0$ and $\nabla\mathbin\times\mathbf B=\mu_0\mathbf J$.

= Magnetic scalar potential
{parent=Magnetostatics}
{title2=$\mathbf B=-\nabla\psi$}
{wiki}

In a simply connected current-free region of constant <magnetic permeability>, <Ampère's circuital law> makes the <magnetic field> curl-free, so it admits a scalar potential. The absence of magnetic monopoles then gives <Laplace's equation> for that potential. Some conventions define the potential for $\mathbf H$ instead of $\mathbf B$, differing by the constant permeability. Boundary conditions, including imposed fields at infinity, determine the potential up to an additive constant.

= Gravity-equivalent magnetic surface density
{parent=Magnetic scalar potential}
{title2=$\Sigma_M=-B_{z0}/\sqrt{\pi G\mu_0}$}

For an isolated razor-thin disk, rescale its exterior <magnetic scalar potential> to satisfy $\sqrt{4\pi G/\mu_0}\mathbf B=-\nabla\Phi_M$. Comparing the normal magnetic boundary derivative with $\partial_z\Phi=2\pi G\Sigma$ identifies the displayed equivalent signed <surface density>. Both exterior potentials satisfy <Laplace's equation>. The equivalent <surface density> is a representation of the magnetic boundary data, rather than physical negative mass.

= Maxwell equations
{parent=Electromagnetism}
{c}
{wiki}

= Maxwell's equations
{c}
{synonym}

The <Maxwell equations> are the field equations of classical <electromagnetism>. In <SI> units, in vacuum with prescribed <charge density> $\rho$ and <current density> $\mathbf J$, they are
$$
\begin{aligned}
\nabla\cdot\mathbf E&=\rho/\epsilon_0,
&\nabla\cdot\mathbf B&=0,\\
\nabla\times\mathbf E&=-\frac{\partial\mathbf B}{\partial t},
&\nabla\times\mathbf B&=\mu_0\mathbf J+\mu_0\epsilon_0\frac{\partial\mathbf E}{\partial t}.
\end{aligned}
$$
Here $\mathbf E$ is the <electric field> in volts per metre, $\mathbf B$ the <magnetic field> in teslas, $\rho$ in coulombs per cubic metre, and $\mathbf J$ in amperes per square metre. The constants are <vacuum permittivity> $\epsilon_0$ and the vacuum value $\mu_0$ of <magnetic permeability>. “Vacuum” specifies the field laws without a material response; “source-free” additionally means $\rho=0$ and $\mathbf J=0$. Matter described by averaged fields instead requires <Maxwell equations in matter> and <constitutive relations>.

The first equation, <Gauss's law>, makes <electric charge> a source of <electric flux>. <Gauss's law for magnetism> sets the net <magnetic flux> through a closed surface to zero. The two <curl> equations, <Faraday's law> and the <Ampère-Maxwell equation>, couple changes of the <electric field> and <magnetic field>. Appropriate <initial conditions> and <boundary conditions> select a physical solution; the two <divergence> equations constrain the initial fields. https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/8-02-physics-ii-electricity-and-magnetism-spring-2007/2d2f227e6fd26ee25f366759e02f03dc_chapte13em_waves.pdf[MIT 8.02 notes, §13.3: Maxwell's equations]{ref}.

= Electromagnetic duality
{parent=Maxwell equations}
{wiki}

Vacuum <Maxwell equations> without sources are invariant under $E'=E\cos\alpha+cB\sin\alpha$, $cB'=cB\cos\alpha-E\sin\alpha$. At $\alpha=\pi/2$ this is $E'=cB$, $B'=-E/c$. Electric-conductor boundary conditions are not invariant under arbitrary duality rotations; the transformed fields must be tested separately against the wall conditions.

= Derivative-order ambiguity in covariant Maxwell potential equations
{parent=Maxwell equations}
{title2=$\nabla_b\nabla^a A^b-\nabla^a\nabla_bA^b=-C^a{}_bA^b$}

Use the negative curvature convention defined in <curvature-sign convention in Killing derivative identities>, and $C_{ab}=C^c{}_{acb}$. Covariant derivatives of a vector obey $[\nabla_b,\nabla^a]A^b=-C^a{}_bA^b$. Hence the flat-space equivalent expressions $\Box A^a-\partial_b\partial^aA^b$ and $\Box A^a-\partial^a\partial_bA^b$ acquire a difference $+C^a{}_bA^b$ under the <comma-to-semicolon prescription>. Covariantizing $F_{ab}=2\nabla_{[a}A_{b]}$ and its <Maxwell equations> gives $\Box A^a-\nabla^a(\nabla_bA^b)+C^a{}_bA^b=\mu_0J^a$. This form respects <gauge invariance>. Dropping the Ricci term is generally not gauge invariant. The two expressions coincide in a Ricci-flat <vacuum spacetime>; vanishing matter alone with nonzero <cosmological constant> does not imply Ricci flatness.

= Magnetic Galilean limit of the pre-Maxwell equations
{parent=Maxwell equations}

The magnetic nonrelativistic limit neglects displacement current and uses $\partial_t\mathbf B=-\nabla\times\mathbf E$, $\nabla\times\mathbf B=\mu_0\mathbf J$, $\nabla\cdot\mathbf B=0$. Under a <Galilean transformation> $\mathbf x'=\mathbf x-\mathbf vt$, $t'=t$, its consistent field transformations are $\mathbf B'=\mathbf B$, $\mathbf J'=\mathbf J$ and $\mathbf E'=\mathbf E+\mathbf v\times\mathbf B$. Indeed $\partial_{t'}=\partial_t+\mathbf v\cdot\nabla$ and $\nabla\times(\mathbf v\times\mathbf B)=-(\mathbf v\cdot\nabla)\mathbf B$, giving covariance of the induction equation. The full charge-current transformation has $\mathbf J'=\mathbf J-\rho_e\mathbf v$; neglecting its convective correction belongs to the magnetic quasineutral approximation, not exact relativistic electrodynamics. With $\mathbf u'=\mathbf u-\mathbf v$, the combination $\mathbf E+\mathbf u\times\mathbf B$ is invariant. Vanishing electric field in the fluid rest frame therefore gives the <ideal magnetohydrodynamic induction equation>.

= Gauss's law for magnetism
{c}
{parent=Maxwell equations}
{wiki}

Gauss's law for magnetism is $\nabla\mathbin\cdot\mathbf B=0$. Its integral form says that the net magnetic flux through every closed surface vanishes, so the normal magnetic field is continuous across an ordinary interface.

= Vacuum Maxwell equations
{c}
{parent=Maxwell equations}

For the electromagnetic two-form $F$, the source-free Maxwell equations are $dF=0$ and $d(*F)=0$. The first is the Bianchi identity and the second is the dynamical equation.

= Anti-self-dual Maxwell equations
{parent=Vacuum Maxwell equations}
{title2=$F_{AA'BB'}=\epsilon_{A'B'}\varphi_{AB},\quad \varphi_{AB}=\varphi_{BA}$}

= ASD Maxwell equations
{synonym}
{c}

In the <two-component spinor> convention with primed symmetric <gauge curvature> self-dual, an <Abelian> <Maxwell field> is anti-self-dual when its primed symmetric <gauge curvature> <two-component spinor> vanishes. For a locally exact field $F=dA$, the <Bianchi identity> gives $dF=0$; the duality relation then also gives $d{*}F=0$. Thus it obeys the source-free <Maxwell equations>. In complexified Lorentzian signature the <Hodge star> <eigenvalues> are $\pm i$; the <two-component spinor> condition specifies which component is meant without imposing a Euclidean eigenvalue on that signature.

// Destination: general-relativity.bigb

= Maxwell equations in matter
{c}
{parent=Maxwell equations}

In macroscopic matter with free charge density $\rho$ and free current density $J$,
$$
\nabla\cdot D=\rho,
\quad
\nabla\cdot B=0,
\quad
\nabla\times E=-\partial_tB,
\quad
\nabla\times H=J+\partial_tD.
$$
The electric displacement $D$ and magnetic field strength $H$ encode the material response.

These are the <Maxwell equations> after separating free and bound material sources.

= Constitutive relations
{parent=Maxwell equations in matter}

Constitutive relations specify how matter responds to fields. A homogeneous isotropic linear electromagnetic medium obeys $\mathbf D=\varepsilon\mathbf E$ and $\mathbf B=\mu\mathbf H$.

= Permeability
{title2=$\mu$}
{disambiguate=electromagnetism}
{parent=Constitutive relations}
{wiki}

= Magnetic permeability
{synonym}

Electromagnetic permeability relates magnetic flux density to magnetic field strength through $\mathbf B=\mu\mathbf H$ in an isotropic linear medium.

= Vacuum permeability
{parent=Permeability (electromagnetism)}
{title2=$\mu_0$}

Vacuum permeability is the <magnetic permeability> appearing in the vacuum <Maxwell equations>. In <SI>, it relates vacuum magnetic field strength to <magnetic flux density> through $\mathbf B=\mu_0\mathbf H$ and obeys $\mu_0\varepsilon_0c^2=1$, where $c$ is the <speed of light> and $\varepsilon_0$ the <vacuum permittivity>.

= Linear anisotropic dielectric
{parent=Maxwell equations in matter}

A linear anisotropic medium has constitutive relations $D_i=\varepsilon_{ij}E_j$ and $B_i=\mu_{ij}H_j$. Symmetric, time-independent tensors make the field-energy density a quadratic form.

Here <permittivity> is a tensor, so the electric displacement need not be parallel to the electric field.

= Birefringence
{parent=Linear anisotropic dielectric}
{title2=$\Delta n=n_e-n_o$}
{wiki}

<Birefringence> is the existence of different optical propagation constants for different eigenpolarizations in an anisotropic material. It provides the propagation-mode separation needed for <adiabatic optical following in a twisted nematic>.

Two different <refractive indices> can produce two displaced images, as in the <calcite double-image experiment>, or a <phase retardation> between overlapping components, as in a <waveplate>. Spatial separation concerns where <energy flux> travels; <phase retardation> concerns the relative <wave phase>. A specimen need not show two visibly separated images to have <birefringence>.

= Calcite double-image experiment
{parent=Birefringence}

Place a clear https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calcite[calcite] specimen on a transparency bearing printed words on an overhead projector. The projected readout is two copies of the lettering. Put a rotatable linear <polarizer> after the specimen: suitable orientations suppress one copy or the other. These are the actual apparatus and observations in the University of Iowa demonstration.<Polarization - Calcite & Iceland Spar by University of Iowa, Department of Physics and Astronomy>[Demonstration 6H35.15, Equipment and Procedure: crystals, polarizers, printed overhead transparency, two sets of words and selective extinction; final note on natural defects]{ref}

For a useful control, remove the specimen while keeping the transparency, projector and screen fixed: the doubling should disappear. Replace it, hold it stationary and rotate only the <polarizer>; complementary brightness changes distinguish two <linear polarizations> from an accidental duplicate transparency. These are suggested controls, rather than additional reported measurements. Ideally the two copies obey complementary <Malus's law> curves, $I_o(\alpha)=I_{o,\max}\cos^2\alpha$ and $I_e(\alpha)=I_{e,\max}\sin^2\alpha$, with <angle> $\alpha$ measured from the exiting <ordinary wave>'s <linear polarization>. Unequal maxima do not invalidate the test. Finite <polarizer> extinction, poor focus and specimen defects limit visibility; image separation alone does not determine either <refractive index> without the specimen geometry.

\Image[birefringent-polarization-ray-splitting.png]
{title=Separated <ordinary wave> and <extraordinary wave> paths carry different <linear polarizations>}
{description=An ideal parallel slab with a tilted <optic axis> illustrates <birefringent walk-off> at normal incidence. Both internal <wavevectors> are normal to the faces, but the <extraordinary wave>'s <Poynting vector> is tilted. The rays emerge parallel with a lateral displacement. The paired letters symbolize two image channels, not an optical prescription for the projector. The <polarizer> inset shows how an exiting channel is selected.}

= Uniaxial optical medium
{parent=Birefringence}
{title2=$\boldsymbol\varepsilon=\varepsilon_0\operatorname{diag}(n_o^2,n_o^2,n_e^2)$}

A transparent, nonmagnetic <linear anisotropic dielectric> is uniaxial when two principal <permittivities> agree. With the <optic axis> along $z$, its <permittivity> <tensor> has the displayed <diagonal matrix> form. Here $n_o$ and $n_e$ are principal <refractive indices> at a specified <frequency>; $n_e$ is not the <extraordinary wave>'s index in every direction.<Fundamentals of Photonics and Quantum Electronics lecture notes by Franz X. Kärtner>[§2.8.1, printed pp. 154–156, equations 2.406–2.413: uniaxial tensor and direction-dependent extraordinary index]{ref}

= Optic axis
{parent=Uniaxial optical medium}

The symmetry direction of a <uniaxial optical medium>. For a <wavevector> along it, the two <refractive indices> coincide: there is no <birefringence> for that propagation direction, even though $n_e\ne n_o$.

= Ordinary wave
{parent=Uniaxial optical medium}
{title2=$n_o$}

The <ordinary wave>'s <electric field> is perpendicular to the plane containing the <wavevector> and <optic axis>. Its <refractive index> $n_o$ is direction independent at fixed <frequency>.

= Extraordinary wave
{parent=Uniaxial optical medium}
{title2=$n_{\mathrm{ex}}(\theta)$}

The <extraordinary wave>'s <electric displacement field> lies in that plane and is perpendicular to the <wavevector>; its <electric field> need not be. For <angle> $\theta$ between the <wavevector> and <optic axis>,
$$
\frac{1}{n_{\mathrm{ex}}(\theta)^2}=\frac{\cos^2\theta}{n_o^2}+\frac{\sin^2\theta}{n_e^2}.
$$
Thus $n_{\mathrm{ex}}(0)=n_o$ and $n_{\mathrm{ex}}(\pi/2)=n_e$.<Fundamentals of Photonics and Quantum Electronics lecture notes by Franz X. Kärtner>[§§2.8.2–2.8.3, printed p. 156, equations 2.410–2.413 and longitudinal electric-field discussion]{ref}

A short derivation starts from the existing <plane wave in an anisotropic dielectric> equation. Put $\mathbf k=(k_x,0,k_z)$ and $k_0=\omega/c$, with <angular frequency> $\omega$ and vacuum <speed of light> $c$. The $y$ component gives the <ordinary wave> relation $k_x^2+k_z^2=n_o^2k_0^2$. The <determinant> of the $x,z$ system gives
$$
\frac{k_x^2}{n_e^2}+\frac{k_z^2}{n_o^2}=k_0^2.
$$
Substituting $k_x=n_{\mathrm{ex}}k_0\sin\theta$ and $k_z=n_{\mathrm{ex}}k_0\cos\theta$ yields the displayed directional <refractive index>.

= Birefringent walk-off
{parent=Extraordinary wave}
{title2=$\rho=\phi-\theta$}

The <extraordinary wave>'s <Poynting vector> can differ in direction from its <wavevector>. The latter specifies <wave phase> propagation, while the former specifies <energy flux>. This angular separation is walk-off.<Fundamentals of Photonics and Quantum Electronics lecture notes by Franz X. Kärtner>[§2.8.3, printed pp. 157–158, equations 2.414–2.419: Poynting direction and walk-off]{ref}

In the lossless, nondispersive model above, take the <gradient> of $\omega=c\sqrt{k_x^2/n_e^2+k_z^2/n_o^2}$ to obtain the <group velocity>, parallel to the <Poynting vector>. If its <angle> to the <optic axis> is $\phi$, this gives $\tan\phi=(n_o^2/n_e^2)\tan\theta$. Consequently the <extraordinary wave> generally carries <energy> away from its <wavevector> direction. An internal <wavevector> normal to a slab face therefore does not guarantee a normal energy path. <Birefringent walk-off> vanishes at $\theta=0$ and $\theta=\pi/2$; the latter direction still has unequal <refractive indices>. This is a useful <waveplate> geometry: overlapping components can acquire <phase retardation> without spatial splitting. A thin specimen, insufficient imaging resolution, or excitation of only one <linear polarization> can also hide a double image, for different reasons.

= Waveplate
{parent=Birefringence}
{wiki}

= Wave plate
{synonym}

A <waveplate> uses <birefringence> to change <polarization of an electromagnetic wave> through <phase retardation>. Its fast axis has the smaller <refractive index>, hence the larger <phase velocity>; its slow axis has the larger index. Consider normal incidence with the <optic axis> in the plate face, so both components follow the same straight path. A <linear polarization> at $45^\circ$ to these axes excites equal <wave amplitudes>.

A complementary experiment uses two sheet <polarizers> with a cellophane sheet between them. First cross the <polarizers> and remove the sheet: the field is dark. Insert and rotate the sheet about the beam: light returns at intermediate orientations, but extinction recurs at two perpendicular orientations. The sheet has changed the <polarization of an electromagnetic wave>, without requiring a resolved double image. With white <light>, different <wavelengths> can produce different transmitted colors.<The Feynman Lectures on Physics, Volume I by Richard Feynman and others>[I §33–3, Fig. 33–3 and the paragraphs describing removal, rotation and white-light colors of cellophane]{ref}. Brightness alone does not establish <circular polarization>: an ideal rotating linear <polarizer> also gives orientation-independent <light intensity> for <unpolarized light>.

= Phase retardation
{parent=Waveplate}
{title2=$\delta=2\pi\Delta n\,d/\lambda_0$}

For the overlapping <monochromatic wave> components, let $d$ be the thickness and $\lambda_0$ the vacuum <wavelength>. Define $\Delta n=n_s-n_f>0$ using the slow and fast <refractive indices>. With <complex amplitude> convention $e^{i(kz-\omega t)}$, the accumulated slow-minus-fast <phase difference> is
$$
\delta=(k_s-k_f)d=\frac{2\pi}{\lambda_0}(n_s-n_f)d.
$$
This is the <optical path difference> $\Delta n\,d$ expressed as a <wave phase>. It is a signed accumulation convention; with the opposite time convention the sign reverses. The outgoing <polarization of an electromagnetic wave> depends on $\delta$ modulo $2\pi$. Here the positive slow-minus-fast $\Delta n$ differs from the signed principal-index $n_e-n_o$ used in the parent <birefringence> entry when $n_e<n_o$.

For an original worked example, stipulate $\Delta n=0.0100$ at $\lambda_0=600\,\mathrm{nm}$ and a uniform thickness $d=15.0\,\mathrm{\mu m}$. These are design assumptions, not measured material data. The <optical path difference> is $150\,\mathrm{nm}$, so
$$
\delta=2\pi\frac{150\,\mathrm{nm}}{600\,\mathrm{nm}}=\frac{\pi}{2}.
$$
It is a <quarter-wave plate>. At the same <wavelength> and <refractive indices>, doubling $d$ to $30.0\,\mathrm{\mu m}$ gives $\delta=\pi$, a <half-wave plate>. A $1.0\,\mathrm{\mu m}$ thickness increase changes $\delta$ by $\pi/30$, or $6^\circ$; retaining the same <birefringence> at $500\,\mathrm{nm}$ would instead give $\delta=3\pi/5$ for the $15.0\,\mathrm{\mu m}$ plate. Real <refractive indices> also vary with <wavelength>, so this last calculation explicitly neglects <optical dispersion>.

\Image[birefringent-waveplate-phase-thickness.png]
{title=<Phase retardation> grows with thickness while overlapping components change <polarization of an electromagnetic wave>}
{description=The stipulated $\Delta n=0.0100$, $\lambda_0=600\,\mathrm{nm}$ model gives quarter-wave and half-wave thicknesses of $15$ and $30\,\mathrm{\mu m}$. Insets trace the normalized <electric field> at a fixed point for equal fast/slow <wave amplitudes>, with $E_f=\cos\tau$ and $E_s=\cos(\tau+\delta)$. The intermediate <ellipse>, circle and rotated line require no spatial beam separation.}

For these equal transmitted <wave amplitudes>, <orthogonal projection> onto a <polarizer> crossed with the initial $45^\circ$ <linear polarization> gives a simple independent readout. After removing a common <wave phase>, the component <complex amplitudes> are proportional to $(1,e^{i\delta})/\sqrt2$; <orthogonal projection> onto $(1,-1)/\sqrt2$ gives $(1-e^{i\delta})/2$. Squaring its <modulus> yields $I_\perp/I_{\mathrm{in}}=\sin^2(\delta/2)$: zero at $d=0$, one half at $15\,\mathrm{\mu m}$, and one at $30\,\mathrm{\mu m}$. This assumes ideal <polarizers>, negligible <specular reflection> and <wave absorption>, overlapping components and stable relative <wave phase>. It measures a periodic function of <phase retardation>, so one brightness measurement cannot determine the thickness uniquely.

= Quarter-wave plate
{parent=Waveplate}
{title2=$\delta=\pm\pi/2\pmod{2\pi}$}

A <quarter-wave plate> gives a quarter-cycle <phase retardation>. Equal transmitted <wave amplitudes> initially in <linear polarization> at $45^\circ$ become <circular polarization>; unequal <wave amplitudes> generally give <elliptical polarization>. Reversing the sign reverses the traversal of the same circle.

= Half-wave plate
{parent=Waveplate}
{title2=$\delta=\pi\pmod{2\pi}$}

A <half-wave plate> reverses the relative sign of the two components. For initial <linear polarization> at <angle> $\alpha$ to the fast axis, the outgoing line is at $-\alpha$ modulo $\pi$. The $45^\circ$ input therefore rotates by $90^\circ$.

= Ampère's circuital law
{parent=Maxwell equations}
{c}
{wiki}

= Ampère-Maxwell equation
{c}
{synonym}

= Ampere-Maxwell law
{c}
{synonym}

= Ampère's law
{c}
{synonym}

The Ampère-Maxwell equation
$$
\nabla\times B=\mu_0J+\mu_0\epsilon_0\frac{\partial E}{\partial t}
$$
relates magnetic circulation to conduction and displacement current.

= Amperian loop
{parent=Ampère's circuital law}
{c}
{wiki}

An Amperian loop is a closed curve used in the integral form of Ampère's circuital law or the Ampère-Maxwell equation.

= Magnetostatic Ampère law
{parent=Ampère's circuital law}
{title2=$\oint_C\mathbf B\cdot d\boldsymbol\ell=\mu_0I_{\mathrm{enc}}$}

In vacuum <magnetostatics>, the <Ampère-Maxwell equation> reduces to $\nabla\times\mathbf B=\mu_0\mathbf J$. Applying <Stokes' theorem> gives $\oint_C\mathbf B\cdot d\boldsymbol\ell=\mu_0I_{\mathrm{enc}}$, where $I_{\mathrm{enc}}$ is the <electric current> crossing an oriented spanning surface. Time-dependent <electric fields> require the additional <displacement current> term in the <Ampère-Maxwell equation>.

= Displacement current
{title2=$\mathbf J_D=\epsilon_0\partial_t\mathbf E$}
{parent=Ampère's circuital law}
{wiki}

In vacuum, <displacement current> is the term $\mathbf J_D=\epsilon_0\partial_t\mathbf E$ in the <Ampère-Maxwell equation>. It has the units of <current density>, but need not describe <electric charge> crossing the region. In <Maxwell equations in matter>, the corresponding term is $\partial_t\mathbf D$, with $\mathbf D$ the <electric displacement field>.

Consider a slowly charging <parallel-plate capacitor> with vacuum gap, plate area $A$, and charge $Q(t)$ on its positive plate. Neglect fringing and assume the charging timescale is long compared with the light-travel time across the apparatus. The nearly uniform gap <electric field> has magnitude $E=Q/(\epsilon_0A)$, so the <displacement current> through the gap is
$$
I_D=\epsilon_0 A\frac{dE}{dt}=\frac{dQ}{dt}=I.
$$
For $A=0.010\,\mathrm{m^2}$ and charging <electric current> $I=1.0\,\mathrm{mA}$, this gives $dE/dt\simeq1.13\times10^{10}\,\mathrm{V\,m^{-1}\,s^{-1}}$ and $J_D=0.10\,\mathrm{A/m^2}$. No conducting <electric current> crosses the gap. A spanning surface cutting the wire and another passing between the plates nevertheless give the same magnetic circulation when the full <Ampère-Maxwell equation> is used. https://www.feynmanlectures.caltech.edu/II_18.html#Ch18-S2[Feynman lectures, II.18.2: charging a capacitor]{ref}.

The local consistency check is short. Without <displacement current>, taking the <divergence> of $\nabla\times\mathbf B=\mu_0\mathbf J$ would force $\nabla\cdot\mathbf J=0$, allowing no local accumulation of <electric charge>. With <displacement current>, the <divergence of a curl is zero> and <Gauss's law> give
$$
0=\mu_0\left(\nabla\cdot\mathbf J+\epsilon_0\partial_t\nabla\cdot\mathbf E\right)
=\mu_0\left(\nabla\cdot\mathbf J+\partial_t\rho\right).
$$
This is precisely the <charge continuity equation>. <Electric charge conservation> fixes the required <divergence> of the added term; it does not by itself exclude other divergence-free additions. The specific <Maxwell equations> also yield the <electromagnetic wave equation>, linking this consistency correction to propagation at the <speed of light>.

= Faraday's law of induction
{parent=Maxwell equations}
{c}
{wiki}
{title2=$\nabla\times\mathbf E=-\partial_t\mathbf B$}

= Faraday's law
{c}
{synonym}

= Faraday law
{c}
{synonym}

<Faraday's law of induction> relates a changing <magnetic field> to circulation of the <electric field>. For a fixed <oriented surface> $S$ with boundary $C=\partial S$, choose the positive direction around $C$ by the <right-hand rule>: the fingers follow $C$ while the thumb follows the chosen <unit normal>. The fixed-loop electromagnetic <electromotive force> is
$$
\mathcal E=\oint_C\mathbf E\cdot d\mathbf l
=-\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\int_S\mathbf B\cdot d\mathbf S
=-\frac{d\Phi_B}{dt},
\qquad
\nabla\times\mathbf E=-\frac{\partial\mathbf B}{\partial t}.
$$
The final <derivative> of <magnetic flux> is an ordinary time derivative, with the <surface> held fixed. Reversing both the <unit normal> and the boundary direction reverses both signs. The loop may be an imagined path: the field law does not require a wire or an <electric current>. https://www.feynmanlectures.caltech.edu/II_17.html#Ch17-S1[Feynman lectures, II.17.1: electric-field circulation and induction]{ref}.

For a computed example, a fixed single-turn loop has <area> $A=0.020\,\mathrm{m^2}$ and out-of-page <unit normal>. Prescribe an external uniform <magnetic field> in that direction with $B(t)=0.10\,\mathrm T+(0.30\,\mathrm{T/s})t$ for $0\le t\le1.0\,\mathrm s$. Then
$$
\Phi_{\mathrm{ext}}(t)=AB(t)
=0.0020\,\mathrm{Wb}+(0.0060\,\mathrm{Wb/s})t,
\qquad
\mathcal E_{\mathrm{CCW}}=-0.0060\,\mathrm V.
$$
In a quasistatic resistive model with <electrical resistance> $R=2.0\,\Omega$, neglect the loop's <self-inductance> and its own contribution to changing <magnetic flux>. <Ohm's law> gives
$$
I_{\mathrm{CCW}}=\frac{\mathcal E_{\mathrm{CCW}}}{R}=-0.0030\,\mathrm A.
$$
The <magnetic flux> rises from $0.0020\,\mathrm{Wb}$ to $0.0080\,\mathrm{Wb}$ while the induced <electromotive force> stays constant: it measures the rate of change, rather than the amount of <magnetic flux>. The negative sign means clockwise <electric current>; its <magnetic field> through the loop points into the page, as explained by <Lenz's law>. The toy model describes the interval of steady ramping, excluding switching transients at its ends.

\Image[faraday-changing-flux-and-lenz-current.png]
{title=<Faraday's law of induction> and <Lenz's law> for increasing out-of-page <magnetic flux>}
{description=Counterclockwise is the positive orientation. Dots denote the external out-of-page <magnetic field>, and crosses denote the in-page <magnetic field> produced by the clockwise induced <electric current>. The plots show $\Phi_{\mathrm{ext}}$ increasing from $2$ to $8\,\mathrm{mWb}$, with constant $\mathcal E=-6\,\mathrm{mV}$ and $I=-3\,\mathrm{mA}$ in the resistive approximation.}

For a moving conducting loop $C(t)$, the electromagnetic <electromotive force> instead includes the <Lorentz force> contribution from the local <velocity> $\mathbf v$ of each conductor segment:
$$
\mathcal E(t)=\oint_{C(t)}(\mathbf E+\mathbf v\times\mathbf B)\cdot d\mathbf l
=-\frac{d}{dt}\int_{S(t)}\mathbf B\cdot d\mathbf S.
$$
This form assumes a thin-wire model with a well-defined closed contour and spanning <oriented surface> that move compatibly, a <magnetic field> satisfying <Gauss's law for magnetism>, and conductor speeds small compared with the <speed of light>. Ideal sliding contacts such as those in <motional electromotive force> can be treated piecewise. Arbitrary changes of electrical connections require checking the actual contour and conductor velocities; differentiating the flux through an arbitrary chosen moving path is insufficient. The <velocity> here describes conductor motion, not the charge carriers' drift along the wire. https://www.feynmanlectures.caltech.edu/II_17.html#Ch17-S2[Feynman lectures, II.17.2: limits of the flux rule]{ref}.

A short derivation of the fixed-loop integral statement applies <Stokes theorem> to the local <curl> equation and differentiates under the fixed <surface integral>. For the moving loop, the <magnetic flux transport for a moving circuit> adds the swept-area term $-\oint_C(\mathbf v\times\mathbf B)\cdot d\mathbf l$ to $d\Phi_B/dt$. Combining these two identities gives the moving-loop statement. This derives the integral forms from the local field law; it is not a proof of that empirical law from mathematics alone.

The useful unity is geometric: changing the <magnetic field>, the enclosed <area>, or the orientation can change <magnetic flux> and hence produce an <electromotive force>. A coil's multiple turns are accounted for by <magnetic flux linkage>, rather than by multiplying a single-turn flux without checking which flux each turn receives. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-2/pages/13-1-faradays-law[OpenStax University Physics 2, §13.1: flux changes and coils]{ref}.

Faraday's report read on 24 November 1831 describes opposite momentary responses when a neighboring circuit is connected and disconnected, with no continuing response while its steady <electric current> is maintained. These are concrete experimental roots of the changing-flux idea. The report predates the modern <Maxwell equations> notation used above. https://www.gutenberg.org/files/14986/14986-h/14986-h.htm[Faraday, Experimental Researches in Electricity, First Series (1831), §§10–11, reprinted in volume I]{ref}.

= Lenz's law
{parent=Faraday's law of induction}
{c}
{wiki}

<Lenz's law> expresses the minus sign in <Faraday's law of induction>: the induced <electromotive force> drives a passive resistive loop's <electric current> in the direction whose own <magnetic field> opposes the imposed change in <magnetic flux>. **It opposes the change of flux, not invariably the external magnetic field.** Choose an <oriented surface> and its positive boundary direction first, find the sign of $d\Phi_{\mathrm{ext}}/dt$, and then use the <right-hand rule> to translate the signed <electric current> into a physical direction. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-2/pages/13-2-lenzs-law[OpenStax University Physics 2, §13.2: current direction and changing flux]{ref}.

In the fixed-loop example in <Faraday's law of induction>, out-of-page <magnetic flux> increases at $0.0060\,\mathrm{Wb/s}$. With counterclockwise positive, $I=-0.0030\,\mathrm A$ is clockwise, producing an in-page <magnetic field> through the loop. Reverse the ramp to $B(t)=0.40\,\mathrm T-(0.30\,\mathrm{T/s})t$ on the same one-second interval: now $\mathcal E=+0.0060\,\mathrm V$ and $I=+0.0030\,\mathrm A$. The counterclockwise <electric current> produces an out-of-page <magnetic field>, supporting the decreasing external <magnetic flux>. A steady out-of-page <magnetic field> with fixed loop geometry gives no induced <electromotive force> in either direction.

For the increasing-area example in <motional electromotive force>, clockwise <electric current> runs down the right-hand rod. Its <magnetic force on a current-carrying wire> points left, opposing the imposed motion to the right. Writing $I_{\mathrm{mag}}=B\ell v/R$ for the current magnitude, the <power> required to maintain constant <speed> is
$$
P_{\mathrm{drive}}=(I_{\mathrm{mag}}\ell B)v
=\frac{(B\ell v)^2}{R}=I_{\mathrm{mag}}^2R=P_{\mathrm{Joule}}.
$$
Thus the sign agrees with <conservation of energy>: the external drive supplies the <Joule heating>. The <magnetic forces do no work> on individual charges; mechanical <work> by the drive supplies the electrical <energy>. This also explains the usefulness of <electromagnetic braking of a sliding rod> and the <terminal speed of a falling conducting loop>. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-2/pages/13-3-motional-emf[OpenStax University Physics 2, §13.3: mechanical power and resistive heating]{ref}.

The sign of induced <electromotive force> alone does not determine an arbitrary circuit's instantaneous <electric current>. Retaining constant <self-inductance> $L$ gives $L\,dI/dt+RI=-d\Phi_{\mathrm{ext}}/dt$, so a pre-existing <electric current> can persist while the induced drive opposes its change. The distinction matters in the <lossless limit of magnetic loop braking>. The resistive toy examples use $L=0$ as an approximation, not a general consequence of <Lenz's law>.

= Terminal speed of a falling conducting loop
{parent=Lenz's law}

If a loop of resistance $R$ has magnetic flux $\Phi_B=-kz$, then its induced current is $I=k\dot z/R$. When its magnetic force is $-kI$ in the vertical direction, its equation under gravity is
$$
m\ddot z=-mg-\frac{k^2}{R}\dot z,
$$
so its downward terminal speed is $Rmg/k^2$.

= Lossless limit of magnetic loop braking
{parent=Terminal speed of a falling conducting loop}
{title2=$\mathcal L I+Kz=\text{constant}$}

For an external loop flux $Kz$, positive current orientation and upward <Lorentz force> $KI$, including <self-inductance> gives $\mathcal L\dot I+RI=-K\dot z$ and $m\ddot z=-mg+KI$. At zero resistance total linked flux is conserved. If initially $I=0$ at $z=z_0$, then $I=-K(z-z_0)/\mathcal L$ and the mechanical motion is an undamped oscillator about $z_0-mg\mathcal L/K^2$. The finite-resistance terminal speed tends to zero as $R\to0$, but neglecting <self-inductance> is a singular approximation in that limit. A lossless loop does not dispose of <energy> through <Joule heating>.

= Gauss's law
{parent=Maxwell equations}
{c}
{wiki}
{title2=$\oint_{\partial V}\mathbf E\cdot\mathbf n\,dS=Q_{\mathrm{enclosed}}/\varepsilon_0$}

= Gauss law
{c}
{synonym}

<Gauss's law> relates the <electric field> on a <closed surface> to the net <electric charge> inside it. In <SI> units, with outward <unit normal> $\mathbf n$,
$$
\boxed{\oint_{\partial V}\mathbf E\cdot\mathbf n\,dS=\frac{Q_{\mathrm{enclosed}}}{\varepsilon_0},\qquad Q_{\mathrm{enclosed}}=\int_V\rho\,dV.}
$$
Here $\rho$ describes a volume <charge density>, and $\varepsilon_0$ is the <vacuum permittivity>; for ideal point or surface charges, use the enclosed <electric charge> directly rather than an ordinary volume-density integral. The left side is <electric flux>: the <surface integral> of the outward normal component of the <electric field>. Field directed outward contributes positively; field directed inward contributes negatively. The equality uses net signed <electric charge>, so positive and negative enclosed charges can cancel. The <Gaussian surface> is an imagined accounting boundary, not necessarily a material object. See https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-2/pages/6-2-explaining-gausss-law[OpenStax, explaining Gauss's law].

Where the <electric field> and <charge density> are smooth, the local statement is $\nabla\cdot\mathbf E=\rho/\varepsilon_0$. The <divergence theorem> connects this <divergence> to the <surface integral>. This is the satisfying connection between local sources and global accounting: a surface can register how much net <electric charge> it encloses without revealing where that charge is located within it. A <point charge> requires care with the singular field at its position; the integral formulation remains useful with a boundary that avoids the charge.

The law fixes total <electric flux>, rather than every value of the <electric field>. In an uncharged region, a uniform <electric field> $\mathbf E=(E_0,0,0)$ crosses a cube of side $\ell$. Its left and right faces contribute $-E_0\ell^2$ and $+E_0\ell^2$; the other four faces contribute zero. Thus **the net electric flux is zero while the electric field is nonzero everywhere**. Charges outside a <Gaussian surface> can change the field on it without changing its net <electric flux>. Conversely, fields cannot generally be reconstructed from enclosed <electric charge> alone. <Electrostatics> also requires the <electric field> to be the negative <gradient> of an <electric potential>, with appropriate boundary information. This distinction is emphasized in https://www.feynmanlectures.caltech.edu/II_05.html[Feynman lectures II.5, application of Gauss' law].

\Image[gaussian-flux-and-field.png]
{title=Zero net flux can coexist with a nonzero uniform field, and equal enclosed charge can give different field magnitudes on different spheres}

<Spherical symmetry> supplies the extra information needed for a particularly simple calculation. An isolated <spherically symmetric> charge distribution in vacuum produces a radial <electric field>, constant in magnitude on each concentric <sphere>. Its <electric flux> is therefore $4\pi r^2E_r(r)$, giving
$$
E_r(r)=\frac{Q_{\mathrm{enclosed}}(r)}{4\pi\varepsilon_0r^2}.
$$
Outside the distribution, doubling $r$ quadruples the <surface area> and divides the radial <electric field> by four, while the <electric flux> stays $Q/\varepsilon_0$. The inverse-square dependence is an area-dilution result. The <electric field of a uniformly charged ball> and <electric field of a uniformly charged spherical shell> show that the same total charge can give different interior fields. These symmetry arguments and their limits are developed in https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-2/pages/6-3-applying-gausss-law[OpenStax, applying Gauss's law].

= Electric field of a uniformly charged ball
{parent=Gauss's law}
{title2=$E_r(r)=Qr/(4\pi\varepsilon_0R^3)\quad(0\leq r\leq R)$}

= Electric field of a uniformly charged solid sphere
{synonym}

Distribute a positive <electric charge> $Q$ uniformly through a <closed ball> of radius $R$ in vacuum, with no external field. Its volume <charge density> is $3Q/(4\pi R^3)$. For $0<r<R$, the enclosed fraction is $(r/R)^3$. Applying <Gauss's law> on a concentric <Gaussian surface> gives
$$
E_r(r)=\begin{cases}\dfrac{Qr}{4\pi\varepsilon_0R^3},&0\leq r\leq R,\\[4pt]\dfrac{Q}{4\pi\varepsilon_0r^2},&r\geq R.\end{cases}
$$
The <electric field> grows linearly inside and decays as an inverse square outside. With $E_R=Q/(4\pi\varepsilon_0R^2)$, its values at $r/R=1/2,1,2$ are $E_R/2,E_R,E_R/4$. The field vanishes at the centre by <spherical symmetry> and is continuous at the surface because this ideal volume distribution has no singular surface layer. Its interior behavior differs from a <conductor in electrostatic equilibrium>, where mobile charge redistributes.

\Image[gauss-charged-ball-and-shell.png]
{title=A uniform volume charge and a uniform thin-shell charge have the same exterior field but different interior fields}

= Electric field of a uniformly charged spherical shell
{parent=Gauss's law}
{title2=$E_r(r)=0\ (r<R),\quad E_r(r)=Q/(4\pi\varepsilon_0r^2)\ (r>R)$}

Place the same positive <electric charge> $Q$ uniformly on the <sphere> of radius $R$, with no external field. Every concentric interior <Gaussian surface> encloses zero charge; <spherical symmetry> then makes the interior <electric field> zero. Outside, it is again $Q/(4\pi\varepsilon_0r^2)$. At $R$, the inner and outer limits differ by $Q/(4\pi\varepsilon_0R^2)=\sigma/\varepsilon_0$, with <surface charge density> $\sigma=Q/(4\pi R^2)$. The ideal surface charge makes the field discontinuous, so the plot displays one-sided limits rather than assigning a single field value on the charged sheet. An isolated charged conducting sphere in <electrostatic equilibrium> realizes this exterior/interior pattern.

= Hyperbolic-space Coulomb field
{parent=Gauss's law}
{title2=$E(\xi)=Q/[4\pi\epsilon_0R^2\sinh^2(\xi/R)]$}

In static time times round three-dimensional <hyperbolic space> of curvature radius $R$, a geodesic sphere has area $4\pi R^2\sinh^2(\xi/R)$. The <Gauss law> therefore gives the displayed radial field. It approaches the flat-space inverse-square field near the charge and falls exponentially at large geodesic distance. The curvature changes the area of a sphere, not the conserved flux.

= Force between hemispheres of a uniformly charged ball
{parent=Gauss's law}
{title2=$F=3Q^2/(64\pi\varepsilon_0R^2)$}

For uniform <charge density> $\rho_e=3Q/(4\pi R^3)$, <Gauss's law> gives the interior <electric field> $E=\rho_e r/(3\varepsilon_0)$. Integrate its vertical force density $\rho_e^2z/(3\varepsilon_0)$ over the upper hemisphere: $\int z\,dV=\pi R^4/4$. Internal pair forces in that hemisphere cancel, so its net force is exactly the force from the lower hemisphere. The result is upward and has magnitude $3Q^2/(64\pi\varepsilon_0R^2)$.

= Gaussian surface
{c}
{parent=Gauss's law}
{wiki}

A <Gaussian surface> is a <closed surface> used with the integral form of <Gauss's law> to relate <electric flux> to enclosed <electric charge>. It need not coincide with the surface of any charged body. Choosing it to match a <symmetry> can make the normal component of the <electric field> constant on a portion of it, allowing that component to be taken outside a <surface integral>. A concentric <sphere> works for an isolated <spherically symmetric> charge distribution; an arbitrarily placed <sphere> generally does not.

Zero enclosed <electric charge> implies zero total <electric flux>. Inferring zero <electric field> additionally needs information such as the <spherical symmetry> used in the <electric field of a uniformly charged spherical shell>. The imagined boundary does not itself block or redirect the <electric field>.

= Gaussian pillbox
{c}
{parent=Gauss's law}

A Gaussian pillbox is a thin cylindrical <Gaussian surface> crossing an interface. Shrinking its height converts a divergence law into a jump condition for the field's normal component.

= Charge conservation from Maxwell equations
{parent=Maxwell equations}

Taking the divergence of the Ampère-Maxwell equation and using Gauss's law gives
$$
\partial_t\rho+\nabla\cdot J=0.
$$
Hence the charge in a fixed volume changes only through current crossing its boundary.

= Charge continuity equation
{parent=Charge conservation from Maxwell equations}
{title2=$\partial_t\rho+\nabla\cdot\mathbf J=0$}

= Continuity equation for electric charge
{synonym}

The local form of <conservation of electric charge> relates <charge density> to <current density>. Taking the <divergence> of the <Ampère-Maxwell equation> and substituting <Gauss's law> proves the equation, using that the <divergence of a curl is zero>.

= Covariant Maxwell equation with the minus-plus-plus-plus metric
{parent=Maxwell equations}
{c}

With metric signature $(-,+,+,+)$, $A_0=-\Phi/c$, and $j^\mu=(c\rho,J)$, the field tensor satisfies
$$
\partial_\nu F^{\mu\nu}=\mu_0j^\mu,
\qquad
\partial_{[\lambda}F_{\mu\nu]}=0.
$$
Its components are $F_{0i}=-E_i/c$ and $F_{ij}=\epsilon_{ijk}B_k$, so these tensor equations reproduce all four Maxwell equations.

= Source-free Maxwell equations in a linear medium
{parent=Maxwell equations}

For $D=\epsilon E$ and $B=\mu H$ with no free charge or current,
$$
\nabla\cdot D=0,\qquad
\nabla\cdot B=0,\qquad
\nabla\times E=-\partial_tB,\qquad
\nabla\times H=\partial_tD.
$$

= Plane electromagnetic wave in a linear medium
{parent=Source-free Maxwell equations in a linear medium}

A plane wave in a homogeneous linear medium satisfies
$$
\omega^2=\frac{|k|^2}{\epsilon\mu},
\qquad
v=\frac1{\sqrt{\epsilon\mu}},
\qquad
B_0=\frac1\omega k\times E_0.
$$

= Transversality of an electromagnetic plane wave
{parent=Plane electromagnetic wave in a linear medium}

The source-free divergence equations imply $\mathbf k\cdot\mathbf E_0=\mathbf k\cdot\mathbf B_0=0$, so both fields are transverse to the wavevector.

= Magnetic vector potential
{parent=Electromagnetism}
{wiki}

A magnetic vector potential satisfies B equal to its curl and is defined up to a gradient.

= Magnetic helicity
{parent=Magnetic vector potential}
{title2=$H=\int_V\mathbf A\cdot\mathbf B\,dV,\quad\mathbf B=\nabla\times\mathbf A$}
{wiki}

The <magnetic helicity> integrates the <dot product> of a <magnetic vector potential> and its <magnetic field>. It measures linkage and twisting of magnetic flux. For a <solenoidal vector field> $\mathbf B$ tangent to the boundary, it is unchanged by the gauge transformation $\mathbf A\mapsto\mathbf A+\nabla\chi$, since the change is $\int_V\nabla\cdot(\chi\mathbf B)\,dV=\int_{\partial V}\chi\mathbf B\cdot d\mathbf S=0$. If its density satisfies $\partial_t h=\nabla\cdot(f\mathbf B-h\mathbf u)$ with both vector fields tangent to the boundary of a fixed volume, the <divergence theorem> gives conservation of its integral. This is an instance of <helicity conservation by tangent boundary conditions>.

= Magnetic-energy lower bound from helicity
{parent=Magnetic helicity}
{title2=$E_B\geq |H_M|/(2\mu_0 C_D)$}

In a bounded regular domain, fix the global extension or circulation convention for the <magnetic vector potential>. A bounded inverse of <curl> on tangent <solenoidal vector fields> gives $\|\mathbf A\|_2\leq C_D\|\mathbf B\|_2$. The <Cauchy-Schwarz inequality> then gives $|H_M|\leq C_D\|\mathbf B\|_2^2=2\mu_0 C_DE_B$. One construction extends the tangent field by zero outside the domain and uses the whole-space <Biot-Savart law>; its kernel of size $|\mathbf x-\mathbf y|^{-2}$ is integrable over a bounded region, giving the required $L^2$ bound. Nonzero conserved <magnetic helicity> therefore prevents complete decay of <magnetic energy>.

= Current helicity
{parent=Magnetic helicity}
{title2=$H_c=\langle\mathbf b\cdot\nabla\times\mathbf b\rangle$}

Current helicity correlates a <magnetic field> with its <curl>, which is proportional to <electric current density>. It differs from <magnetic helicity>, which uses a <magnetic vector potential>. For a solenoidal monochromatic field of wavenumber $k$ in a periodic domain, choose $\mathbf a_b=\nabla\times\mathbf b/k^2$. Then $\nabla\times\mathbf a_b=\mathbf b$ and $H_c=k^2\langle\mathbf a_b\cdot\mathbf b\rangle$.

= Gauge dependence of helicity in an open magnetic flux tube
{parent=Magnetic helicity}
{title2=$H_m/L=\pi bCR_0^2$}

An open <flux tube> carries <magnetic flux> through its end caps. Its ordinary <magnetic helicity> can therefore change under a single-valued <gauge transformation>. For $\mathbf B=aR\mathbf e_\phi+b\mathbf e_z$ inside $R<R_0$ and zero outside, a regular family of <magnetic vector potentials> is $A_\phi=bR/2$, $A_z=C-aR^2/2$ inside and $A_\phi=bR_0^2/(2R)$, $A_z=C-aR_0^2/2$ outside. Their tangential components agree at $R_0$. Inside, $\mathbf A\cdot\mathbf B=bC$, so the helicity per unit length is $\pi bCR_0^2$. Changing $C$ is the gauge transformation $\Lambda=\Delta C\,z$, and changes this result whenever $b\ne0$. The convention $A_z=0$ outside gives $C=aR_0^2/2$ and helicity per unit length $\pi abR_0^4/2$, while $C=0$ gives zero. Without an end-cap or gauge convention the ordinary helicity is not unique.

= Material transport of magnetic helicity density
{parent=Magnetic helicity}
{title2=$D_t(\mathbf A\cdot\mathbf B/\rho)$}

For smooth <ideal magnetohydrodynamics>, write $\mathbf B=\nabla\times\mathbf A$ and $\partial_t\mathbf A=\mathbf u\times\mathbf B-\nabla\varphi$. The <continuity equation> and induction equation give $D_t(\mathbf B/\rho)=(\mathbf B/\rho)\cdot\nabla\mathbf u$. The vector-potential equation becomes $D_t\mathbf A=\nabla(\mathbf u\cdot\mathbf A-\varphi)-(\nabla\mathbf u)^T\mathbf A$. Contracting these two equations cancels their velocity-gradient terms and proves
$$
D_t\left(\frac{\mathbf A\cdot\mathbf B}{\rho}\right)=\frac{\mathbf B}{\rho}\cdot\nabla(\mathbf u\cdot\mathbf A-\varphi).
$$
Consequently the <magnetic helicity> in a material volume satisfies $dH_m/dt=\int_{\partial V}(\mathbf u\cdot\mathbf A-\varphi)\mathbf B\cdot\mathbf n\,dS$. It is conserved when the <magnetic field> is tangent to the boundary. A single-valued <gauge transformation> changes $H_m$ by $\int_{\partial V}\Lambda\mathbf B\cdot\mathbf n\,dS$, which also vanishes under that condition.

= Radial-gauge potential for a divergence-free magnetic field
{parent=Magnetic vector potential}
{title2=$\mathbf A(\mathbf x)=\int_0^1s\,\mathbf B(s\mathbf x)\times\mathbf x\,ds$}

For a smooth <divergence>-free field on $\mathbb R^3$, the <differential form> $F=\iota_{\mathbf B}(dx^1\wedge dx^2\wedge dx^3)$ is closed. The radial homotopy formula gives a global <magnetic vector potential> $\mathbf A(\mathbf x)=\int_0^1s\,\mathbf B(s\mathbf x)\times\mathbf x\,ds$ with <curl> $\mathbf B$. For constant $\mathbf B$, this reduces to $\mathbf A=\tfrac12\mathbf B\times\mathbf x$.

= Neumann's mutual-inductance formula
{parent=Magnetic vector potential}
{c}

For thin closed wire loops $C_1,C_2$, the mutual inductance is
$$
L_{12}=L_{21}
=\frac{\mu_0}{4\pi}
\oint_{C_1}\oint_{C_2}
\frac{dx_1\cdot dx_2}{|x_1-x_2|}.
$$

= Coulomb gauge
{parent=Magnetic vector potential}
{c}
{wiki}

The Coulomb gauge imposes
$$
\nabla\cdot A=0
$$
on the <magnetic vector potential>. A <gauge transformation> $A\mapsto A+\nabla\chi$ reaches this gauge when $\chi$ solves
$$
\nabla^2\chi=-\nabla\cdot A.
$$

= Radiation gauge
{parent=Coulomb gauge}
{title2=$A_0=0,\quad\partial_iA_i=0$}

For a free <electromagnetic field> with suitable boundary conditions, combine <Coulomb gauge> $\partial_iA_i=0$ with $A_0=0$. <Gauss's law> makes the second condition consistent in the source-free problem. The remaining field operator is spatially transverse and describes the two radiative <photon> polarizations. A constrained scalar potential must be restored when charges are present.

= Gauge transformation
{parent=Magnetic vector potential}
{wiki}

The transformation $A\mapsto A+\nabla\chi$ leaves $B=\nabla\times A$ unchanged because the <curl> of a <gradient> vanishes.

= Gauge-covariant time translation
{parent=Gauge transformation}
{title2=$\delta A_\nu=\alpha F_{0\nu}$}

For the <Maxwell field>, the variation $\delta A_\nu=\alpha F_{0\nu}=\alpha(\partial_0A_\nu-\partial_\nu A_0)$ is time translation combined with a <gauge transformation>. With constant $\alpha$, $\delta F_{\mu\nu}=\alpha\partial_0F_{\mu\nu}$, and the Lagrangian changes by $\alpha\partial_0\mathcal L$. Its <Noether current> is directly the time component of the <gauge-invariant Maxwell stress-energy tensor>, providing the physical energy density without a potential-dependent improvement step.

= Magnetic dipole moment
{parent=Magnetic vector potential}
{wiki}
{title2=$\mathbf m$}

= Magnetic moment
{synonym}

A <magnetic dipole moment> is a <vector> that summarizes the strength and orientation of a localized magnetic source. For a planar <current loop>, it is the <electric current> times the <vector area>: $\mathbf m=IA\widehat{\mathbf n}$. The <right-hand rule> fixes the unit <normal vector> $\widehat{\mathbf n}$: curl the fingers of the right hand along the <conventional current>, and the thumb points along $\mathbf m$. This single <vector> predicts both the distant <magnetic dipole field> and the turning effect of an imposed <magnetic field>.<University Physics Volume 2 by Samuel J. Ling and others/11.5 Force and Torque on a Current Loop>[§11.5, equations 11.20–11.22: loop moment, winding count and torque]{ref}

The <SI> unit is <ampere> times square metre, $\mathrm{A\,m^2}$, equivalently <joule> per <tesla (unit)>. A <magnetic dipole moment> is neither a <magnetic field> nor a <magnetic monopole>: a closed <current loop> can have a nonzero <magnetic dipole moment> while its net outward <magnetic flux> through any closed surface remains zero, as required by <Gauss's law for magnetism>.

For a localized, steady <electric current density> $\mathbf J(\mathbf r)$, the more general definition is
$$
\mathbf m=\frac12\int_{\mathbb R^3}\mathbf r\times\mathbf J(\mathbf r)\,d^3r.
$$
Here the <cross product> is integrated over the complete source, including return paths. Steadiness requires $\nabla\cdot\mathbf J=0$, and localization requires sufficiently rapid decay for the boundary terms to vanish. Under these assumptions the <localized steady-current moment identities> give $\int\mathbf J\,d^3r=0$: shifting the origin by $\mathbf a$ changes the proposed <magnetic dipole moment> by $-\tfrac12\mathbf a\times\int\mathbf J\,d^3r=0$. An isolated open wire segment does not satisfy these assumptions. <Classical Electromagnetism by Richard Fitzpatrick/Localized Current Distribution>{ref}.

= Current loop
{parent=Magnetic dipole moment}
{title2=$\mathbf m=I\mathbf A$}

A <current loop> is a closed path carrying <electric current>. For a thin wire following a closed <curve> $C$, the <volume integral> defining its <magnetic dipole moment> becomes a <line integral>:
$$
\mathbf m=\frac I2\oint_C\mathbf r\times d\mathbf r=I\mathbf A,
\qquad
\mathbf A=\frac12\oint_C\mathbf r\times d\mathbf r.
$$
The <vector area> $\mathbf A$ is $A\widehat{\mathbf n}$ for a planar <current loop>. For a nonplanar <current loop>, use the <vector area>, rather than the scalar <area> of an arbitrarily chosen spanning surface. Reversing the <electric current> reverses $\mathbf m$ without changing the geometry. <Classical Electromagnetism by Richard Fitzpatrick/Localized Current Distribution>{ref}.

\Image[current-loop-moment-right-hand-rule.png]
{title=Current direction and the right-hand rule for a planar magnetic dipole moment}
{description=Viewed face-on, counterclockwise <conventional current> gives a <magnetic dipole moment> out of the page, shown by a dot; reversing the <electric current> gives a <magnetic dipole moment> into the page, shown by a cross. The curled fingers follow the <electric current> and the thumb follows the unit <normal vector>.}

For $N$ closely spaced identical turns carrying the same <electric current>, the <magnetic dipole moments> add: $\mathbf m=NIA\widehat{\mathbf n}$. Each turn contributes its own <vector area>; unequal areas or differently oriented turns require a <vector> sum. As a calculated example, twelve circular turns of <radius> $R=3.0\,\mathrm{cm}$ carrying $I=0.40\,\mathrm A$ give $m=NI\pi R^2=1.36\times10^{-2}\,\mathrm{A\,m^2}$. This is an idealized example, not an experimental measurement.

= Helmholtz coil
{c}
{parent=Current loop}
{title2=$B_0=(4/5)^{3/2}\mu_0N_HI_H/R_H$}
{wiki}

A <Helmholtz coil> arrangement uses two identical coaxial circular windings separated by their <radius>, carrying equal <electric currents> in the same circulation sense. Their combined <magnetic field> is especially uniform near the midpoint, making it useful for a <current-loop torque experiment>. The cancellation of the axial second <derivative> there follows from the <on-axis magnetic field of a circular current loop>.

= Magnetic dipole torque
{parent=Magnetic dipole moment}
{title2=$\boldsymbol\tau=\mathbf m\times\mathbf B_{\mathrm{ext}}$}

An externally imposed <magnetic field> that is nearly uniform over a rigid <current loop> produces the <torque>
$$
\boldsymbol\tau=\mathbf m\times\mathbf B_{\mathrm{ext}},
\qquad
U=-\mathbf m\cdot\mathbf B_{\mathrm{ext}}=-mB\cos\theta.
$$
The <angle> $\theta$ is measured between the <magnetic dipole moment> and the external <magnetic field>, not between the plane of the <current loop> and the <magnetic field>. The <magnetic dipole potential energy> uses a <dot product>, whereas the <torque> uses a <cross product>. The <torque> is largest when the <magnetic dipole moment> is perpendicular to the <magnetic field>; parallel alignment minimizes the <potential energy>.<University Physics Volume 2 by Samuel J. Ling and others/11.5 Force and Torque on a Current Loop>[§11.5, equations 11.22–11.23: vector torque and orientation energy]{ref}

For the sign convention $\mathbf B_{\mathrm{ext}}=B\widehat{\mathbf x}$ and $\mathbf m=m(\cos\theta\widehat{\mathbf x}+\sin\theta\widehat{\mathbf y})$, positive rotation increases $\theta$ and
$$
\tau_z=-mB\sin\theta=-\frac{dU}{d\theta}.
$$
Thus at $0<\theta<\pi$ the <torque> turns the <magnetic dipole moment> toward the external <magnetic field>.
